Which law, which year, which regime? (read this first)

The frame for every answer

Previous Year (PY) 2025-26 (1 April 2025 – 31 March 2026) → income is taxed in Assessment Year (AY) 2026-27, under the Income-tax Act, 1961 as amended by the Finance Act, 2025. Your course plan and textbooks (Singhania, Mehrotra & Goyal, Manoharan, Gaur & Narang) are all "AY 2026-27" editions, and your syllabus says "computation of tax liabilities under the new tax regime" (section 115BAC, which is the default regime).

Course vs industry — the new Act

Parliament passed the Income-tax Act, 2025, which replaces the 1961 Act from 1 April 2026. It drops the "previous year / assessment year" pair and uses a single term, "tax year", and it renumbers the sections (for example, new-regime rates sit in a new section instead of 115BAC). Your course plan names both Acts. But the incomes you are examined on were earned in PY 2025-26, so they are still governed by the 1961 Act. In the exam, quote 1961 section numbers (sec 10, 15–17, 22–27, 28–44, 45–55, 56–59, 80C–80U, 115BAC, 139). If a question explicitly asks about the 2025 Act, say that it simplifies language, keeps the same five heads of income, and introduces the "tax year" concept.

Warning — rates in the question paper win

This guide uses Finance Act 2025 figures for AY 2026-27 (new-regime slabs 0–4L nil / 4–8L 5% / 8–12L 10% / 12–16L 15% / 16–20L 20% / 20–24L 25% / above 24L 30%; standard deduction ₹75,000; rebate u/s 87A up to ₹60,000 where total income ≤ ₹12 lakh; 4% cess). If your question paper prints different rates, limits or a different population/city list, use the paper's figures and write one line: "Solved as per rates given in the question." Where the course notes use older perquisite values (e.g. car ₹1,800/₹2,400 per month), those are still the correct values for AY 2026-27.

Old regime — why you still need it

The course plan lists "Tax Regimes: Old and New" in Unit I and "Choosing between old and new regimes" in Unit V. Exemptions such as HRA (sec 10(13A)), LTC, children-education/hostel allowance and most Chapter VI-A deductions (80C, 80D…) survive only in the old regime. Throughout this guide these are tagged OLD REGIME ONLY; items valid in both are tagged BOTH; new-regime-specific items are tagged NEW REGIME.

Exam presentation (worth marks)

For every numerical: (1) heading "Computation of … of Mr X for AY 2026-27", (2) a two-column table (Particulars | ₹), (3) working notes numbered below, (4) section number next to each exemption, (5) a closing line stating regime and assumptions. Section C case studies reward "organises incomes as per tax laws" and "applies provisions in determining GTI" (from your CIA-2 rubric), so name the head and the section for every item.

Unit I — Fundamentals of Income Tax and Statutory Framework

10 hours in class; CO1 carries 20 of the CIA-1 marks and appears in the ESE. This unit gives you the vocabulary for everything else and one heavy numerical type: residential status.

1.1 Tax: meaning, types, direct vs indirect, duty, cess, surcharge, principles, objectives

Intuition

Think of the country as a housing society. Roads, police, courts, defence, schools and hospitals are "common facilities". Tax is the compulsory maintenance charge each member pays. Unlike a fee, you cannot say "I did not use the park, so I will not pay" — that is why tax is compulsory and carries no direct quid pro quo.

Definition

Tax is a compulsory contribution levied by the government, under the authority of law, on persons, incomes, goods, services or transactions, to meet public expenditure, without any direct and specific benefit being given in return to the taxpayer. (Article 265 of the Constitution: "No tax shall be levied or collected except by authority of law.")

Features of a tax

  1. Compulsory — non-payment attracts interest, penalty and prosecution.
  2. Levied by law — only Parliament/State Legislatures can impose it (Art. 265).
  3. No quid pro quo — contrast with a fee (e.g., passport fee) or a price.
  4. For public purpose — funds the Consolidated Fund of India/State.
  5. Paid out of income or wealth of the taxpayer, periodically.

Three-tier federal structure (course plan emphasis)

TierConstitutional sourceMain taxes
Union GovernmentSeventh Schedule, List I (Union List)Income tax on non-agricultural income (Entry 82), corporation tax, customs duty, Central GST, IGST, central excise on petroleum/tobacco
State GovernmentsList II (State List)Tax on agricultural income (Entry 46), State GST, stamp duty, state excise on alcohol, VAT on petroleum/alcohol, land revenue, vehicle tax
Local bodies (municipalities, panchayats)Powers delegated by State laws (73rd/74th Amendments)Property tax, water tax, advertisement tax, entry-related levies

Direct vs indirect taxes

BasisDirect taxIndirect tax
Impact and incidenceBoth on the same person (the person who pays bears it)Impact on seller/supplier, incidence shifted to the consumer
Levied onIncome, wealth, profitsGoods, services, transactions
NatureUsually progressive (rate rises with income)Usually regressive (same rate for rich and poor)
EvasionEasier to evade (self-declared income)Harder to evade (collected at the point of sale)
Administered byCBDT (Central Board of Direct Taxes)CBIC (Central Board of Indirect Taxes and Customs) and State tax departments
Inflation effectCan check inflation (reduces disposable income)Can raise prices directly
ExamplesIncome tax, corporation tax, securities transaction taxGST (CGST, SGST, IGST, UTGST), customs duty, excise on petroleum and alcohol
Indian example

GST came into force on 1 July 2017 (101st Constitutional Amendment), merging central excise, service tax, VAT and many other indirect taxes. Wealth tax was abolished by the Finance Act, 2015 (from AY 2016-17). On the direct side, the Union Budget of 1 February 2025 raised the new-regime rebate threshold so that resident individuals with total income up to ₹12 lakh pay no income tax.

Duty, cess and surcharge (course plan lists these)

TermMeaningIncome-tax example
DutyA tax on goods or transactions, typically on manufacture, import/export, or documentsCustoms duty, stamp duty (not an income-tax item, but a tax on transactions)
SurchargeAn additional tax calculated as a percentage of the tax, imposed on high-income taxpayers; goes wholly to the Union (not shared with States)10% where total income exceeds ₹50 lakh, 15% above ₹1 crore, 25% above ₹2 crore (new regime cap 25%)
CessA tax on tax, collected for a specific earmarked purpose; also not shared with StatesHealth & Education Cess at 4% of (income tax + surcharge)

Principles (canons) of a good tax system

Adam Smith's four canons, plus modern additions:

  1. Equity — tax according to ability to pay (progressive slabs).
  2. Certainty — amount, time and manner of payment must be clear (published slabs, due dates).
  3. Convenience — collected when easiest to pay (TDS from salary every month).
  4. Economy — cost of collection should be small (e-filing, faceless assessment).
  5. Productivity — should raise adequate revenue.
  6. Elasticity — revenue should rise with national income.
  7. Simplicity — easy to understand (a stated aim of the new regime and the 2025 Act).
  8. Flexibility and diversity — many tax sources; can be changed by each Finance Act.

Objectives of levying taxes

  • Raise revenue for public expenditure (defence, infrastructure, welfare).
  • Reduce inequality of income and wealth (progressive income tax).
  • Economic stability — control inflation/deflation through fiscal policy.
  • Discourage harmful consumption (high GST compensation cess on tobacco, for example).
  • Encourage savings, investment and priority sectors (old-regime 80C; deductions for new employment u/s 80JJAA).
  • Balanced regional development and protection of domestic industry (customs duty).
Why it matters

Section A of the ESE often asks a 5-mark "Distinguish between direct and indirect tax" or "Explain cess and surcharge". These are free marks if you use a table.

Self-check
1. Is GST a direct tax because the shopkeeper pays it to the government?

No. The shopkeeper bears the impact (pays the government) but passes on the incidence to the customer through the price. Impact and incidence fall on different persons, so it is indirect.

2. On what is the 4% cess calculated?

On income tax plus surcharge (after the 87A rebate), not on income.

3. Which constitutional entry lets the Union tax income, and why is agricultural income outside it?

Entry 82 of List I covers "taxes on income other than agricultural income"; agricultural income is a State subject (Entry 46, List II). Hence sec 10(1) exempts it from central income tax.

1.2 Legal enactments governing income tax in India

Intuition

The Act is the rule book; the Rules are the detailed instruction manual; the Finance Act changes the prices (rates) every year; CBDT circulars are the department's FAQs; court decisions are the umpire's rulings.

SourceWhat it does
Constitution of IndiaArt. 265 (no tax without law), Art. 246 + Seventh Schedule (Entry 82 List I; Entry 46 List II), Art. 270 (sharing of taxes with States; surcharge and cess excluded).
Income-tax Act, 1961Came into force 1 April 1962; charging section 4, residence sec 6, exemptions sec 10, five heads sec 14, deductions Chapter VI-A, rates via Finance Act and sec 115BAC. Governs AY 2026-27.
Income-tax Rules, 1962Framed by CBDT u/s 295: perquisite valuation (Rule 3), exempt allowances (Rule 2BB), depreciation rates (Appendix I), forms (ITR, Form 16).
Annual Finance ActPassed after the Union Budget; prescribes rates of tax for the AY (Part I of First Schedule) and TDS rates, and amends the Act. The Finance Act, 2025 governs AY 2026-27.
CBDT circulars and notificationsCBDT (Central Boards of Revenue Act, 1963) issues circulars u/s 119 (binding on the department, not on the assessee or courts) and notifications (e.g., Cost Inflation Index 376 for FY 2025-26; Notification 133/2025 raising the specified-employee limit).
Judicial decisionsSupreme Court decisions are the law of the land (Art. 141); High Court decisions bind authorities within that State.
Income-tax Act, 2025Replaces the 1961 Act from 1 April 2026 (tax year 2026-27 onwards); simplified language, "tax year" instead of PY/AY. Not applicable to income of PY 2025-26.
Course vs industry

Your course plan cites both "Income Tax Act of 1961 and 2025". Practitioners filing returns in 2026 for income earned in 2025-26 still use the 1961 Act and ITR forms for AY 2026-27. Mention the 2025 Act as the "future framework" in theory answers.

1.3 Tax regimes: old and new (introduction)

Definition

The new tax regime (sec 115BAC) is the default method of computing tax for individuals, HUFs, AOPs, BOIs and artificial juridical persons from AY 2024-25: lower slab rates, but most exemptions and deductions are withdrawn. The old regime keeps higher slab rates with exemptions (HRA, LTC) and deductions (80C–80U). An individual without business income can choose the old regime every year in the return filed by the due date u/s 139(1); an individual with business/professional income must file Form 10-IEA to opt out and can switch back only once.

Feature (AY 2026-27)New regime (115BAC) DEFAULTOld regime OPT-IN
Basic exemption₹4,00,000 (all ages)₹2,50,000; ₹3,00,000 (60–79 yrs); ₹5,00,000 (80+)
Slabs5/10/15/20/25/30% in ₹4 lakh steps5% (2.5–5L), 20% (5–10L), 30% (above 10L)
87A rebate (residents)Up to ₹60,000 if total income ≤ ₹12,00,000, with marginal reliefUp to ₹12,500 if total income ≤ ₹5,00,000
Standard deduction (salary/pension)₹75,000₹50,000
HRA, LTC, children education/hostel allowanceNot exemptExempt within limits
Entertainment allowance (Govt) & professional tax u/s 16Not deductibleDeductible
Interest on self-occupied house loan u/s 24(b)Not allowedUp to ₹2,00,000
Chapter VI-AOnly 80CCD(2) (employer NPS up to 14%), 80CCH, 80JJAA80C, 80CCC, 80CCD(1)/(1B)/(2), 80D, 80E, 80G, 80TTA/TTB etc.
Family pension deduction u/s 57(iia)1/3 or ₹25,000, lower1/3 or ₹15,000, lower
Maximum surcharge25%37%

1.4 Person and assessee

Definition — Person, sec 2(31)

"Person" includes (i) an individual, (ii) a Hindu Undivided Family (HUF), (iii) a company, (iv) a firm (including LLP), (v) an association of persons (AOP) or body of individuals (BOI), whether incorporated or not, (vi) a local authority, and (vii) every artificial juridical person not falling in the above. An AOP, BOI, local authority or artificial juridical person is deemed a person whether or not formed for deriving income or profits.

CategoryMeaningExample
IndividualA natural human being, including a minor or person of unsound mindArjun, a salaried manager, a minor child actor
HUFFamily of persons lineally descended from a common ancestor, including wives and unmarried daughters; governed by Hindu law (also Jain, Sikh, Buddhist families)"Agrawal HUF" with the karta as head
CompanyIndian company, body corporate incorporated abroad, or institution declared by CBDTInfosys Ltd, Tata Consultancy Services Ltd
FirmPartnership firm under the Indian Partnership Act, 1932, and LLP under the LLP Act, 2008A CA firm
AOP / BOIAOP: persons (can include companies) joining for a common purpose to earn income; BOI: only individualsJoint venture of two companies (AOP); co-heirs jointly receiving income (BOI)
Local authorityPanchayat, municipality, cantonment board, district boardBruhat Bengaluru Mahanagara Palike
Artificial juridical personLegal person not covered aboveA university set up by statute, a deity/idol
Definition — Assessee, sec 2(7)

"Assessee" means a person by whom any tax or any other sum of money (interest, penalty, fee) is payable under the Act, and includes (a) every person in respect of whom any proceeding has been taken for assessment of his income, loss or refund; (b) every person deemed to be an assessee; and (c) every person deemed to be an assessee in default.

TypeWhoExample
Ordinary assesseePerson liable to pay tax on own income, or against whom proceedings are takenA salaried employee filing ITR-1
Representative / deemed assesseePerson assessed for income of another personLegal heir of a deceased person (sec 159); guardian of a minor or lunatic; agent of a non-resident (sec 163)
Assessee in defaultPerson who fails to fulfil a statutory obligation such as deducting/depositing TDS (sec 201) or paying tax demandedAn employer who deducts TDS from salary but does not deposit it
Trap

Every assessee is a person, but not every person is an assessee: a person becomes an assessee only when some sum is payable by him or proceedings are taken against him. A minor with no income is a person, not an assessee.

1.5 Previous year and assessment year

Intuition

You earn this year; the department checks and taxes it next year. Earn in PY 2025-26 → taxed in AY 2026-27. (The 2025 Act merges the two into "tax year".)

Definition

Assessment year [sec 2(9)]: the period of 12 months commencing on 1 April every year, in which income of the previous year is assessed. Previous year [sec 3]: the financial year immediately preceding the assessment year. For a newly set-up business or profession, or a new source of income, the PY begins on the date of setting up / the date the new source comes into existence and ends on the following 31 March (so it can be shorter than 12 months, but never longer).

Worked example
  1. Ms Riya starts a café on 10 October 2025. Her first PY is 10-10-2025 to 31-03-2026 (less than 6 months); AY 2026-27.
  2. Her salary job ended on 30 September 2025: salary from 1-4-2025 to 30-9-2025 is also taxed in AY 2026-27 (same PY for all sources).

Exceptions: when income of the PY is taxed in the PY itself

SectionSituationReason
172Shipping business of non-residents: freight from a port in IndiaShip may never return; 7.5% of freight deemed income
174Persons leaving India permanently (or with no intention of returning)Tax may become uncollectable
174AAOP/BOI/artificial juridical person formed for a particular event or purpose, likely to be dissolvedEntity may cease to exist
175Persons likely to transfer assets to avoid taxProtect revenue
176Discontinued business or professionAssessing Officer may assess at once (discretionary)
Memory hook

"Ships Leave And Transfer Discontinued" → 172 Shipping, 174 Leaving India, 174A AOP for event, 175 Transfer to avoid tax, 176 Discontinued business.

Self-check
1. A profession set up on 15 March 2026 — what is its first PY?

15-3-2026 to 31-3-2026 (17 days); AY 2026-27.

2. Can a previous year exceed 12 months?

No. It is 12 months or less (only for a new business/source).

1.6 Income — definition and characteristics

Definition — sec 2(24)

"Income" is an inclusive definition. It includes: profits and gains; dividend; voluntary contributions received by trusts/institutions; value of perquisites and profits in lieu of salary (sec 17); special allowances or benefits; capital gains (sec 45); insurance profits; winnings from lotteries, crossword puzzles, races, card games, gambling or betting; employees' contributions to PF/ESI received by the employer; sums received under a Keyman insurance policy; sums or property received without (or for inadequate) consideration (sec 56(2)(x)); and compensation for termination or modification of employment or business contracts.

Because the definition is inclusive, courts give "income" its ordinary, natural meaning as well: a periodical monetary return coming in with some regularity, or expected regularity, from definite sources.

Characteristics of income (from your course notes)

  1. Regular and definite source — usually; but casual receipts such as lottery winnings are specifically taxed.
  2. Money or money's worth — income may be received in kind (e.g., rent-free house is valued as a perquisite).
  3. Net receipts, not gross — income means receipts minus the expenditure allowed under each head. Presumptive schemes (44AD/44ADA/44AE) take a percentage of gross receipts as income.
  4. Due basis vs receipt basis — salary is taxed on due or receipt, whichever is earlier; PGBP and other sources follow the method of accounting regularly employed (cash or mercantile); some items (e.g., interest on compensation/enhanced compensation) are taxable only on receipt.
  5. Temporary or permanent, lump sum or instalment — irrelevant.
  6. Legal or illegal — illegal income (smuggling, bribery, unlicensed betting) is taxable; tax law does not examine the morality of the source. Expenses incurred for an illegal purpose or for an offence are, however, not deductible (Explanation 1 to sec 37(1)).
  7. Dispute on title does not stop taxation — income is taxed in the hands of the recipient till the dispute is settled.
  8. Relief income or pin money — not income (e.g., a housewife's savings from household allowance, reimbursement of actual expenses).
  9. Income received on behalf of another — not the receiver's income (see diversion by overriding title below).

Diversion by overriding title vs application of income

Intuition

If a court decree says half of the rent of your shop must go straight to your former business partner, that half never really becomes yours — it is diverted at the source. If you receive the full rent and then choose (or are obliged) to pay your son's college fees, that is application of your own income — still taxable in your hands.

BasisDiversion by overriding titleApplication of income
When the obligation operatesBefore the income reaches the assesseeAfter the income has accrued to/been received by the assessee
Nature of obligationLegal compulsion or prior contractual charge (overriding title)Gratuitous or self-imposed
Taxability in assessee's handsNot taxable (the diverted part is not his income)Taxable (the way he spends it is irrelevant)
ExampleMaintenance charged on property income by a court decree before it reaches the ownerPaying one's own income tax, loan instalment or donation out of salary

The decisive test (course notes): did the amount, in truth, never reach the assessee as his own income? The nature of the obligation is the deciding fact.

Self-check
1. A smuggler earns ₹20 lakh. Is it taxable? Can he deduct bribe payments?

Yes, illegal income is taxable (normally under PGBP or other sources). Bribes are expenditure for a purpose prohibited by law and are disallowed under Explanation 1 to sec 37(1).

2. Salary is ₹1,00,000 but ₹10,000 is deducted as TDS and ₹12,000 as employee PF. What is included in income?

₹1,00,000 — TDS and own PF contribution are applications of income.

1.7 Capital and revenue receipts and expenditure

Intuition

A fruit tree and its fruits: selling the tree (the income-earning apparatus) is capital; selling the fruits it yields every season is revenue. Income tax is a tax on fruits, so capital receipts are exempt unless the Act specifically taxes them (capital gains u/s 45, compensation u/s 28(ii) or 17(3), gifts u/s 56(2)(x)).

Definition

Capital receipt: a receipt referable to fixed capital — received in exchange for, or as compensation for loss of, a source of income (sale of a fixed asset, loan taken, capital introduced). Revenue receipt: a receipt referable to circulating capital — arising from the regular course of business or from the use of assets (sales, fees, rent, interest). No single test is decisive; each case depends on its facts (course notes).

Tests from your course notes (ICAI material)

  1. Fixed vs circulating capital — receipts referable to fixed capital are capital; to circulating capital (turned over in trade) are revenue.
  2. Capital asset vs trading asset — profit on sale of a capital asset is capital gain (sec 45); profit on sale of a trading asset (stock-in-trade) is business income (sec 28). Land is a capital asset for a share trader but stock-in-trade for a property dealer.
  3. Transaction in the course of business — a bank's dealing in foreign exchange, a share broker's purchase of shares on own account: revenue.
  4. Shares and securities — motive test — held as investment or for controlling interest: capital; held as a dealer: stock-in-trade (revenue); speculative: revenue but treated separately.
  5. Single transaction — even one bulk purchase followed by sale can be an "adventure in the nature of trade" (revenue). Conduct of assessee, nature and quantity of the article, nature of operations and other circumstances decide.
  6. Liquidated damages linked to procurement of a capital asset (delay in setting up the profit-making apparatus) — capital receipt.
  7. Compensation on termination of agency — if the only agency (source of income) is lost: capital in nature, but now taxable u/s 28(ii)(c); if one of many agencies is lost: revenue. Compensation from employer for premature termination: taxable as profit in lieu of salary u/s 17(3); from any other person: taxable u/s 56(2)(xi).
  8. Gifts — normally capital, but taxed u/s 56(2)(x) when from non-relatives above ₹50,000.

Capital vs revenue expenditure

BasisCapital expenditureRevenue expenditure
BenefitEnduring benefit, lasting more than one year ("enduring benefit" test, British Insulated & Helsby Cables v. Atherton)Benefit consumed within the year
PurposeAcquire/extend/improve a fixed asset or the profit-earning structureRun the business day-to-day
RecurrenceUsually non-recurringUsually recurring
Tax treatmentNot deductible u/s 37; recovered through depreciation (sec 32) or specific sections (35(1)(iv), 35D)Deductible under sec 30–37 if for business
ExamplesBuying machinery, building, patent; cost of installing a new plantSalaries, rent, repairs, advertisement, interest on working-capital loan
Indian example

When a listed company such as Infosys buys back shares, the shareholders' treatment follows the Act's specific rules; when a steel company pays for relining a blast furnace every few years, courts have often treated it as a current repair (revenue), whereas building a new furnace is capital. The label is decided by the nature of the benefit, not by the amount.

Self-check
1. A property dealer sells a flat at a profit. Capital gain or business income?

Business income — the flat is his stock-in-trade.

2. Is a loan received from a bank income?

No — it is a capital receipt (a liability).

3. Premium paid to acquire a 99-year lease of factory land — capital or revenue?

Capital: it secures an enduring advantage (right to use land for 99 years).

1.8 Agricultural income — tests and partial integration

Definition — sec 2(1A)

Agricultural income means (a) any rent or revenue derived from land situated in India and used for agricultural purposes; (b) any income derived from such land by (i) agriculture, (ii) any process ordinarily employed by a cultivator or receiver of rent-in-kind to render the produce fit to be taken to market, or (iii) sale by a cultivator or receiver of rent-in-kind of the produce raised or received by him, without further processing; (c) income from a farm building required for agricultural operations (subject to conditions); and (d) income from saplings or seedlings grown in a nursery. It is exempt u/s 10(1).

Tests (all must be satisfied)

  1. There must be land, and it must be situated in India (agricultural income from land abroad is taxable under "Other sources").
  2. The land must be used for agricultural purposes — basic operations (tilling, sowing, irrigation) involving human skill and labour on the land itself; subsequent operations (weeding, pruning) alone on spontaneously grown produce do not qualify.
  3. The income must be derived from that land directly (not second-degree income such as dividend from a tea company).
  4. Processing should only make the produce marketable; value addition beyond that (sugar from cane, cloth from cotton) is business income.
Agricultural income (exempt)Not agricultural income (taxable)
Sale of wheat/paddy grown by the farmer; rent from agricultural land in India; compensation from tenant for crop damage on land; income from a nurseryDairy, poultry, fisheries, bee-keeping, sericulture; income from spontaneously grown forest trees; dividend from an agricultural company; salary of a farm manager; interest on arrears of rent; agricultural income from land outside India; royalty from mines; sale of butter/cheese

Composite (partly agricultural) income — Rules 7A, 7B, 8

ActivityBusiness income (taxable)Agricultural (exempt)
Tea grown and manufactured (Rule 8)40%60%
Rubber grown and processed (Rule 7A)35%65%
Coffee grown and cured (Rule 7B)25%75%
Coffee grown, cured, roasted and grounded (Rule 7B)40%60%

Partial integration (why exempt income can still raise your tax)

Intuition

Agricultural income itself is never taxed, but if you have a lot of it, it is used to push your non-agricultural income into higher slabs — otherwise a rich farmer-cum-businessman would pay the same low rate as someone with only the business income.

Formula — applies to individuals/HUF/AOP/BOI when (i) net agricultural income > ₹5,000 AND (ii) non-agricultural income > basic exemption limit

Step 1: Tax A = tax on (non-agricultural income + net agricultural income)
Step 2: Tax B = tax on (net agricultural income + basic exemption limit)
Step 3: Tax on non-agricultural income = A − B; then apply 87A (if eligible), surcharge, cess.

Basic exemption limit = ₹4,00,000 under the new regime (₹2,50,000 / 3,00,000 / 5,00,000 under the old regime by age).

Worked example 1 — new regime

Mr Harish (resident, 45) has non-agricultural income (total income) ₹18,00,000 and agricultural income ₹3,00,000. Both conditions satisfied.

  1. Tax on ₹21,00,000: 20,000 (4–8L) + 40,000 (8–12L) + 60,000 (12–16L) + 80,000 (16–20L) + 25,000 (1L at 25%) = ₹2,25,000.
  2. Tax on ₹7,00,000 (3,00,000 + 4,00,000): 3,00,000 × 5% = ₹15,000.
  3. Tax = 2,25,000 − 15,000 = ₹2,10,000. Rebate u/s 87A: not available (total income 18 lakh > 12 lakh).
  4. Cess 4% = ₹8,400. Tax payable = ₹2,18,400.
  5. Without agricultural income the tax on ₹18,00,000 would be ₹1,60,000 (+cess). Partial integration added ₹50,000 before cess.
Worked example 2 — conditions not met
  1. Ms Leela: agricultural income ₹5,00,000, non-agricultural income ₹3,80,000 (below ₹4,00,000). Condition (ii) fails → no integration; tax on ₹3,80,000 = nil.
  2. Mr Sohan: agricultural income ₹4,500, other income ₹9,00,000. Condition (i) fails (≤ ₹5,000) → tax only on ₹9,00,000 = 20,000 + 10,000 = ₹30,000, fully rebated u/s 87A (total income ≤ 12L). Tax nil.
Trap

Agricultural income is not included in total income or GTI. It is used only for rate purposes. Do not write "GTI = 21,00,000" in example 1; GTI is ₹18,00,000.

Self-check
1. Income from sale of sugar produced from own sugarcane — agricultural?

Only the market value of sugarcane (the marketable produce) is agricultural; the profit from converting it into sugar is business income.

2. Rent from agricultural land in Nepal received by a resident Indian?

Taxable under "Income from other sources" — the land is not in India.

More on agricultural income (your "Incomes which do not form part of total income" notes)

Your professor's notes go further than the basic tests. The extra points are below: the three sources of agricultural income, basic vs subsequent operations, the nursery rule, the Rule 7 market-value method, farm buildings, and borderline cases decided by courts.

Definition — three ways agricultural income can arise [sec 2(1A)]

(i) Rent or revenue derived from land in India used for agricultural purposes. Ownership is not needed: rent received by the original tenant from a sub-tenant also qualifies. "Revenue" is wider than rent (e.g., a fee received for renewing a lease of the land). (ii) Income derived from such land by (a) agriculture, (b) a process ordinarily employed by a cultivator or receiver of rent-in-kind to make the produce fit for the market, or (c) sale of such produce by the cultivator or receiver of rent-in-kind with no process other than (b). (iii) Income from a farm building required for agricultural operations, subject to conditions.

Basic and subsequent operations

Basic operations are the work on the land itself that makes the crop sprout: tilling, sowing, planting and similar operations needing human skill and labour. Subsequent operations come after the produce sprouts: weeding, digging, pruning, watering. Subsequent operations count as agriculture only when they continue basic operations. This is the ratio of the Supreme Court in CIT v. Raja Benoy Kumar Sahas Roy (1957) 32 ITR 466: income from forest trees of spontaneous growth is not agricultural even if the owner later tends them, because no basic operation was ever performed. The produce may be food grains, fruit, vegetables, grass or pasture for cattle, luxury crops (betel, coffee, tea, spices, tobacco), commercial crops (cotton, flax, jute, hemp, indigo) and forest produce such as sal and tendu leaves, provided basic operations were performed.

Nursery income — how the law settled it

Earlier rulings required basic operations on land, so plants grown straight in pots were not agricultural. The Madras High Court in CIT v. Soundarya Nursery (2000) 241 ITR 530 held that nursery work involves operations on land before saplings go into pots, so it is agricultural. Explanation 3 to sec 2(1A) now deems income from saplings or seedlings grown in a nursery to be agricultural income whether or not basic operations were carried out on land.

Process to make produce fit for the market

Threshing, winnowing, cleaning, drying and crushing are ordinary processes. For paddy: removing the hay, separating the chaff, filtering stones and packing rice in gunny bags are all part of making it marketable, whether done by hand or machine. The produce must keep its original character. If the produce can already be sold raw and a further manufacturing process is applied (sugar from cane, cured and roasted coffee, made tea), the profit is partly agricultural and partly business income, and Rules 7, 7A, 7B and 8 split it.

Formula — Rule 7 (any produce grown and then used in the assessee's own business)

Business income = sale proceeds of the manufactured product − market value of the agricultural produce used as raw material − manufacturing expenses.
Agricultural income = market value of that produce − cost of cultivation.

Market value = (i) the average price at which the produce was sold during the PY, if it is ordinarily sold raw or after an ordinary process; otherwise (ii) cost of cultivation + land revenue or rent for the area + a reasonable profit fixed by the Assessing Officer. No further deduction is allowed in the business for costs incurred as a cultivator.

From your course material — Mr Amar (sugarcane and sugar, Rule 7)

Mr Amar grows sugarcane. 40% of the crop is sold for ₹12 lakh; its cost of cultivation is ₹6 lakh. The other 60% (cost of cultivation ₹15 lakh, market value ₹25 lakh) is made into sugar after manufacturing expenses of ₹1.5 lakh, and the sugar is sold for ₹30 lakh. Compute business income and agricultural income.

Particulars₹

From your course material — Mr Nilesh (latex from own rubber, Rule 7A)
  1. Latex manufactured from rubber plants grown by him in India is sold for ₹36 lakh. Cost of growing the plants ₹16 lakh; cost of manufacturing latex ₹12 lakh.
  2. Total profit = 36,00,000 − 16,00,000 − 12,00,000 = ₹8,00,000.
  3. Agricultural income (65%) = ₹5,20,000 (exempt). Business income (35%) = ₹2,80,000 (taxable).
Extra worked example — coffee and tea
  1. A coffee estate grows, cures, roasts and grinds its own coffee in India; profit on sale ₹10,00,000. Rule 7B: business 40% = ₹4,00,000; agricultural 60% = ₹6,00,000. (If the coffee were only grown and cured: 25% business, 75% agricultural.)
  2. A tea company grows and manufactures tea in India; profit ₹15,00,000. Rule 8: business 40% = ₹6,00,000; agricultural 60% = ₹9,00,000.

Income from a farm building

Income from a farm building is agricultural if: (a) the building is on or in the immediate vicinity of the agricultural land; (b) the cultivator or receiver of rent needs it, because of his connection with the land, as a dwelling house, store house or other out-building; and (c) either the land is assessed to land revenue in India (or to a local rate collected by Government officers), or, if it is not so assessed, the land is not in an area within a municipality or cantonment board with population of 10,000 or more, and not within the following aerial distance of such a municipality or cantonment:

Aerial distance from the local limitsPopulation of that municipality/cantonment
Up to 2 kmMore than 10,000
More than 2 km, up to 6 kmMore than 1,00,000
More than 6 km, up to 8 kmMore than 10,00,000

Income from using the farm building for any other purpose (letting it as a residence, or for a business or profession) is not agricultural income.

Urban agricultural land: by Explanation 1 to sec 2(1A), a capital gain on transfer of agricultural land in an urban area (as above) is not agricultural income; it is taxed u/s 45 (Unit IV). Rural agricultural land is not a capital asset at all.

From your course material — Mr X (partial integration, both regimes, ages 40 and 75)

Mr X, resident, PY 2025-26: income from salary (computed) ₹11,50,000; income from house property (computed) ₹3,80,000; agricultural income from land in Assam ₹4,50,000; expenses to earn it ₹1,60,000. Compute tax liability for AY 2026-27 if he is (a) 40 years old, (b) 75 years old.

Common figures: non-agricultural income (GTI = total income, no deductions) = 11,50,000 + 3,80,000 = ₹15,30,000. Net agricultural income = 4,50,000 − 1,60,000 = ₹2,90,000 (> ₹5,000). Non-agricultural income exceeds the basic exemption limit in every case, so partial integration applies. No surcharge (income below ₹50 lakh); no 87A rebate (total income above ₹12 lakh new / ₹5 lakh old).

Slab workings: new regime tax on 18,20,000 = 5% of 4,00,000 + 10% of 4,00,000 + 15% of 4,00,000 + 20% of 2,20,000 = 20,000 + 40,000 + 60,000 + 44,000. Old regime (age 40) tax on 18,20,000 = 5% of 2,50,000 + 20% of 5,00,000 + 30% of 8,20,000 = 12,500 + 1,00,000 + 2,46,000; on 5,40,000 = 12,500 + 20% of 40,000 = 20,500. Old regime (age 75) tax on 18,20,000 = 5% of 2,00,000 + 1,00,000 + 2,46,000 = 3,56,000; on 5,90,000 = 10,000 + 20% of 90,000 = 28,000. Under the new regime age does not change the answer. Since the new regime is the default and gives the lower tax, he should stay in it.

Self-check (agricultural income — extra)
1. A tenant sub-lets agricultural land in Punjab and receives rent. Agricultural income?

Yes. Rent or revenue from agricultural land in India qualifies even if the receiver is not the owner.

2. Seedlings grown in pots in a nursery without tilling the land. Agricultural?

Yes, by Explanation 3 to sec 2(1A).

3. Farm building income: land not assessed to land revenue, 5 km from a town of 2,50,000. Agricultural?

No. It is within the 2–6 km band of a town with population above 1,00,000.

4. Latex profit ₹10 lakh from own rubber. Split?

Business 35% = ₹3,50,000; agricultural 65% = ₹6,50,000.

1.9 Gross total income, total income, heads of income, average rate of tax

Definition

Gross total income (GTI) [sec 80B(5)]: total income computed under the five heads (sec 14), after clubbing and after set-off of current and brought-forward losses, before Chapter VI-A deductions. Total income [sec 2(45)]: GTI minus Chapter VI-A deductions (80C to 80U), rounded off to the nearest multiple of ₹10 (sec 288A). Tax is levied on total income.

Formulas

GTI = Σ income under the five heads (after clubbing and set-off)
Total income = GTI − Chapter VI-A deductions
Average rate of tax (course plan item) = (Total tax payable incl. cess ÷ Total income) × 100 — the effective rate on the whole income.
Marginal rate = rate applicable to the last rupee of income (the highest slab reached).

Worked example — average vs marginal rate
  1. Total income ₹18,00,000 (new regime). Tax = 20,000 + 40,000 + 60,000 + (2,00,000 × 20%) 40,000 = ₹1,60,000.
  2. Cess 4% = ₹6,400 → total ₹1,66,400.
  3. Average rate = 1,66,400 ÷ 18,00,000 × 100 = 9.24% (rounded to two decimals).
  4. Marginal rate = 20% slab + 4% cess = 20.8%. The average rate is always below the marginal rate under a progressive system.
Self-check
1. Is standard deduction a Chapter VI-A deduction?

No. It is a deduction u/s 16(ia) within the head "Salaries", so it reduces income under that head before GTI.

2. Total income ₹7,45,634 — what is taxed?

₹7,45,630 (sec 288A: round to nearest ₹10; 4 or less rounds down).

1.10 Tax evasion, tax avoidance and tax planning

Intuition

Three drivers approaching a toll: the planner takes a legitimate toll-free highway built by the government for that purpose; the avoider uses a loophole side-lane that is technically open but clearly not intended; the evader drives through the barrier without paying.

BasisTax planningTax avoidanceTax evasion
LegalityLegal and ethicalLegal in form, against the spirit of lawIllegal
MethodUses incentives the law itself offers (80C in old regime, choosing the regime, sec 54 reinvestment, 80CCD(2) NPS)Exploits loopholes, colourable devices, circular transactions, treaty shoppingSuppressing income, inflating expenses, fake bills, false claims
ConsequenceTax lawfully reducedMay be struck down under GAAR (Chapter X-A, from AY 2018-19) or specific anti-avoidance rulesTax, interest, penalty (sec 270A: 200% of tax for misreporting) and prosecution
TimeBefore the liability arisesBefore the liability arisesAfter the liability has arisen
Judicial viewAcceptedMcDowell & Co. v. CTO (SC, 1985): colourable devices cannot be part of tax planningPunished
Indian example

A salaried employee whose employer contributes 14% of salary to NPS (deductible u/s 80CCD(2) even in the new regime) is doing tax planning. Your CIA-1 interview with a CA noted that many clients confuse legitimate planning with evasion — for example, believing that fake rent receipts for HRA are "planning" (they are evasion).

Definition

Exempt incomes are incomes which do not form part of total income by virtue of sec 10 (and similar sections). They are excluded before GTI is computed; expenditure incurred to earn them is not deductible (sec 14A).

Sec 10 exemptions for individuals in detail (your professor's notes)

Intuition

Sec 10 is a list of "keep out" signs. Some keep income out to avoid taxing the same money twice (HUF and firm distributions), some reward or compensate people (gallantry pensions, disaster compensation, awards), and a long list protects bodies that work for the public (local authorities, welfare funds, regulators). The exam usually asks for the individual items with conditions, and sometimes a short list of the institutional ones.

SectionExemptionConditions / limits (as in your notes)
10(2)Sum received by a member from the HUFPaid out of the income of the family, or out of the income of an impartible estate of the family. The HUF is taxed on its income; exempting the member avoids double taxation.
10(2A)Partner's share in the total income of a firmFirm must be separately assessed as such. By CBDT Circular No. 8/2014 (31-3-2014) the whole share of profit credited to partners is exempt even if the firm's taxable income becomes nil because of exemptions/deductions. (Interest and salary from the firm are taxed as business income of the partner, Unit III.)
10(10BB)Payments to Bhopal gas leak victimsPaid under the Bhopal Gas Leak Disaster (Processing of Claims) Act, 1985 or a scheme under it. Not exempt to the extent a deduction was already allowed for the same loss.
10(10BC)Compensation on account of a disasterReceived by an individual or legal heir from the Central/State Government or a local authority. "Disaster" (as in the Disaster Management Act, 2005) = catastrophe, mishap, calamity or grave occurrence from natural or man-made causes, or by accident or negligence, causing substantial loss of life, human suffering, damage to property or to the environment, beyond the coping capacity of the community. Not exempt to the extent the loss was already allowed as a deduction.
10(16)ScholarshipGranted to meet the cost of education; exempt whatever the amount or source.
10(17)Allowances of MPs and MLAs OLD ONLYDaily allowance of an MP/MLA or of a committee of Parliament/State Legislature; constituency allowance of MPs under the MP (Constituency Allowance) Rules, 1986; constituency allowance of MLAs under State law.
10(17A)Awards and rewardsAny payment in cash or kind under an award instituted in the public interest by the Central/State Government or a body approved by the Central Government, or a reward by the Central/State Government for purposes approved by the Central Government in public interest.
10(18)Pension of gallantry award winnersIndividual was a Central/State Government employee awarded the Param Vir Chakra, Maha Vir Chakra, Vir Chakra or another notified gallantry award. After his death, family pension to a member of his family is also exempt. Family = spouse and children, and parents, brothers and sisters wholly or mainly dependent on him.
Circular 13/2019Disability pension of armed forces personnelThe whole disability pension (disability element and service element) is exempt for personnel of any rank invalided out of the naval, military or air forces because of a bodily disability attributable to or aggravated by service. Not for those retiring on superannuation or otherwise.
10(19A)Annual value of one palace of a former RulerPalace in the Ruler's occupation, whose annual value was exempt before 28-12-1971 under the Merged States / Part B States (Tax Concessions) Orders. In Maharao Bhim Singh of Kota v. CIT (2017) 390 ITR 532 the Supreme Court held the whole residential palace exempt even if part is let to a tenant.
From your course material — compensation to Amit's parents (sec 10(10BC))

₹5 lakh was paid on 17-3-2026 by the Government of Chhattisgarh to the parents of Amit, their only son, who died in a Maoist bomb blast on a local bus at Dantewada. Is it taxable in AY 2026-27?

  1. Sec 10(10BC) exempts compensation received by an individual or legal heir, from the Central/State Government or a local authority, on account of a disaster.
  2. A bomb blast is a man-made grave occurrence causing loss of life and human suffering beyond the coping capacity of the community, so it is a "disaster" as defined in the Disaster Management Act, 2005.
  3. The payer is a State Government and the receivers are the legal heirs. No deduction for the same loss has been claimed.
  4. The ₹5 lakh is fully exempt u/s 10(10BC); nothing is taxable in AY 2026-27.

Exemptions for institutions, funds and bodies — sec 10(20) to 10(48F)

These do not arise in individual computations, but your notes list them, so a Section A question can ask "state any five incomes exempt u/s 10 in the hands of institutions". Learn the section, the body, and one condition.

Definition — sec 14A (expenditure on exempt income)

No deduction is allowed, under any head, for expenditure incurred in relation to income that does not form part of total income. The Assessing Officer determines the disallowance by the method prescribed by the CBDT if he is not satisfied with the assessee's own claim, or if the assessee claims that no expenditure was incurred.

Exam tip

In a computation, show every exempt receipt with "Exempt u/s 10(…)" and "Nil" in the amount column. The examiner then sees you considered it. For a 5-mark "explain any five exempt incomes", pick items with clear conditions: 10(1), 10(2A), 10(10BC), 10(16), 10(18).

Self-check (exempt incomes)
1. A partner's firm pays no tax because of a deduction; is his share of profit taxable?

No. Circular 8/2014: the share of profit is exempt u/s 10(2A) even if the firm's tax is nil.

2. Flood compensation of ₹2 lakh from a State Government to a farmer. Taxable?

Exempt u/s 10(10BC), except to the extent the loss was already allowed as a deduction.

3. Is the daily allowance of an MLA exempt under the new regime?

No. Sec 10(17) is one of the exemptions not available under sec 115BAC.

4. A registered trade union earns rent and business income. What is exempt?

Only the house property and other-sources income, u/s 10(24). Business income is taxable.

Trap

Dividends from Indian companies are taxable in the hands of shareholders from AY 2021-22 (Dividend Distribution Tax was abolished by the Finance Act, 2020). Do not list dividends as exempt.

1.12 Residential status (sec 6)

Intuition

India taxes you on the basis of where you live in the previous year (days of physical presence), not your passport. Residents are taxed on world income; non-residents only on Indian income. The in-between category RNOR (resident but not ordinarily resident) exists for people who have just come back after long absence, so their foreign income is not taxed immediately.

Definition — individual, sec 6(1)

An individual is resident in India in a previous year if he satisfies any one of the basic conditions:
(a) he is in India for 182 days or more in the previous year; or
(b) he is in India for 60 days or more in the previous year and 365 days or more in the 4 years immediately preceding the previous year.
If neither is satisfied he is a non-resident (NR).

A resident is resident and ordinarily resident (ROR) if he satisfies both additional conditions [sec 6(6)]: (i) resident in at least 2 out of 10 preceding previous years, and (ii) in India for 730 days or more in the 7 preceding previous years. If he fails either, he is resident but not ordinarily resident (RNOR).

Exceptions to condition (b) — only the 182-day test applies

  1. An Indian citizen who leaves India in the PY for the purpose of employment outside India.
  2. An Indian citizen who leaves India in the PY as a member of the crew of an Indian ship. (For crew of a ship on a foreign-bound voyage, days on the voyage from the date of joining to the date of signing off, as per the Continuous Discharge Certificate, are excluded from stay in India — Explanation 2 and Rule 126.)
  3. An Indian citizen or a person of Indian origin (PIO) who, being outside India, comes on a visit to India in the PY — but if his total income, other than income from foreign sources, exceeds ₹15 lakh, condition (b) applies with 120 days in place of 60 days (Finance Act, 2020). Such a person, if resident only because of the 120-day rule, is always RNOR.
Definition — deemed resident, sec 6(1A)

An Indian citizen whose total income (other than income from foreign sources) exceeds ₹15 lakh and who is not liable to tax in any other country by reason of domicile or residence is deemed to be resident in India. He is always RNOR [sec 6(6)(d)]. Purpose: to catch "stateless" high-income citizens who spread their stay so as to be resident nowhere.

RNOR — four routes, sec 6(6)

A resident individual is RNOR if: (a) he has been a non-resident in 9 out of 10 preceding years, or (b) his stay in the 7 preceding years totals 729 days or less, or (c) he is a citizen/PIO resident only under the 120-day rule, or (d) he is a deemed resident u/s 6(1A).

Residential status of other persons

PersonResidentNon-residentROR / RNOR
HUF (sec 6(2))Control and management wholly or partly in IndiaControl and management wholly outside IndiaResident HUF is ROR if the karta satisfies both additional conditions of sec 6(6); otherwise RNOR
Firm / AOP / BOI / others (sec 6(2), 6(4))Control and management wholly or partly in IndiaWholly outside IndiaOnly resident or NR (no RNOR)
Company (sec 6(3))Indian company, or place of effective management (POEM) in India in that yearOtherwiseOnly resident or NR
Traps
  • "Resident" in sec 6 has nothing to do with citizenship: a foreigner can be ROR; an Indian citizen can be NR.
  • Status is decided for each previous year separately; last year's status does not carry over.
  • Stay need not be continuous or at the same place (Mr Sen's "not more than 15 days at any place" is irrelevant).
  • For the ROR test use 10 and 7 years preceding the PY (i.e., 2015-16 to 2024-25 and 2018-19 to 2024-25), not including the PY itself.
  • Maintaining a house in India (Rajinder) is irrelevant for individuals.
Worked example 1 — citizen leaving for employment
  1. Arjun, Indian citizen who always lived in India, leaves on 20 September 2025 for a job in Dubai. Stay in PY 2025-26 = Apr 30 + May 31 + Jun 30 + Jul 31 + Aug 31 + Sep 20 = 173 days.
  2. 173 < 182. Condition (b) would be satisfied (173 ≥ 60 and far more than 365 days in four preceding years) but it does not apply to a citizen leaving for employment.
  3. Non-resident for AY 2026-27. (Had he left on 1 October 2025, stay = 184 days ≥ 182 → resident.)
Worked example 2 — the 120-day rule
  1. Priya, Indian citizen settled in the USA, visits India for 130 days in PY 2025-26; she was in India for 400 days in the four preceding years. Her Indian income is ₹18 lakh.
  2. Indian income > ₹15 lakh, so condition (b) reads 120 days + 365 days: 130 ≥ 120 and 400 ≥ 365 → resident.
  3. Resident only because of the 120-day rule → RNOR [sec 6(6)(c)].
  4. If her Indian income were ₹10 lakh, only the 182-day test would apply → 130 < 182 → NR.
Worked example 3 — deemed resident
  1. Kabir, Indian citizen, lives in a country that levies no personal income tax and is not liable to tax there; stays 40 days in India; Indian income ₹20 lakh.
  2. Basic conditions fail (40 < 60). But he is a citizen, Indian income > ₹15 lakh, not liable to tax elsewhere → deemed resident u/s 6(1A).
  3. Status: RNOR [sec 6(6)(d)] — foreign income stays outside Indian tax unless from a business controlled or profession set up in India.

1.13 Residential status — all course problems solved

Convention used in every solution: both the day of arrival and the day of departure are counted as days in India. 2024 is a leap year (February 2024 has 29 days; 1 April 2023 – 31 March 2024 = 366 days) and 2020 is a leap year (1 April 2019 – 31 March 2020 = 366 days).

From your course material — (i) Mohan

Mohan, a citizen of India, left for Iran on 18 April 2025 and could not return till the end of FY 2025-26. Determine his status for AY 2026-27.

PeriodDays
1 April 2025 to 18 April 2025 (both days counted)18
  1. Condition (a): 18 < 182 — not satisfied.
  2. Condition (b): 18 < 60 — not satisfied (the four-year figure need not be examined).
  3. Mohan is a Non-resident for AY 2026-27. (Whether he left for employment or not makes no difference here.)
From your course material — (ii) Ramavtar

Ramavtar left for the USA on 10 March 2023 after living in India for 20 years. He returned on 10 September 2025.

  1. Basic condition (a) satisfied: 203 days ≥ 182 → Resident.
  2. Additional condition (i): resident in at least 2 of the 10 preceding years (2015-16 to 2022-23 — eight years) → satisfied.
  3. Additional condition (ii): stay in 7 preceding years (2018-19 to 2024-25) = 365 + 366 + 365 + 365 + 344 + 0 + 0 = 1,805 days ≥ 730 → satisfied.
  4. Ramavtar is Resident and Ordinarily Resident (ROR) for AY 2026-27.
From your course material — (iii) Rajinder

Rajinder, a citizen of India, left on 15 May 2025 for London for higher studies and came back on 25 May 2026. He maintained a dwelling place in India.

  2. His return on 25-5-2026 falls in PY 2026-27 and is irrelevant; the dwelling place in India is irrelevant for an individual.
  3. Deemed residence u/s 6(1A) cannot be applied: nothing suggests Indian income above ₹15 lakh (a student).
  4. Rajinder is a Non-resident for AY 2026-27.
From your course material — Illustration 4(a): Mr Sen

Mr Sen came to India on 10 May 2023 after staying 10 years in the USA. He stayed in India up to 4 January 2026 and left for the USA on 5 January 2026. During his stay he did not stay more than 15 days at any place. Determine his status for PY 2025-26.

  1. Basic condition (a): 280 ≥ 182 → Resident. (The result is the same even if the departure day is excluded: 279 days.)
  2. Additional condition (i): resident in 2 of 10 preceding years (2023-24, 2024-25) → satisfied.

Answer layout that earns full marks

(1) State sec 6(1) basic conditions and sec 6(6) additional conditions; (2) day-count table; (3) test each condition with "satisfied / not satisfied"; (4) conclusion in bold with AY; (5) note any red herrings (dwelling place, 15 days per place, citizenship).

1.14 Scope of total income (sec 5) and incidence of tax

Definition

Sec 5 defines the scope of total income according to residential status. ROR: global income. RNOR: Indian income + foreign income from a business controlled from India or a profession set up in India. NR: only Indian income (received or deemed received in India, or accruing or deemed to accrue in India — sec 7 and sec 9).

Nature of incomeRORRNORNR
1. Received or deemed to be received in India (wherever earned)TaxableTaxableTaxable
2. Accrues/arises or deemed to accrue in India (wherever received)TaxableTaxableTaxable
3. Accrues and received outside India from a business controlled from India or a profession set up in IndiaTaxableTaxableNot taxable
4. Accrues and received outside India from any other source (business controlled from outside, foreign rent, foreign interest, foreign agricultural income)TaxableNot taxableNot taxable
5. Past untaxed foreign income brought into India in the PYNot taxableNot taxableNot taxable
Key rules
  • The first receipt decides place of receipt: income received abroad and later remitted to India is "received outside India".
  • Salary for services rendered in India is deemed to accrue in India (sec 9(1)(ii)) even if paid abroad.
  • Indian agricultural income is exempt for everyone; foreign agricultural income is taxable only for ROR (category 4).
Worked example — classification of income (typical 10-mark problem)

Mr Anand has the following incomes for PY 2025-26. Compute his gross total income if he is (a) ROR, (b) RNOR, (c) NR.

#Income₹CategoryRORRNORNR
1Salary for services in India, received in India3,00,0001/23,00,0003,00,0003,00,000
2Business in London, controlled from India (received in London)1,00,00031,00,0001,00,000–
3Profit of business in Delhi, received in London80,000280,00080,00080,000
4Dividend from a foreign company, received abroad20,000420,000––
5Rent of house in Paris received in Paris, later remitted to India60,000460,000––
6Past untaxed foreign income brought into India50,0005–––
7Interest on UK bonds received in London30,000430,000––
8Agricultural income from Kenya, received there40,000440,000––
9Short-term capital gain on shares of an Indian company, received abroad70,000270,00070,00070,000
10Profession in Singapore, set up and controlled there, received there90,000490,000––

Check: ROR = 3,00,000 + 1,00,000 + 80,000 + 20,000 + 60,000 + 30,000 + 40,000 + 70,000 + 90,000 = 7,90,000; RNOR = 3,00,000 + 1,00,000 + 80,000 + 70,000 = 5,50,000; NR = 3,00,000 + 80,000 + 70,000 = 4,50,000.

Self-check
1. An NR's salary for work done in Pune is paid into his US account. Taxable?

Yes — salary for services rendered in India is deemed to accrue in India (sec 9).

2. An RNOR earns rent from a flat in Dubai, received in Dubai. Taxable?

No — it is foreign income from a source other than a business controlled from India.

1.15 Unit I exam kit

Likely questions: Distinguish direct and indirect taxes (5) · Define person/assessee; types of assessee (5) · PY and exceptions (5) · Agricultural income and partial integration (5/10) · Capital vs revenue receipts — tests (5) · Residential status rules for individuals with problems (10) · Scope of total income with classification problem (10) · Tax evasion vs avoidance vs planning (5).

Model answer (5 marks) — "Explain 'previous year' and the exceptions to the general rule"

Introduction. Income tax is charged in an assessment year on the income of the previous year (sec 4). Assessment year [sec 2(9)] is the 12-month period starting 1 April; previous year [sec 3] is the financial year immediately preceding it. For example, income of PY 2025-26 is assessed in AY 2026-27.

Newly set-up business / new source. The PY starts on the date of setting up (or when the source comes into existence) and ends on the next 31 March, so it may be shorter than 12 months.

Exceptions (income taxed in the PY itself): (1) shipping business of non-residents (sec 172); (2) persons leaving India permanently (sec 174); (3) AOP/BOI formed for a particular event (sec 174A); (4) persons likely to transfer property to avoid tax (sec 175); (5) discontinued business (sec 176). These protect revenue where the assessee may not be available later.

Conclusion. The PY/AY pair gives time to compute and verify income; from 1 April 2026 the Income-tax Act, 2025 replaces it with a single "tax year".

Model answer (10 marks) — "Explain the rules for determining the residential status of an individual and the incidence of tax"

Introduction. Tax incidence in India depends on residential status (sec 6) and not on citizenship. Status is determined for each previous year separately on the basis of physical stay in India.

1. Basic conditions — sec 6(1). Resident if (a) in India 182 days or more in the PY, or (b) 60 days or more in the PY and 365 days or more in the four preceding years. Failing both → non-resident.

2. Exceptions to (b). Only 182 days applies for (i) Indian citizens leaving for employment abroad, (ii) Indian citizens leaving as crew of an Indian ship, (iii) citizens/PIOs visiting India. For visitors with Indian income above ₹15 lakh, 60 days becomes 120 days.

3. Deemed resident — sec 6(1A). Indian citizen with Indian income above ₹15 lakh not liable to tax in any country → deemed resident (RNOR).

4. Additional conditions — sec 6(6). A resident is ROR if resident in 2 of 10 preceding years and in India 730 days or more in 7 preceding years; otherwise RNOR. 120-day-rule residents and deemed residents are always RNOR.

5. Incidence — sec 5. ROR: global income. RNOR: Indian income plus foreign income from a business controlled or profession set up in India. NR: Indian income only. Past untaxed foreign income remitted to India is not taxable for anyone.

Illustration. Mr Sen (course problem) stayed 280 days in PY 2025-26 → resident; but only 692 days in 7 preceding years → RNOR; his US income is not taxable in India unless from a business controlled from India.

Conclusion. Residential status is the first step of every computation because it fixes which incomes enter GTI.

Model answer (5 marks) — "What is agricultural income? Explain partial integration."

Meaning. Sec 2(1A): rent or revenue from land in India used for agriculture; income from agriculture, from processes that make the produce marketable, and from sale of such produce by the cultivator; income from farm buildings; income from nursery saplings. Exempt u/s 10(1) because agricultural income is a State subject (Entry 46, List II).

Tests. Land in India; used for agricultural purposes (basic operations); income directly derived from the land; processing limited to making produce marketable.

Partial integration. For individuals/HUF, if net agricultural income exceeds ₹5,000 and non-agricultural income exceeds the basic exemption limit: tax on (non-agri + agri) minus tax on (agri + basic exemption limit). Example: non-agri 18L, agri 3L → 2,25,000 − 15,000 = ₹2,10,000 + cess.

Conclusion. Agricultural income is exempt but raises the rate applicable to other income.

Unit II — Income from Salary

10 hours; CO2. The most numerical unit after Unit V. Master the three-column habit: Amount received | Exempt | Taxable, and always mark which exemptions survive the new regime.

2.1 Meaning, chargeability and basis of charge (sec 15, 16, 17)

Intuition

Anything of value an employee gets because of the job — cash, a flat, a car, a loan at zero interest, even a lump sum when the job ends — is "salary" in the wide tax sense. The Act then carves out specific exemptions and allows one flat deduction.

Definition — sec 17(1) (from your course notes)

"Salary" includes: (i) wages; (ii) any annuity or pension; (iii) any gratuity; (iv) any fees, commission, perquisites or profits in lieu of or in addition to salary or wages; (v) any advance of salary; (vi) any payment received for any period of leave not availed (leave salary / leave encashment); (vii) the portion of annual accretion to the balance of a recognised provident fund to the extent taxable, and the transferred balance to the extent taxable; (viii) the contribution made by the Central Government or any other employer to the employee's account under a pension scheme referred to in sec 80CCD (NPS); and (ix) the contribution by the Central Government to the Agniveer Corpus Fund account of a person enrolled in the Agnipath Scheme (sec 80CCH).

Chargeability — sec 15

Salary is chargeable in the PY on the earliest of: (a) salary due from an employer (present or former) in the PY, whether paid or not; (b) salary paid or allowed in the PY, though not due, e.g., advance salary; (c) arrears of salary paid in the PY, if not taxed earlier. Salary once taxed on due basis is not taxed again on receipt.

Essential features

  1. Employer–employee relationship must exist. Tests: control and supervision over the manner of work; right to hire and dismiss; payment of wages; exclusive service; integration into the organisation. A contract of service → salary; a contract for service (consultant, professional) → PGBP.
  2. Payment may be from a present, former or prospective employer (pension from former employer; signing bonus from prospective employer).
  3. Salary forgone voluntarily after it accrues is still taxable (application of income); surrender of salary to the Central Government u/s 2 of the Voluntary Surrender of Salaries (Exemption from Taxation) Act, 1961 is not taxable.
  4. Tax paid by the employer on non-monetary perquisites (sec 10(10CC)) is exempt; tax borne by employer on salary otherwise is a perquisite.
  5. Place of accrual: salary for services in India accrues in India (sec 9(1)(ii)), wherever paid.
  6. MP/MLA salaries are taxed under "Other sources" (no employer–employee relationship); partner's salary from the firm is PGBP (sec 28(v)); director's sitting fees are "Other sources" unless he is an employee.
  7. Salary includes salary received in the capacity of an employee from a foreign government or UN subject to specific exemptions.
Format of computation

Basic salary + DA + bonus + commission + fees + taxable allowances + taxable perquisites + taxable retirement benefits + employer's contribution to RPF in excess of 12% + interest on RPF above 9.5% + employer's NPS contribution = Gross salary − deductions u/s 16 = Income under the head "Salaries" (also called taxable salary).

2.2 Treatment of allowances

Definition

An allowance is a fixed monetary amount paid regularly by the employer, in addition to salary, to meet specific expenses (rent, travel, children's education) or particular working conditions. All allowances are taxable unless specifically exempt u/s 10.

A. Fully taxable allowances BOTH

Dearness allowance / dearness pay; city compensatory allowance; overtime allowance; lunch/tiffin allowance; fixed medical allowance; servant allowance; cash allowance; officiating allowance; non-practising allowance; project/deputation allowance; family allowance; warden and proctor allowance; entertainment allowance (for all employees — the deduction for Government employees comes separately u/s 16(ii), old regime only); transport allowance to an ordinary (non-disabled) employee.

B. Partly exempt allowances — exempt to the extent spent [sec 10(14)(i), Rule 2BB(1)]

AllowancePurposeExemptionNew regime?
Travelling / tour / transfer allowanceOfficial travel or transferAmount received or spent for the purpose, whichever is lessALLOWED
Daily allowanceOrdinary daily charges while on tour away from normal duty placeSameALLOWED
Conveyance allowanceConveyance in performance of official duties (not home–office travel)SameALLOWED
Helper allowanceHelper engaged for official dutiesSameNot allowed
Academic / research allowanceAcademic, research or training in educational/research institutionsSameNot allowed
Uniform allowancePurchase/maintenance of uniform for official dutiesSameNot allowed

C. Partly exempt — fixed monthly limits [sec 10(14)(ii), Rule 2BB(2)] OLD REGIME ONLY unless stated

AllowanceExempt amount
Children education allowance₹100 per month per child, maximum 2 children
Hostel expenditure allowance₹300 per month per child, maximum 2 children
Transport allowance to a blind, deaf and dumb, or orthopaedically handicapped employee (home–office commuting)₹3,200 per month ALLOWED IN NEW REGIME
Transport allowance to employees of a transport undertaking (running allowance)70% of the allowance, up to ₹10,000 per month
Tribal area / Scheduled area allowance (specified States)₹200 per month
Underground allowance (mines)₹800 per month
High altitude allowance₹1,060 per month (9,000–15,000 ft); ₹1,600 per month (above 15,000 ft)
Border area / remote area / difficult area allowance₹200 to ₹1,300 per month depending on the notified location

D. Fully exempt allowances

Allowances paid by the Government to Indian citizens posted abroad (sec 10(7)); sumptuary allowance to High Court/Supreme Court judges; allowances from the UNO; compensatory allowance to judges under Article 222(2).

E. Entertainment allowance — deduction u/s 16(ii) OLD REGIME ONLY

First include the full allowance in salary. Then, for Government employees only, deduct the least of: (a) ₹5,000; (b) 20% of basic salary (excluding allowances, perquisites); (c) actual entertainment allowance received. Actual expenditure on entertainment is irrelevant. Non-Government employees get no deduction.

Indian example

In a typical IT-company salary slip in Bengaluru (e.g., at a firm like Wipro or Infosys), components such as "HRA", "LTA", "special allowance" and "meal card" appear. Under the default new regime, the HRA and LTA lines are fully taxable, which is why many employees compare regimes each April and declare their choice to the employer for TDS.

Trap

Conveyance allowance for travel between home and office is not "official duty" — it is fully taxable (see Mr A's problem). Transport allowance is exempt only for specially-abled employees.

2.3 House rent allowance — sec 10(13A), Rule 2A OLD REGIME ONLY

Formula — exempt HRA = least of

(a) HRA received for the period of residence in rented accommodation;
(b) Rent paid in excess of 10% of salary (Rent − 10% of salary);
(c) 50% of salary if the house is in Mumbai, Kolkata, Delhi or Chennai; 40% of salary elsewhere.

Salary = basic + DA (only if it forms part of retirement benefits) + commission as a fixed % of turnover, for the period of residence in rented house, on due basis. Taxable HRA = received − exempt. If the employee lives in his own house or pays no rent, HRA is fully taxable. Under the new regime the whole HRA is taxable.

Worked example 1 — Mumbai
  1. Basic ₹50,000 p.m., DA ₹10,000 p.m. (forms part of retirement benefits), HRA ₹25,000 p.m., rent paid ₹28,000 p.m. in Mumbai. Salary = (50,000 + 10,000) × 12 = ₹7,20,000.
  2. (a) HRA received = ₹3,00,000.
  3. (b) Rent − 10% of salary = 3,36,000 − 72,000 = ₹2,64,000.
  4. (c) 50% of salary (Mumbai) = ₹3,60,000.
  5. Exempt = least = ₹2,64,000; taxable HRA = 3,00,000 − 2,64,000 = ₹36,000 (old regime); ₹3,00,000 (new regime).
Worked example 2 — reverse problem

Salary for HRA is ₹6,00,000; the employee in Pune receives HRA ₹1,80,000. What minimum annual rent makes the whole HRA exempt?

  1. Condition (c): 40% × 6,00,000 = 2,40,000 ≥ 1,80,000 — not binding.
  2. Condition (b) must be at least 1,80,000: Rent − 60,000 ≥ 1,80,000 ⇒ Rent ≥ ₹2,40,000.
  3. Minimum rent = ₹2,40,000 a year (₹20,000 p.m.).
Course vs industry

For AY 2026-27 only the four cities (Mumbai, Kolkata, Delhi, Chennai) get 50%; Bengaluru and Hyderabad use 40%. The Income-tax Rules framed under the 2025 Act, applicable from tax year 2026-27, propose adding Bengaluru, Hyderabad, Pune and Ahmedabad to the 50% list — not relevant for your AY 2026-27 exam unless the question says so.

2.4 Perquisites and their valuation — sec 17(2), Rule 3

Definition

A perquisite is any casual emolument, benefit or advantage, in cash or in kind, provided by the employer to the employee (or his household) over and above salary, by virtue of employment. Sec 17(2) lists: (i) rent-free accommodation; (ii) concession in rent; (iii) benefits to specified employees (car, servants, utilities, free education etc.); (iv) any obligation of the employee met by the employer; (v) life-insurance/annuity premium paid by the employer; (vi) specified securities/sweat equity (ESOPs); (vii) employer's contribution to RPF + NPS + superannuation fund in excess of ₹7,50,000 in a year (and accretions on it); (viii) any other fringe benefit as prescribed (Rule 3(7)).

Three categories

Taxable for all employeesTaxable only for specified employeesTax-free for all
Rent-free / concessional accommodation; any obligation of the employee paid by the employer (his electricity bill, his servant's salary, his club bill, school fees paid directly); life insurance premium on the employee's life; ESOPs; interest-free loans (above ₹20,000); gifts above ₹5,000; credit card, club facility; use/transfer of movable assets; employer's excess retirement contributionsMotor car provided by employer and used partly/wholly for private purposes; domestic servants engaged by the employer; gas, electricity, water in the employer's name; free/concessional education to members of household (value > ₹1,000 p.m. per child in employer's institution); free transport by a transport undertakingMedical treatment in employer-maintained or Government hospital (and approved hospitals for prescribed diseases); telephone/mobile/computer/laptop; refreshments during office hours; free meals at the office up to ₹50 per meal BOTH (through paid vouchers OLD ONLY); employer's contribution to group insurance/ESI; training/refresher courses; tax on non-monetary perquisites paid by employer; recreational facilities for employees generally; accommodation at a mining site/remote area
Definition — specified employee, sec 17(2)(iii)

(a) A director-employee; (b) an employee with substantial interest in the employer company (beneficial owner of 20% or more voting power); (c) any other employee whose income under the head "Salaries" (excluding the value of non-monetary perquisites) exceeds ₹4,00,000 — raised from ₹50,000 by the Finance Act, 2025 read with CBDT Notification No. 133/2025, applicable for AY 2026-27.

Course vs industry

Older textbooks and many question papers still use ₹50,000. In every course problem below the employee exceeds ₹4,00,000 as well, so the answer does not change. If a paper states ₹50,000, follow the paper.

2.4.1 Rent-free accommodation (RFA) — Rule 3(1), revised from 1-9-2023 (your course notes)

EmployeeAccommodationValue of unfurnished RFA
Central/State GovernmentAnyLicence fee determined by the Government
Non-Government (others)Owned by employerPopulation (2011 census) > 40 lakh: 10% of salary; > 15 lakh but ≤ 40 lakh: 7.5%; other places: 5%. (FRV/MV is irrelevant.)
Taken on lease/rent by employerActual lease rent paid/payable by employer or 10% of salary, whichever is lower
  • Salary for RFA = basic + DA (if part of retirement benefits) + bonus + commission + fees + all taxable allowances + any monetary payment chargeable as salary, for the period of occupation, on due basis. Excludes: DA not forming part, employer's PF contribution, exempt allowances, value of perquisites, lump-sum terminal benefits (gratuity, leave encashment on retirement, commuted pension, VRS).
  • Furnished: add 10% p.a. of cost of furniture (if owned by employer) or actual hire charges (if hired), for the period.
  • Concessional accommodation: value as above minus rent recovered from the employee.
  • Hotel accommodation: 24% of salary or actual charges, whichever lower; nil if on transfer for up to 15 days.
  • Accommodation at a mining site, project site, dam site, remote area (conditions) — not a perquisite.

2.4.2 Motor car — Rule 3(2) (values applicable for AY 2026-27)

CaseUsed only for official dutiesUsed only for private purposesUsed partly official, partly private
1. Car owned/hired by employer; running and maintenance borne by employerNil (with records)Actual running + maintenance + chauffeur's salary + 10% p.a. of cost (or hire charges) − recovery₹1,800 p.m. (engine ≤ 1.6 L) or ₹2,400 p.m. (> 1.6 L) + ₹900 p.m. if chauffeur provided
2. Car owned/hired by employer; expenses borne by employeeNil10% p.a. of cost (or hire charges) + chauffeur's salary − recovery₹600 p.m. (≤ 1.6 L) or ₹900 p.m. (> 1.6 L) + ₹900 p.m. for chauffeur
3. Car owned by employee; expenses reimbursed by employerNil (with records)Actual expenditure incurred by employer (an obligation — taxable for all employees)Actual expenditure − ₹1,800 p.m. (≤ 1.6 L) or ₹2,400 p.m. (> 1.6 L) − ₹900 p.m. if chauffeur
4. Employee's own other conveyance (e.g., two-wheeler), expenses by employerNilActual expenditureActual expenditure − ₹900 p.m.

Travel between residence and office counts as private use. Cases 1 and 2 (car provided by employer) are taxable only for specified employees; case 3 private use is an obligation of the employee and taxable for all.

Course vs industry

The Income-tax Rules under the 2025 Act (effective 1 April 2026, tax year 2026-27) propose much higher car values (for example ₹5,000 and ₹7,000 per month, and ₹3,000 for a chauffeur). For AY 2026-27 — your exam year — use ₹1,800 / ₹2,400 / ₹900 as in your course notes.

2.4.3 Other perquisites (summary of valuation)

PerquisiteValuation rule
Domestic servants (sweeper, gardener, watchman, personal attendant)Salary paid by employer − amount recovered. Engaged by employer: specified employees only. Engaged by employee and paid by employer: obligation, taxable for all.
Gas, electricity, waterAmount paid by employer to the agency (or manufacturing cost if from own resources) − recovery. Connection in employer's name: specified only; in employee's name: all.
Free/concessional educationIn employer's own institution: cost of such education in a similar institution nearby, less ₹1,000 p.m. per child (exempt if value ≤ ₹1,000 p.m.). Fees paid directly to school or reimbursed: fully taxable for all.
Interest-free / concessional loanInterest at SBI's lending rate as on 1 April of the PY on the maximum outstanding monthly balance − interest paid. Nil if aggregate loans ≤ ₹20,000 or loan for treatment of specified diseases (Rule 3A).
Free food / non-alcoholic beveragesCost to employer − recovery. Exempt: tea/snacks in office hours; food in remote area/offshore site; meals up to ₹50 per meal provided at the office or business premises during working hours BOTH; the same ₹50 relief for meals through non-transferable paid vouchers OLD ONLY (the second proviso to Rule 3(7)(iii) takes away only the voucher route from a new-regime employee).
Gifts, vouchers, tokensIf the aggregate value of gifts in kind/vouchers in the PY is below ₹5,000: nil; if ₹5,000 or more, the entire value is taxable (ICAI view). A cash gift is fully taxable.
Medical facilitiesExempt: treatment in employer's hospital, Government hospital, or approved hospital for prescribed diseases; medical insurance premium paid by employer. Treatment abroad: cost of treatment and stay exempt to the extent permitted by RBI; travel exempt only if GTI ≤ ₹8,00,000 (raised from ₹2 lakh by Notification 133/2025). Reimbursement of treatment in a private hospital: fully taxable.
