Use of movable assets (other than computers/laptops)10% p.a. of actual cost (if owned) or hire charges − recovery.
Transfer of movable assetsCost − depreciation (computers 50% WDV, cars 20% WDV, others 10% SLM per completed year) − amount paid.
Free/concessional travel (LTC through employer, holiday)Value at which such facility is offered to the public − recovery (LTC exemption u/s 10(5) in old regime only).
Employer-paid insurance premiumPremium on life insurance of employee: taxable; group insurance/ESI/accident insurance for all employees: exempt.
ESOPs / sweat equity (sec 17(2)(vi))FMV of shares on the date of exercise − amount paid by employee. Later sale: capital gain with cost = that FMV. Employees of eligible start-ups can defer TDS/payment.
Employer's contribution to RPF + NPS + approved superannuation fundAggregate above ₹7,50,000 in a PY is taxable (sec 17(2)(vii)) with annual accretion on it (17(2)(viia)).
Worked example — ESOP and loan
  1. ESOP: 500 shares allotted on exercise at ₹100 each when FMV is ₹460. Perquisite = 500 × (460 − 100) = ₹1,80,000 (salary). If sold later at ₹600, capital gain = 500 × (600 − 460) = ₹70,000.
  2. Loan: an employee takes an interest-free housing loan of ₹5,00,000 on 1-4-2025, repaid only in 2026-27; assume SBI rate 10% p.a. (given in question). Perquisite = 5,00,000 × 10% = ₹50,000.
Self-check
1. Employer pays the school fees of the employee's child directly to the school. Specified-employee test needed?

No. It discharges the employee's obligation (sec 17(2)(iv)) — taxable for all employees.

2. A 1.8-litre company car with chauffeur, used partly privately, expenses by employer — monthly value?

₹2,400 + ₹900 = ₹3,300 p.m. (for a specified employee).

3. Diwali gift voucher of ₹4,500 — taxable?

No, aggregate below ₹5,000.

2.5 Treatment of provident funds

Intuition

A provident fund is a compulsory savings pot: part of your salary plus the employer's matching share, earning interest, paid out when you leave. The tax treatment depends on how "official" the fund is: Statutory (Government) > Recognised (approved by the PF Commissioner) > Unrecognised. The Public PF is open to anyone, salaried or not.

ItemStatutory PF (SPF)Recognised PF (RPF)Unrecognised PF (URPF)Public PF (PPF)
WhoGovernment, railways, universities, local authorities (Provident Funds Act, 1925)Organisations covered by EPF & MP Act, 1952, or recognised by Commissioner of Income-taxFund not recognisedAny individual (Public Provident Fund Act, 1968 / PPF Scheme, 2019)
Employer's contributionExemptExempt up to 12% of salary; excess taxable as salaryNot taxable yearlyNo employer contribution
Employee's contribution80C deduction OLD80C deduction OLDNo deduction80C deduction OLD
Interest creditedExempt*Exempt up to 9.5% p.a.; excess taxable as salary*Not taxable yearlyExempt
Lump sum on retirement/terminationExempt (sec 10(11))Exempt (sec 10(12)) if continuous service ≥ 5 years, or termination due to ill-health/closure, or transfer to new employer's RPF/NPS; otherwise treated as URPF from the beginningEmployer's contribution + interest on it: taxable as salary (profits in lieu); interest on employee's contribution: taxable under other sources; employee's own contribution: not taxableExempt

"Salary" for RPF = basic + DA (if part of retirement benefits) + commission as a fixed % of turnover. *Interest on the employee's own contribution exceeding ₹2,50,000 per year (₹5,00,000 where the employer does not contribute) is taxable (provisos to sec 10(11) and 10(12), from AY 2022-23). Employer's contributions to RPF + NPS + superannuation above ₹7,50,000 in aggregate are taxable u/s 17(2)(vii).

Worked example — RPF
  1. Basic ₹40,000 p.m., DA ₹10,000 p.m. (forms part). Employer's RPF contribution 14% of basic + DA; interest credited at 11% = ₹22,000.
  2. Salary for RPF = 50,000 × 12 = ₹6,00,000. Employer's contribution = 14% = ₹84,000; exempt 12% = ₹72,000; taxable ₹12,000.
  3. Interest: taxable portion = 22,000 × (11 − 9.5) ÷ 11 = ₹3,000.

2.6 Retirement benefits and profits in lieu of salary

2.6.1 Gratuity — sec 10(10) BOTH

Definition

Gratuity is a lump-sum payment by the employer in recognition of long and meritorious service, paid on retirement, death, disablement, resignation or termination. Gratuity received during service is fully taxable.

Traps
  • PoG-covered: "salary" = basic + all DA, last drawn; 15/26 (26 working days a month); 26 years 8 months → 27 years.
  • Not covered: "salary" = basic + DA only if part of retirement benefits + commission as a fixed % of turnover; average of 10 months; half month; 26 years 8 months → 26 years.
  • Bonus is never part of "salary" for gratuity.
  • The ₹20 lakh limit is a lifetime aggregate across employers.

2.6.2 Pension — uncommuted and commuted, sec 10(10A) BOTH

  • Uncommuted pension (periodic): fully taxable as salary for all employees (standard deduction available).
  • Commuted pension (lump sum in lieu of a part of pension):
    • Government, local authority, statutory corporation, defence employees: fully exempt.
    • Others, if gratuity is also received: 1/3 of the full value of commuted pension is exempt.
    • Others, if gratuity is not received: 1/2 of the full value of commuted pension is exempt.
  • Full value of commuted pension = commuted amount received ÷ percentage commuted × 100.
  • Family pension received by legal heirs: taxable under "Other sources" with sec 57(iia) deduction (Unit IV).

2.6.3 Leave encashment — sec 10(10AA) (theory, as per course plan) BOTH

  • During service: fully taxable (for all employees).
  • On retirement/resignation — Government employees: fully exempt.
  • On retirement — non-Government employees: exempt up to the least of:
    1. ₹25,00,000 (limit from 1-4-2023; aggregate for all employers);
    2. Leave salary received;
    3. 10 months' average salary;
    4. Cash equivalent of leave to credit, computed at 30 days for every completed year of service (leave entitlement above 30 days/year is ignored), based on average salary.
  • Average salary = average of salary for the 10 months immediately preceding retirement; salary = basic + DA (if terms provide) + commission as a fixed % of turnover.
  • Leave encashment received by legal heirs of a deceased employee is not taxable.
Worked example — leave encashment (non-Government)
  1. Mr Kumar retires after 20 years; average salary of last 10 months = ₹50,000 p.m.; leave entitlement 45 days per year; leave availed during service 400 days; leave encashment received ₹6,00,000.
  2. Leave to credit for exemption = (20 × 30) − 400 = 200 days = 200 ÷ 30 = 6.667 months.
  3. Limits: (a) ₹25,00,000; (b) actual ₹6,00,000; (c) 10 × 50,000 = ₹5,00,000; (d) 6.667 × 50,000 = ₹3,33,333.
  4. Exempt = ₹3,33,333; taxable = 6,00,000 − 3,33,333 = ₹2,66,667.

2.6.4 Retrenchment compensation — sec 10(10B)

Exempt: least of (a) actual compensation, (b) amount calculated under sec 25F(b) of the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 (15 days' average pay for every completed year of continuous service or part in excess of 6 months), (c) ₹5,00,000. Fully exempt if paid under a scheme approved by the Central Government.

Worked example
  1. Service 12 years 7 months → 13 years; average pay ₹52,000 p.m.; compensation received ₹6,00,000.
  2. ID Act amount = 15/26 × 52,000 × 13 = ₹3,90,000.
  3. Exempt = least of 6,00,000; 3,90,000; 5,00,000 = ₹3,90,000; taxable ₹2,10,000.

2.6.5 Voluntary retirement compensation — sec 10(10C)

Exempt up to ₹5,00,000 if the scheme conforms to Rule 2BA (employee has completed 10 years or is 40 years old, etc.). Amount beyond the limit is taxable; relief u/s 89 is not available if exemption is claimed. The compensation limit under Rule 2BA is 3 months' salary for each completed year of service or salary × balance months of service left, whichever is less.

2.6.6 Profits in lieu of salary — sec 17(3)

  1. Compensation received from the employer on termination of employment or modification of its terms (unless exempt under 10(10B)/(10C) etc.).
  2. Payment from an unrecognised provident fund or unapproved superannuation fund to the extent of employer's contribution and interest thereon.
  3. Sum received under a Keyman insurance policy, including bonus.
  4. Any lump sum received before joining (e.g., joining bonus) or after cessation of employment.

Exceptions: gratuity u/s 10(10), commuted pension u/s 10(10A), HRA, etc. are dealt with under their own sections.

Self-check
1. A private employee (not PoG-covered) served 18 years 11 months. How many years count for gratuity?

18 years (fraction ignored).

2. A non-Government employee receives ₹2,00,000 commuted pension for 50% of pension and also gratuity. Exempt amount?

Full value = 2,00,000 ÷ 50% = 4,00,000; exempt 1/3 = ₹1,33,333; taxable ₹66,667.

2.7 Deductions from gross salary — sec 16

SectionDeductionNew regimeOld regime
16(ia)Standard deduction: flat amount or salary, whichever is lower; available to all salaried employees and pensioners₹75,000₹50,000
16(ii)Entertainment allowance — Government employees only: least of ₹5,000, 20% of basic, actual allowanceNot allowedAllowed
16(iii)Professional tax (tax on employment levied by States under Art. 276, maximum ₹2,500 a year) paid by the employee, or by the employer on his behalf (then first added as a perquisite)Not allowedAllowed
Trap

Standard deduction is available per assessee, not per employer: two employers in the year still give one ₹75,000.

2.8 Salary problems — every course problem solved in full

OCR reconstruction note

Your salary handout was scanned; symbols such as "%", "&", "Z", "€" and "#" stand for "₹". Where a word or figure was unreadable, the assumption used is stated inside each solution. All problems are solved for PY 2025-26 / AY 2026-27.

From your course material — Mr Ravi (gratuity, three cases)

Mr Ravi retired on 15-6-2025 after 26 years 8 months of service and received gratuity of ₹15,00,000. Salary at retirement: basic ₹50,000 p.m.; DA ₹10,000 p.m. (60% of which is for retirement benefits); commission 1% of turnover (turnover in the last 12 months ₹1,20,00,000); bonus ₹25,000 p.a. Compute taxable gratuity if (a) private employee covered by the Payment of Gratuity Act, 1972; (b) private employee not covered; (c) Government employee.

(a) Covered by the Payment of Gratuity Act

  1. Salary = last drawn basic + full DA = 50,000 + 10,000 = ₹60,000 p.m. (commission and bonus excluded).
  2. Completed years: 26 years 8 months → part exceeding 6 months counts as a full year → 27 years.
  3. 15/26 × 60,000 × 27 = ₹9,34,615 (rounded to the nearest rupee).
Particulars₹
Gratuity received15,00,000
Less: exempt u/s 10(10)(ii) — least of: (i) statutory limit ₹20,00,000; (ii) actual ₹15,00,000; (iii) 15/26 × 60,000 × 27 = ₹9,34,6159,34,615
Taxable gratuity5,65,385

(b) Not covered by the Payment of Gratuity Act

  1. Salary = basic + DA forming part of retirement benefits + commission at a fixed % of turnover, averaged over the 10 months preceding the month of retirement (August 2024 – May 2025).
  2. DA forming part = 60% × 10,000 = ₹6,000 p.m.
  3. Commission: turnover of the last 12 months is ₹1,20,00,000; assuming it accrued evenly, turnover of the last 10 months = ₹1,00,00,000; commission at 1% = ₹1,00,000 for 10 months = ₹10,000 p.m.
  4. Average salary = 50,000 + 6,000 + 10,000 = ₹66,000 p.m.
  5. Completed years = 26 (the 8 months are ignored).
  6. 1/2 × 66,000 × 26 = ₹8,58,000.
Particulars₹
Gratuity received15,00,000
Less: exempt u/s 10(10)(iii) — least of: (i) ₹20,00,000; (ii) ₹15,00,000; (iii) ₹8,58,0008,58,000
Taxable gratuity6,42,000

(c) Government employee: gratuity is fully exempt u/s 10(10)(i). Taxable gratuity = Nil.

Bonus of ₹25,000 is ignored in all three cases. The taxable gratuity is part of gross salary; exemption u/s 10(10) is available under both regimes.

From your course material — Mr Sagar (commuted pension, three cases)

Mr Sagar retired on 1-10-2025 and receives pension of ₹5,000 p.m. On 1-2-2026 he commuted 60% of his pension and received ₹3,00,000. Compute taxable pension if (a) Government employee; (b) private employee who received gratuity of ₹5,00,000; (c) private employee who received no gratuity.

Working notes

  1. Uncommuted pension (fully taxable in all cases): October 2025 – January 2026 = 4 × 5,000 = ₹20,000; February – March 2026 = 2 × (40% × 5,000) = 2 × 2,000 = ₹4,000. Total = ₹24,000.
  2. Full value of commuted pension = 3,00,000 ÷ 60% = ₹5,00,000.
Particulars(a) Govt(b) Private, with gratuity(c) Private, no gratuity
Uncommuted pension24,00024,00024,000
Commuted pension received3,00,0003,00,0003,00,000
Less exempt u/s 10(10A)3,00,000 (fully)1,66,667 (1/3 of 5,00,000)2,50,000 (1/2 of 5,00,000)
Taxable commuted pensionNil1,33,33350,000
Taxable pension24,0001,57,33374,000

These amounts are included in gross salary; standard deduction of ₹75,000 (new regime) is then allowed on the total salary income. In case (b), the gratuity of ₹5,00,000 is separately examined u/s 10(10) — the question asks only for pension.

From your course material — Mr Ramesh Swain (allowances)

Mr Ramesh Swain is employed at Hyderabad at a basic salary of ₹25,000 p.m. and gets: DA ₹2,000 p.m.; education allowance ₹200 p.m. per child for three children; lunch allowance ₹1,000 p.m.; servant allowance ₹1,000 p.m. (he pays ₹1,200 p.m. to a servant); transport allowance ₹2,000 p.m.; hostel allowance for one child ₹500 p.m.; conveyance allowance ₹800 p.m. (he spends nothing); overtime ₹2,000 p.m.; officiating ₹2,000 p.m.; cash allowance ₹1,200 p.m.; entertainment ₹2,000 p.m.; medical ₹800 p.m.; CCA ₹600 p.m.; HRA ₹5,000 p.m. He has a family house at the place of his posting, but he lives in a rented house and pays rent of ₹7,000 p.m. Find his gross salary.

Figures checked against the original page image in your handout: HRA is ₹5,000 p.m. (the scanned text had misread it). He is treated as a non-Government employee, and DA as not forming part of retirement benefits (not stated). Solved under both regimes because the course plan teaches the old-regime exemptions for these allowances. Owning a family house at the place of posting does not bar the HRA exemption: sec 10(13A) only asks that he pays rent for the house he occupies (the “must not own a house there” condition belongs to sec 80GG, which is for people who get no HRA).

HRA working (old regime): salary = basic only = 25,000 × 12 = ₹3,00,000. Least of (a) HRA received 5,000 × 12 = ₹60,000; (b) rent − 10% of salary = 84,000 − 30,000 = ₹54,000; (c) 40% of salary (Hyderabad, non-metro for AY 2026-27) = ₹1,20,000 → exempt ₹54,000; taxable HRA = 60,000 − 54,000 = ₹6,000.

From your course material — Mr Rahul (RFA and gross salary)

Mr Rahul gets basic salary ₹40,000 p.m., medical allowance ₹1,000 p.m., transport allowance ₹1,200 p.m., bonus ₹24,000 and commission ₹60,000. He is provided rent-free unfurnished accommodation at Ludhiana (population 38 lakh as per 2011 census), FRV ₹24,000 p.m. He received leave encashment of ₹24,000 for the current PY during the year. House provided w.e.f. 1-7-2025. Salary due on the last day of every month. Compute the value of RFA and gross salary.

Period of occupation: 1-7-2025 to 31-3-2026 = 9 months. Bonus, commission and leave encashment are annual amounts; they are assumed to accrue evenly and are apportioned 9/12 to the period of occupation.

From your course material — employee at Coimbatore (taxable salary)

An employee (female, name illegible in the scan) working at Coimbatore (population 32 lakh, 2011): (a) salary ₹22,000 p.m.; (b) DA 100% of salary; (c) employer's contribution to RPF 14% of basic salary; (d) rent-free unfurnished accommodation, FRV ₹80,000 p.a.; expenses on maintenance of garden met by employer ₹3,000; (e) interest on PF balance at 13% p.a. — ₹3,900; (f) a car (1.4 litre) provided by the employer, all expenses borne by employer, used both for official and private purposes, used for only 11 months; (g) she paid professional tax ₹1,200; (h) fixed medical allowance ₹500 p.m. Find taxable salary.

Assumptions: non-Government employer; accommodation owned by employer; DA does not form part of retirement benefits (not stated); no chauffeur. She is a specified employee: monetary salary = 2,64,000 + 2,64,000 + 6,000 = 5,34,000, which after standard deduction (4,59,000) still exceeds ₹4,00,000.

  1. RPF: salary for RPF = basic ₹2,64,000. Employer's contribution 14% = ₹36,960; exempt 12% = ₹31,680; taxable excess = ₹5,280.
  2. Interest on RPF: taxable portion above 9.5% = 3,900 × 3.5 ÷ 13 = ₹1,050.
  3. RFA: population 32 lakh → 7.5%. Salary for RFA = basic 2,64,000 + medical allowance 6,000 = ₹2,70,000 (DA excluded; PF contribution excluded) → 7.5% = ₹20,250. FRV of ₹80,000 is irrelevant.
  4. Garden maintenance met by employer: perquisite for a specified employee = ₹3,000.
  5. Car (≤ 1.6 L, employer bears expenses, mixed use): ₹1,800 × 11 months = ₹19,800.

From your course material — Mrs Swati (salary under new regime)

Particulars for the year ending 31-3-2026: (1) net salary received ₹4,00,000 after deduction of TDS ₹6,000, own contribution to RPF ₹40,000 and rent of residential house provided ₹4,000; (2) profit bonus ₹24,000; (3) entertainment allowance ₹12,000 p.a.; (4) travelling allowance for official tour ₹6,000; (5) medical bills reimbursed (private hospital) ₹12,000; (6) house owned by the company at Patna (population 35 lakh), with a gardener paid by the company ₹500 p.m.; (7) electricity and water bills of the house paid by the company ₹1,200 p.m.; (8) a 1.2-litre car used partly for personal and partly for official purposes; (9) company contributed ₹56,400 to RPF; (10) interest-free loan ₹12,000 against salary, repayable in 6 monthly instalments from August 2025.

Assumptions: the ₹4,000 deducted from salary is rent recovered for the company house, so the accommodation is concessional (value minus recovery); car expenses borne by the company; utilities connection in the company's name. Mrs Swati is a specified employee (monetary salary 4,86,000 exceeds ₹4,00,000 even after standard deduction).

  1. Basic salary = 4,00,000 + 6,000 + 40,000 + 4,000 = ₹4,50,000 (deductions are applications of income).
  2. Accommodation: salary = basic 4,50,000 + profit bonus 24,000 + entertainment allowance 12,000 = ₹4,86,000; population 35 lakh → 7.5% = ₹36,450; less rent recovered ₹4,000 → ₹32,450.
  3. RPF: exempt 12% of 4,50,000 = ₹54,000; contribution ₹56,400 → taxable ₹2,400.
  4. Car: ≤ 1.6 L, mixed use → ₹1,800 × 12 = ₹21,600.
  5. Loan: aggregate ₹12,000 ≤ ₹20,000 → perquisite nil.
  6. Travelling allowance for official tour: exempt u/s 10(14)(i) (allowed in new regime).
  7. Medical reimbursement in a private hospital: fully taxable ₹12,000.

Under the old regime, HRA would be exempt (salary for HRA = basic 7,20,000 + DA forming part 1,80,000 = 9,00,000; least of HRA 1,44,000, rent − 10% of salary = 1,80,000 − 90,000 = 90,000, and 40%/50% of salary (3,60,000/4,50,000) → exempt ₹90,000 in any city), the education allowance ₹2,400 would be exempt, and helper/uniform allowances would be exempt to the extent spent on official duties. The fact that DA partly forms "salary" matters only for such old-regime computations and for perquisite valuation.

Additional salary problems (your second salary handout)

Five more problems from your "Problems on Salary" handouts (the extended set). The figures below were read from the original page images, so they do not depend on the OCR text. All are for PY 2025-26 / AY 2026-27 under the new regime u/s 115BAC, which is what each question asks for.

From your course material — Mr A of Ranchi (hired house, car > 1.6 L, free education)

Mr A, an employee of a Ranchi-based company [population 30 lakh (2011)]: (i) basic salary ₹32,000 p.m.; (ii) profit bonus ₹42,000; (iii) commission on turnover achieved by him ₹42,000; (iv) entertainment allowance ₹2,000 p.m.; (v) club facility ₹6,000; (vi) transport allowance ₹1,800 p.m.; (vii) free use of a car of more than 1.6 litre for personal and official purposes, expenses met by the employer; (viii) rent-free house hired by the employer at ₹6,000 p.m.; (ix) free education for three children, bills in the employer's name, ₹22,500; (x) gas, water and electricity bills in the employee's name paid by the employer ₹16,800. Compute income under the head Salaries for AY 2026-27 under the new regime.

Assumptions: non-Government employer; car used for the whole year with no chauffeur; club facility used for personal purposes; the education is at an outside school (not an institution run by the employer), so the ₹1,000 p.m. per child relief does not apply.

Specified employee test: monetary salary = basic 3,84,000 + bonus 42,000 + commission 42,000 + entertainment allowance 24,000 + transport allowance 21,600 = ₹5,13,600; after the standard deduction it is ₹4,38,600, above ₹4,00,000. So he is a specified employee and the car and education perquisites are taxable.

Working note 1 — RFA (house hired by employer): value = lease rent paid by the employer or 10% of salary, whichever is lower. City population is irrelevant for a hired house.

From your course material — Mr Imran (no option exercised: default new regime)

Salary ₹40,000 p.m. (due on the last day of the month) ₹4,80,000; bonus equal to one month's salary ₹48,000; entertainment allowance ₹5,000 p.m. ₹60,000; free gas and water supply ₹5,000. Rent-free accommodation in Delhi owned by the employer, FRV ₹10,000 p.m. (population above 90 lakh). A car of 1.8 litre capacity (printed as "18 cubic capacity") used for private and official purposes. A domestic servant engaged by him at ₹1,000 p.m. whose salary is paid by the employer. Free lunch in office during lunch break valued at ₹100 per day for 250 days. Stay of 10 days at the employer's holiday home at Shimla, cost to employer ₹10,000. Air tickets for himself and his wife to Bombay for a family function, ₹24,000, bought on the employer's credit card. Employer and employee each contribute 14% of salary to RPF. Compute his salary income assuming he does not exercise any option.

Assumptions: non-Government employer; car running expenses borne by the employer, no chauffeur; gas and water connection in the employer's name. "Does not exercise any option" means he stays in the default regime u/s 115BAC (new regime).

Specified employee: monetary salary 4,80,000 + 48,000 + 60,000 = ₹5,88,000; after standard deduction ₹5,13,000 > ₹4,00,000. Specified.

WN 1 — RFA: employer-owned, population above 40 lakh → 10% of salary. Salary = 4,80,000 + 48,000 + 60,000 = ₹5,88,000 → ₹58,800. FRV is irrelevant.

WN 2 — RPF: salary for RPF = basic ₹4,80,000. Employer's 14% = ₹67,200; exempt 12% = ₹57,600; taxable ₹9,600. His own 14% gives no deduction in the new regime.

WN 3 — free lunch: meals at the office during working hours are exempt up to ₹50 per meal (this relief is lost in the new regime only for paid vouchers). Taxable = (100 − 50) × 250 = ₹12,500.

Why the air tickets are fully taxable: the journey was for a family function, not official work, and was paid through the employer's credit card (Rule 3(7)(v)). LTC exemption u/s 10(5) cannot be claimed: it needs a journey on leave under an LTC scheme, and it is not available in the new regime anyway.

From your course material — Mr Jadav (loan, medical, transfer, hotel stay)

(i) Basic pay ₹15,000 p.m. (due on last day of month); (ii) DA 60% of salary [50% of DA forms part of salary from 1-1-2026]; (iii) bonus: basic for one month; (iv) commission ₹66,000; (v) leave encashment ₹24,000 for the current year's leave; (vi) travelling allowance ₹18,000 (actual expenditure ₹14,400); (vii) medical bills of ₹50,000 reimbursed: notified hospital for a notified disease ₹12,000, private nursing home ₹38,000; (viii) personal mobile telephone bill paid by employer ₹15,000; (ix) cook employed by him at ₹1,000 p.m., salary reimbursed by employer; (x) on 1-12-2025, a loan of ₹6,00,000 from the employer for a car at 7.25% p.a., repayment of ₹5,000 p.m. to start 4 months after the loan; SBI rate on 1-4-2025 assumed 9.25%; (xi) he and the employer each contribute ₹3,000 p.m. to RPF; (xii) interest credited to RPF at 10% ₹20,000; (xiii) LTC ₹20,000 received but he did not travel; (xiv) car of 1.8 L used for employment purposes only; (xv) rent-free house hired by the employer at ₹10,000 p.m. He is posted at Nagpur (population 30 lakh); on 10 March he was transferred to Bhopal while his family stayed at Nagpur; he joined at Bhopal on 12 March and stayed in a hotel for 10 days at ₹1,500 per day paid by the employer. Compute his salary income for AY 2026-27 under the new regime.

Assumptions: non-Government employer; the Nagpur house (family still there) is valued for the whole year; the car's official use is supported by records. Specified-employee status does not matter here: every perquisite that is taxed below is taxable for all employees (his monetary salary after standard deduction is ₹3,41,600, so he is not a specified employee, and the only specified-employee perquisite, the car, is nil anyway).

WN 1 — basic figures: basic 15,000 × 12 = ₹1,80,000; DA 60% = 9,000 p.m. × 12 = ₹1,08,000; DA forming part of salary = 50% × 9,000 × 3 months (Jan–Mar 2026) = ₹13,500; bonus ₹15,000.

WN 2 — RPF: salary for RPF = basic 1,80,000 + DA forming part 13,500 = ₹1,93,500; 12% = ₹23,220. Employer's contribution 3,000 × 12 = ₹36,000 → taxable 36,000 − 23,220 = ₹12,780. Interest: taxable part above 9.5% = 20,000 × 0.5 ÷ 10 = ₹1,000.

WN 3 — car loan: repayment starts 4 months after 1-12-2025, i.e. from April 2026, so the maximum outstanding monthly balance is ₹6,00,000 for December–March (4 months). Interest at SBI rate = 6,00,000 × 9.25% × 4/12 = ₹18,500; interest charged = 6,00,000 × 7.25% × 4/12 = ₹14,500; perquisite = ₹4,000 (taxable for all; loan above ₹20,000 and not for medical treatment).

WN 4 — RFA at Nagpur (hired house): lower of lease rent 10,000 × 12 = ₹1,20,000 and 10% of salary.

Less: standard deduction u/s 16(ia)75,000
Less: professional tax ₹3,000 u/s 16(iii) (not allowed in new regime)Nil
Taxable salary income4,81,576

The fair rent of ₹8,000 p.m. is irrelevant for employer-owned accommodation. The wife's travel is a private benefit even though Rahul himself was on an official tour.

From your course material — Mr Sarangi, Cuttack (car used only privately, loans)

Mr Sarangi, employee of a public limited company at Cuttack, PY 2025-26: (i) basic ₹30,000 p.m. = ₹3,60,000; (ii) DA as per terms of employment ₹3,000 p.m. = ₹36,000; (iii) bonus equal to one month's salary ₹33,000; (iv) commission ₹60,000; (v) advance salary ₹66,000; (vi) employee's RPF contribution ₹48,000; (vii) employer's RPF contribution ₹48,000; (viii) special allowance ₹2,000 p.m. = ₹24,000; (ix) HRA ₹10,000 p.m. = ₹1,20,000; (x) rent paid ₹12,000 p.m. = ₹1,44,000; (xi) entertainment allowance ₹3,000 p.m. = ₹36,000 (spent wholly on official duties); (xii) a Honda City car of 1,600 cc with chauffeur, used for personal purposes; employer's running, maintenance and driver cost ₹1,20,000; cost of car ₹7,50,000; (xiii) interest credited to RPF at 12% ₹30,000; (xiv) free club facility costing ₹24,000 and free lunch for 300 days at ₹150 per day; (xv) interest-free loan ₹18,000 for a motor cycle, and in November 2025 an interest-free loan ₹50,000 for his father's treatment (disease specified in Rule 3A). Find his salary income for AY 2026-27 under the new regime (hand-corrected from "old").

Assumptions: car provided for the whole year; lunch served at the office during working hours; DA "as per terms" forms part of salary for retirement benefits.

WN 1 — RPF: salary for RPF = basic + DA = ₹3,96,000; 12% = ₹47,520; taxable = 48,000 − 47,520 = ₹480. Interest above 9.5%: 30,000 × 2.5 ÷ 12 = ₹6,250.

WN 2 — car used only for private purposes: actual running and maintenance including chauffeur ₹1,20,000 + 10% of cost (10% × 7,50,000) ₹75,000 = ₹1,95,000. (The ₹1,800/₹2,400 flat rates apply only to mixed use.)

WN 3 — loans: the medical loan for a Rule 3A disease is exempt; the other loan of ₹18,000 does not exceed ₹20,000 → perquisite nil.

WN 4 — lunch: (150 − 50) × 300 = ₹30,000 taxable.

Self-check (second salary handout)
1. A house hired by the employer at ₹6,000 p.m.; salary for RFA ₹5,13,600. Value?

Lower of ₹72,000 and 10% of 5,13,600 = ₹51,360.

2. Free lunch at office at ₹120 per meal for 200 days, new regime. Taxable?

(120 − 50) × 200 = ₹14,000. The ₹50 relief is lost in the new regime only for paid vouchers.

3. Company car of 1.6 L with chauffeur used only privately; running cost incl. driver ₹90,000; car cost ₹6,00,000. Perquisite?

90,000 + 10% × 6,00,000 = ₹1,50,000.

4. Hotel stay on transfer for 12 days paid by employer. Perquisite?

Nil: hotel accommodation on transfer for up to 15 days in aggregate is not a perquisite.

Extra worked example — Government employee, old regime deductions u/s 16
  1. Ms Meera, a State Government officer: basic ₹30,000 p.m.; entertainment allowance ₹1,500 p.m.; professional tax paid ₹2,500. Old regime opted.
  2. Gross salary = 3,60,000 + 18,000 = ₹3,78,000.
  3. 16(ii): least of ₹5,000, 20% of basic (₹72,000), actual (₹18,000) = ₹5,000.
  4. Deductions = standard 50,000 + 5,000 + 2,500 = ₹57,500. Income under Salaries = ₹3,20,500. (New regime: 3,78,000 − 75,000 = ₹3,03,000.)

2.9 Unit II exam kit

Likely questions: Define salary and explain its basis of charge (5) · Distinguish allowance and perquisite (5) · Explain the treatment of gratuity/commuted pension with example (10) · Explain types of PF and their tax treatment (5/10) · RFA valuation (5) · Specified employee and perquisites taxable only for them (5) · Full salary computation (15, case).

Model answer (10 marks) — "Explain the tax treatment of different provident funds"

Introduction. A provident fund is a retirement savings scheme to which the employee (and usually the employer) contributes a part of salary every month, and on which interest accrues. The Income-tax Act recognises four types, and the treatment depends on the degree of statutory control over the fund.

1. Statutory PF (Provident Funds Act, 1925; Government, railways, universities): employer's contribution and interest are exempt; lump-sum payment is exempt u/s 10(11); employee's contribution qualifies for 80C (old regime).

2. Recognised PF (EPF & MP Act, 1952 or recognised by the Commissioner): employer's contribution exempt up to 12% of salary, excess taxable; interest exempt up to 9.5%, excess taxable; lump sum exempt u/s 10(12) after 5 years' continuous service.

3. Unrecognised PF: nothing is taxed annually; on payment, employer's contribution and interest thereon are taxed as salary, and interest on the employee's contribution as other sources; employee's contribution is not taxed and got no 80C.

4. Public PF: voluntary scheme for all; contribution under 80C (old regime); interest and maturity exempt.

Common limits. Interest on employee contributions above ₹2.5 lakh a year is taxable; employer contributions to RPF + NPS + superannuation above ₹7.5 lakh are taxable u/s 17(2)(vii).

Example. Basic + DA ₹6,00,000; employer contributes 14% = 84,000 → exempt 72,000, taxable 12,000.

Conclusion. RPF and SPF enjoy "EEE" status within limits; URPF is taxed on withdrawal; PPF is fully exempt. Under the new regime the exemptions on contributions/interest continue but the 80C deduction on employee contributions is lost.

Model answer (5 marks) — "Distinguish between allowance and perquisite"
BasisAllowancePerquisite
FormFixed cash paymentBenefit in kind (or employer paying employee's obligation)
SectionSec 17(1), exemptions u/s 10(13A), 10(14)Sec 17(2), valuation under Rule 3
ValuationAmount receivedPrescribed rules (e.g., RFA 5/7.5/10% of salary; car ₹1,800/2,400 p.m.)
Specified-employee ruleNot relevantSome perquisites taxable only for specified employees
ExampleHRA, conveyance allowanceRent-free house, company car, interest-free loan

Conclusion: both are part of gross salary; allowances are taxed on receipt less exemption, perquisites on their valued amount.

Model answer (5 marks) — "Who is a specified employee? Which perquisites are taxable only for them?"

Meaning (sec 17(2)(iii)). (a) Director-employee; (b) employee holding 20% or more voting power in the employer company; (c) employee whose salary income excluding non-monetary perquisites exceeds ₹4,00,000 (AY 2026-27; earlier ₹50,000).

Perquisites taxable only for them: motor car provided by the employer for private use; domestic servants engaged by the employer; gas, electricity and water in the employer's name; free/concessional education in employer's institution above ₹1,000 p.m. per child; free transport by a transport undertaking.

Example: Mrs Swati (salary ₹4,86,000) is a specified employee, so her car (₹21,600), gardener and utilities are taxable.

Conclusion: the rule protects low-paid employees from being taxed on minor benefits.

Unit III — Income from House Property and Profits & Gains of Business or Profession

10 hours; CO3 carries weight in CIA-1, CIA-2 (mid-sem case study) and ESE. Two very scoring computations: GAV→NAV→income from HP, and adjusting a P&L account to taxable business income.

3.1 House property — basis of charge (sec 22) and scope

Intuition

The law taxes the earning capacity of a building you own, not just the rent you receive. That is why an empty but let-able second house can be taxed on a notional ("deemed") rent, and why the law gives a flat 30% for repairs instead of looking at your actual repair bills.

Definition — sec 22

The annual value of property consisting of any buildings or lands appurtenant thereto of which the assessee is the owner, other than such portions as he occupies for the purposes of any business or profession carried on by him the profits of which are chargeable to tax, is chargeable under the head "Income from house property".

Three conditions

  1. Building or land appurtenant (compound, garage, garden attached). Vacant land let out → Other sources.
  2. Assessee is the owner — legal owner or deemed owner (sec 27). A tenant who sublets is not the owner: sub-letting income → Other sources.
  3. Not used for own business/profession — property used for own business gets no notional rent (depreciation instead).

Deemed owners — sec 27

  • Individual who transfers a house to spouse (not in connection with an agreement to live apart) or to a minor child (other than a married daughter) without adequate consideration.
  • Holder of an impartible estate.
  • Member of a co-operative society/company/AOP to whom a building is allotted or leased under a house-building scheme.
  • Person in possession under part-performance of a contract (sec 53A, Transfer of Property Act).
  • Person acquiring rights by lease for 12 years or more (not a month-to-month lease).

Special points

  • Property income exempt: farm house (agricultural income), one palace of an ex-ruler, property of a local authority, political party, registered charitable trust, and property used for own business.
  • Composite rent (building + other services): if services (lift, security) are separable, building portion → HP, services → PGBP/Other sources; if letting of building with plant/machinery/furniture is inseparable → PGBP or Other sources (sec 56(2)(iii)).
  • Property held as stock-in-trade and not let out: annual value nil for 2 years from the end of the FY in which the completion certificate is obtained (sec 23(5)).
  • Co-owners (sec 26): each co-owner is assessed on his share; each gets the self-occupied benefits separately.
  • Income from HP is taxable in the hands of the owner even if he is not the recipient; the residential status rules of sec 5 apply to foreign property.

3.2 Gross annual value (GAV) and net annual value (NAV) — let-out property (sec 23(1))

Key terms
  • Municipal value (MV): value fixed by the municipal authority for levying property tax.
  • Fair rent / fair rental value (FRV): rent a similar property in the same locality would fetch.
  • Standard rent (SR): maximum rent a landlord can legally charge under the Rent Control Act (only where the Act applies).
  • Expected rent (ER) = higher of MV and FRV, but not exceeding SR.
  • Actual rent (AR): rent received or receivable for the period let out, excluding unrealised rent (Rule 4 conditions).
Formula — step-by-step (ICAI method used in your notes)

Step 1: ER = min{ max(MV, FRV), SR }
Step 2: AR = rent received/receivable for the let-out period − unrealised rent
Step 3: If AR ≥ ER → GAV = AR. If AR < ER because of vacancy → GAV = AR. If AR < ER for other reasons → GAV = ER.
Step 4: NAV = GAV − municipal taxes paid by the owner during the PY (paid basis; arrears paid now are allowed; taxes paid by tenant are not deducted).
Step 5: Income = NAV − 30% of NAV [24(a)] − interest on borrowed capital [24(b)].

Unrealised rent — Rule 4 conditions

Unrealised rent is excluded from AR only if: (a) the tenancy is bona fide; (b) the defaulting tenant has vacated, or steps have been taken to compel him to vacate; (c) the defaulting tenant is not in occupation of any other property of the assessee; (d) the assessee has taken all reasonable steps to institute legal proceedings for recovery or satisfies the AO that such proceedings would be useless.

Worked example — four GAV cases (no vacancy)
CaseMVFRVSRARERGAVReason
A1,00,0001,20,0001,10,0001,32,0001,10,0001,32,000AR > ER
B1,00,0001,20,0001,50,0001,32,0001,20,0001,32,000AR > ER
C1,50,0001,20,0001,40,0001,20,0001,40,0001,40,000ER > AR (not vacancy)
D1,00,0001,20,0001,10,00090,0001,10,0001,10,000ER > AR (not vacancy)
Self-check
1. Municipal tax ₹12,000 due for 2025-26 but paid in April 2026. Deductible in AY 2026-27?

No. Only taxes paid during the PY are deductible; it will be deductible in AY 2027-28.

2. Tenant pays the municipal tax. Deduct it?

No — only taxes borne and paid by the owner are deductible.

3.3 Self-occupied property (SOP) and deemed let-out property — sec 23(2), 23(4)

Rule

The annual value of up to two houses occupied by the owner for his own residence (or which cannot be occupied by him for any reason — relaxed by the Finance Act, 2025) is taken as nil, provided the house is not in fact let out during any part of the year and no other benefit is derived from it. If the owner has more than two such houses, he chooses any two as SOP; the others are deemed to be let out and their GAV = ER.

ItemOld regimeNew regime (115BAC)
Annual value of up to 2 SOPsNilNil
24(a) 30%Nil (NAV is nil)Nil
24(b) interest on SOP — loan for acquisition/construction taken on/after 1-4-1999 and completed within 5 years from the end of FY of borrowingUp to ₹2,00,000 (aggregate for both SOPs, incl. pre-construction instalment)Not allowed
24(b) interest on SOP — loan for repairs/renewal, or loan before 1-4-1999, or construction beyond 5 yearsUp to ₹30,000Not allowed
24(b) interest on let-out / deemed let-out propertyFull amount (no ceiling)Full amount
Set-off of HP loss against other headsUp to ₹2,00,000 (sec 71(3A)); balance carried forward 8 yearsNot allowed
Indian example

Home-loan borrowers from banks such as SBI or HDFC Bank commonly receive an "interest certificate" each April. Under the old regime, the SOP interest in that certificate (up to ₹2 lakh) reduces taxable income; under the new regime it gives no benefit unless the house is let out — a key factor in the regime choice.

3.4 Deductions from NAV — sec 24

  1. Standard deduction u/s 24(a): 30% of NAV, irrespective of actual repair/insurance expenditure. Nothing else (insurance, repairs, collection charges, land revenue) is separately deductible.
  2. Interest on borrowed capital u/s 24(b): interest on loans for purchase, construction, repair, renewal or reconstruction, on accrual basis (whether paid or not). Interest on a fresh loan taken to repay the original loan is allowed; interest on unpaid interest and brokerage/commission for arranging the loan are not.
  3. Pre-construction interest: interest for the period from the date of borrowing to 31 March immediately before the year in which construction/acquisition is completed; deducted in 5 equal instalments starting from the year of completion.
Formula

Pre-construction period = date of borrowing → 31 March preceding the year of completion.
Annual instalment = Total pre-construction interest ÷ 5 (years 1 to 5 from the year of completion).
24(b) for the year = current-year interest + one-fifth of pre-construction interest (subject to ₹2 lakh for SOP in old regime; nil for SOP in new regime).

Trap

Principal repayment is not a sec 24 deduction; it qualifies u/s 80C only in the old regime. Interest paid outside India without TDS is not deductible.

3.5 Arrears of rent and recovery of unrealised rent — sec 25A

Rule

Arrears of rent received, and unrealised rent later recovered, are taxable in the year of receipt under the head HP, whether or not the assessee is the owner in that year; 30% of the amount is deducted; no other deduction.

Worked example
  1. In PY 2025-26 Mr Nair receives ₹60,000 of rent arrears for 2022-23 (after the property was sold) and recovers ₹20,000 of unrealised rent allowed as a deduction in 2023-24.
  2. Arrears: 60,000 − 30% = ₹42,000. Recovery: 20,000 − 30% = ₹14,000.
  3. Income from HP u/s 25A = ₹56,000.

3.6 House property — worked problems (let-out, SOP, deemed let-out, decision making)

Problem 1 — let-out property with vacancy, unrealised rent and pre-construction interest

Mr Vivek owns a house in Mysuru: MV ₹3,00,000; FRV ₹3,60,000; SR ₹3,30,000. Let out at ₹30,000 p.m.; vacant for 2 months; rent for 1 month could not be realised (Rule 4 conditions satisfied). Municipal tax paid by him ₹20,000. Loan of ₹5,00,000 at 12% taken on 1-6-2021 for construction; construction completed on 15-8-2024; no repayment yet. Compute income from HP for AY 2026-27.

  1. ER = higher of MV 3,00,000 and FRV 3,60,000, capped at SR 3,30,000 → ₹3,30,000.
  2. AR for 10 months = 30,000 × 10 = 3,00,000; less unrealised rent 30,000 → ₹2,70,000.
  3. Had the house not been vacant, annual rent would be 3,60,000 ≥ ER, so AR is below ER because of vacancy → GAV = AR = ₹2,70,000.
  4. Pre-construction period: 1-6-2021 to 31-3-2024 = 34 months; interest = 5,00,000 × 12% × 34/12 = ₹1,70,000; instalment = 1,70,000 ÷ 5 = ₹34,000 (2024-25 to 2028-29).
  5. Current-year interest = 5,00,000 × 12% = ₹60,000.
Computation of income from house property₹
Gross annual value2,70,000
Less: municipal taxes paid20,000
Net annual value2,50,000
Less: 24(a) 30% of NAV75,000
Less: 24(b) interest: current 60,000 + pre-construction 34,00094,000
Income from house property81,000

Same answer under both regimes (the property is let out).

Problem 2 — self-occupied property: old vs new regime

Ms Anita occupies her own flat in Bengaluru. Interest on the housing loan (taken in 2021 for purchase) for the PY = ₹2,40,000; principal repaid ₹1,20,000; municipal tax paid ₹8,000.

ParticularsOld regime ₹New regime ₹
Annual value u/s 23(2)NilNil
Municipal tax (not deductible for SOP)NilNil
24(b) interest: 2,40,000 limited to 2,00,000 (old); not allowed (new)(2,00,000)Nil
Income / (loss) from HP(2,00,000)Nil

Old regime: the loss of ₹2,00,000 is set off against salary (limit ₹2 lakh); principal ₹1,20,000 qualifies u/s 80C. New regime: no benefit.

Problem 3 — three houses, all self-occupied (tax-planning decision)

Mr Gopal has three houses used for his own residence. ER: House X ₹3,00,000, House Y ₹1,80,000, House Z ₹2,50,000. Municipal tax paid on Z ₹10,000; interest on loan for Z ₹50,000; no loans on X and Y. Which house should be deemed let out?

  1. Income if deemed let-out: X = 3,00,000 − 30% = ₹2,10,000; Y = 1,80,000 − 30% = ₹1,26,000; Z = (2,50,000 − 10,000) = 2,40,000 − 72,000 − 50,000 = ₹1,18,000.
  2. Choose as deemed let-out the house with the lowest resulting income → House Z; X and Y are SOP with nil annual value.
  3. Income from HP = ₹1,18,000 (both regimes: interest on a deemed let-out house is allowed under the new regime too).
Self-check
1. A house is self-occupied for 8 months and let out for 4 months. Can annual value be nil?

No. Sec 23(2) applies only if the house is not let out during any part of the year; compute GAV as a let-out property (ER vs AR for the let period).

2. Loan taken 1-10-2023, house completed 20-7-2025. Pre-construction period?

1-10-2023 to 31-3-2025 (18 months); 1/5 deductible from PY 2025-26 to 2029-30.

3.7 Profits and gains of business or profession — meaning and incomes chargeable (sec 28)

Definitions

Business [sec 2(13)] includes any trade, commerce or manufacture or any adventure or concern in the nature of trade, commerce or manufacture. Profession [sec 2(36)] includes vocation; it requires intellectual skill based on special learning (doctor, lawyer, CA, architect). Vocation is a natural ability or calling (singer, dancer, writer).

Incomes chargeable u/s 28

  1. 28(i): profits and gains of any business or profession carried on at any time during the PY.
  2. 28(ii): compensation or other payment for termination or modification of management contracts, agency, or any business contract.
  3. 28(iii): income of a trade/professional association from specific services to members.
  4. 28(iiia)–(iiie): export incentives (profit on sale of import licences, cash assistance, duty drawback, profit on transfer of DEPB/DFRC).
  5. 28(iv): value of any benefit or perquisite arising from business, whether convertible into money or not (e.g., a car received free from a supplier).
  6. 28(v): interest, salary, bonus, commission or remuneration received by a partner from the firm (to the extent allowed to the firm).
  7. 28(va): sum received for a non-compete agreement or for not sharing know-how/patent.
  8. 28(vi): sum received under a Keyman insurance policy.
  9. 28(via): FMV of inventory converted into a capital asset.
  10. 28(vii): sum received on transfer of an asset whose whole cost was allowed u/s 35AD.

General principles

  • Income of all businesses of the assessee is aggregated; speculation business is kept separate.
  • Tax is on real profit computed per the method of accounting regularly employed (cash or mercantile) (sec 145), after adjustments required by the Act.
  • Business must be carried on during the PY (except certain receipts after discontinuance: sec 41, 176).
  • Illegal business profits are taxable; losses from illegal business are generally not allowed where the activity itself is an offence.
  • Anticipated losses are not allowed (except valuation of stock at cost or market price, whichever lower); anticipated profits are not taxed.
  • Recovery of a loss or expense earlier allowed (e.g., bad debts recovered) is taxable u/s 41.
BasisBusinessProfession
NatureTrade, commerce, manufactureIntellectual/manual skill from special learning
QualificationNone requiredUsually a recognised qualification and membership of a body (ICAI, Bar Council, NMC)
Presumptive scheme44AD (8%/6%), 44AE (goods carriages)44ADA (50%)
ExampleKirana store, textile millDoctor, lawyer, CA, architect

Depreciation — block of assets (common rates, Appendix I)

BlockRate
Residential building (other than hotels/boarding houses)5%
Building (general, office, factory)10%
Furniture and fittings10%
Plant and machinery (general), motor cars (not used in hiring business)15%
Computers including software40%
Intangible assets (patents, copyrights, know-how, trademarks, licences, franchises)25%
Formula — WDV of a block

WDV at the end of the year (before depreciation) = Opening WDV + actual cost of assets acquired during the year − moneys receivable on assets sold/discarded/destroyed (including scrap value), but not below nil.
Depreciation = rate × (opening WDV + additions used ≥ 180 days − sale proceeds) + 50% of rate × additions used < 180 days.
If sale proceeds exceed (opening WDV + additions), the excess is a short-term capital gain u/s 50 and no depreciation is allowed, even if some assets remain in the block. If all assets of the block are sold and the proceeds are lower than (opening WDV + additions), the shortfall is a short-term capital loss and no depreciation is allowed.

Worked example 1 — depreciation on a 15% block
  1. Opening WDV 1-4-2025: ₹5,00,000. Machine bought 10-6-2025 for ₹3,00,000 (used > 180 days). Machine bought 5-12-2025 for ₹1,00,000 (used < 180 days). Old machine sold for ₹1,50,000.
  2. WDV before depreciation = 5,00,000 + 3,00,000 + 1,00,000 − 1,50,000 = ₹7,50,000.
  3. Full-rate portion = 5,00,000 + 3,00,000 − 1,50,000 = 6,50,000 × 15% = ₹97,500; half-rate portion = 1,00,000 × 7.5% = ₹7,500.
  4. Depreciation = ₹1,05,000; closing WDV = ₹6,45,000.
Worked example 2 — block ceases to exist
  1. Same block, but all machines are sold during the year for ₹10,00,000.
  2. Opening WDV + additions = 5,00,000 + 3,00,000 + 1,00,000 = ₹9,00,000.
  3. Short-term capital gain u/s 50 = 10,00,000 − 9,00,000 = ₹1,00,000; no depreciation.

35(1)(iia)Contribution to an approved Indian company for scientific research100%Not allowed
35(1)(iii)Contribution to an approved association/university for research in social science or statistical research100%Not allowed
35(1)(iv) read with 35(2)Capital expenditure (other than on land) on scientific research related to the business; pre-commencement capital expenditure of 3 years allowed in the year of commencement100% in the year incurred; no depreciation on that assetAllowed
35(2AB)In-house R&D facility approved by DSIR (companies in manufacturing)100% (weighted deduction ended after AY 2020-21)Allowed

If a scientific-research asset is later used for business: no depreciation (cost already allowed). If sold without use for business: sale price up to the deduction allowed is business income (sec 41(3)); the excess is capital gain. Unabsorbed capital SR expenditure is carried forward like unabsorbed depreciation.

Worked example 3 — sec 35
  1. Ms Kavya starts manufacturing on 1-6-2025. Research staff salaries in the 3 prior years (certified) ₹60,000; research materials ₹40,000; in 2025-26 revenue SR expenses ₹1,50,000; SR laboratory building ₹5,00,000 and land for it ₹3,00,000.
  2. 35(1)(i): 1,50,000 + pre-commencement (60,000 + 40,000) = ₹2,50,000.
  3. 35(1)(iv): building ₹5,00,000 (land excluded).
  4. Total deduction u/s 35 for AY 2026-27 = ₹7,50,000 (allowed in both regimes).

3.9 Disallowed expenses — sections 40, 40A, 43B and personal items

SectionDisallowance
40(a)(i)Interest, royalty, fees for technical services or other sums payable to a non-resident without deducting/depositing TDS — 100% disallowed (allowed in the year TDS is paid)
40(a)(ia)Sums payable to a resident on which TDS is not deducted or not paid by the due date of return — 30% disallowed (allowed in the year of payment)
40(a)(ii)Income tax (and interest thereon), wealth tax — disallowed
40(b)Interest to partners above 12% simple p.a., and remuneration above the limits, disallowed in the firm's hands
40A(2)Excessive or unreasonable payments to relatives or related persons (having regard to FMV of goods/services) — excess disallowed
40A(3)Expenditure paid otherwise than by account-payee cheque/draft/ECS/prescribed electronic mode, where payment to a person in a day exceeds ₹10,000 (₹35,000 for plying, hiring or leasing goods carriages) — 100% disallowed. Rule 6DD exceptions: payments to banks/Government, agriculturists for produce, in villages without banks, etc.
40A(3A)Expenditure allowed earlier on accrual, later paid in cash above the limit — deemed income of the year of payment
40A(7)Provision for gratuity (except contribution to an approved gratuity fund or actual payment)
40A(9)Contributions to non-statutory funds (e.g., unrecognised PF)
43BDeductible only on actual payment (on or before the due date of return u/s 139(1) for the year): taxes, duties, cess, fees; employer's contribution to PF/superannuation/gratuity/welfare funds; bonus/commission to employees; interest on loans from public financial institutions, banks, co-operative banks, NBFCs; leave encashment; payments to Indian Railways. 43B(h) (from AY 2024-25): sums payable to micro and small enterprises beyond the time limit of sec 15 of the MSMED Act are allowed only in the year of actual payment.
37 / generalPersonal expenses (proprietor's salary, drawings, household expenses, life insurance of proprietor); capital expenditure; penalties/fines for infraction of law; CSR spending; donations (allowed only u/s 80G, old regime); provisions for unascertained liabilities; notional interest on own capital
Indian example

Sec 43B(h), introduced by the Finance Act, 2023 to protect MSMEs, made many large buyers settle supplier invoices within 45 days before 31 March 2024; delayed payments to registered micro/small suppliers are now deductible only when paid.

Traps
  • Income tax, interest on capital to the proprietor, and drawings are never deductible for a sole proprietor.
  • Depreciation debited in books is replaced by depreciation as per sec 32.
  • Income credited to P&L but taxable under another head (interest on securities, dividends, rent from HP, capital gains) must be deducted from business income and taxed under its own head.
  • Expenses of an exempt income (sec 14A) are not allowed.

3.10 Presumptive taxation — sec 44AD, 44ADA, 44AE

Worked example — presumptive incomes
  1. 44AD: turnover ₹1.80 crore, of which ₹1.62 crore by bank/UPI and ₹18 lakh cash (10% cash, so limit is ₹2 crore — eligible). Deemed profit = 1,62,00,000 × 6% + 18,00,000 × 8% = 9,72,000 + 1,44,000 = ₹11,16,000.
  2. 44ADA: architect with gross receipts ₹60 lakh, cash below 5% (limit ₹75 lakh). Deemed profit = 50% = ₹30,00,000.
  3. 44AE: 4 light goods vehicles all year + 2 heavy vehicles of 16 tonnes each owned for 10 months. Income = 4 × 7,500 × 12 + 2 × 1,000 × 16 × 10 = 3,60,000 + 3,20,000 = ₹6,80,000.
Indian example

Your CIA-1 interviewee runs a coal-transport business with about 40 trucks — far more than 10 goods carriages, so sec 44AE is not available; such a business must maintain books (sec 44AA) and may need a tax audit (sec 44AB).

Problem — Dr Nisha, medical practitioner (normal computation vs 44ADA)

Receipts: consultation fees ₹24,00,000 (all digital), visiting fees ₹3,00,000. Expenses: clinic rent ₹3,60,000; salary to nurse and receptionist ₹4,80,000; medicines consumed ₹2,40,000; electricity ₹60,000; depreciation on equipment (as per sec 32) ₹90,000; purchase of a new car for personal use ₹8,00,000 (capital, personal); her own life insurance premium ₹50,000.

  1. Normal computation: receipts 27,00,000 − (3,60,000 + 4,80,000 + 2,40,000 + 60,000 + 90,000) = 27,00,000 − 12,30,000 = ₹14,70,000. The car and life insurance are personal and not deductible.
  2. Sec 44ADA (gross receipts ₹27 lakh ≤ ₹75 lakh with digital receipts): 50% × 27,00,000 = ₹13,50,000.
  3. 44ADA gives lower income (₹13,50,000) and needs no books — a legitimate tax-planning choice.

3.12 Unit III exam kit

Likely questions: Explain annual value and its determination (5/10) · Deductions u/s 24 (5) · Deemed owner (5) · Unrealised rent and arrears (5) · Incomes chargeable u/s 28 (5) · Expenses expressly disallowed (5/10) · Sec 43B and 40A(3) (5) · Scientific research u/s 35 (5) · Presumptive taxation (5) · P&L computation (10/15).

Model answer (10 marks) — "How is the annual value of a let-out house property determined?"

Introduction. Sec 22 charges the annual value of a building owned by the assessee and not used for his business. Sec 23 lays down how annual value is determined.

Step 1 — Expected rent. Higher of municipal value and fair rent, limited to standard rent under the Rent Control Act.

Step 2 — Actual rent. Rent received or receivable for the let-out period, excluding unrealised rent if the four Rule 4 conditions are met (bona fide tenancy, tenant vacated or steps taken, not occupying other property, legal steps taken).

Step 3 — GAV. Higher of ER and AR; but if AR is lower than ER owing to vacancy, AR is the GAV.

Step 4 — NAV. GAV less municipal taxes paid by the owner during the PY.

Special cases. Up to two self-occupied houses: annual value nil; additional houses deemed let out at ER; property held as stock-in-trade: nil for 2 years after completion.

Example. MV 3,00,000, FRV 3,60,000, SR 3,30,000; rent 30,000 p.m. for 10 months, one month unrealised → ER 3,30,000, AR 2,70,000 (lower due to vacancy) → GAV 2,70,000; municipal tax 20,000 → NAV 2,50,000.

Conclusion. From NAV, 30% standard deduction and interest u/s 24(b) are allowed to arrive at income from house property.

Model answer (5 marks) — "Explain sec 43B"

Purpose. To stop businesses from claiming statutory dues and employee benefits on accrual without paying them.

Items covered. Taxes, duties, cess, fees; employer contributions to PF/superannuation/gratuity funds; bonus/commission to employees; interest on loans from banks, PFIs, co-operative banks and NBFCs; leave encashment; railway dues; payments to micro and small enterprises beyond the MSMED Act time limit (43B(h)).

Rule. Deductible in the year of actual payment; but if paid on or before the due date of the return for the year of accrual, allowed in that year (except 43B(h), allowed only in the year of payment).

Example. Bonus of ₹40,000 for 2025-26 of which ₹10,000 is paid after the due date → ₹10,000 disallowed in AY 2026-27, allowed in the year of payment.

Unit IV — Capital Gains and Income from Other Sources

10 hours; CO4. Capital gains changed sharply with the Finance (No. 2) Act, 2024 (transfers on or after 23 July 2024). Every PY 2025-26 transfer falls under the new rules, so learn them first; learn the old ones only to answer "what changed".

4.1 Capital gains — chargeability, capital asset, transfer

Intuition

If you buy a plot for ₹10 lakh and sell it later for ₹80 lakh, the ₹70 lakh increase is not salary or business profit — it is growth in the value of what you own. The Act taxes such growth only when you transfer the asset, and at lower rates if you held it long.

Definition — sec 45(1)

Any profits or gains arising from the transfer of a capital asset effected in the previous year are chargeable under the head "Capital gains" and are deemed to be the income of the previous year in which the transfer took place (unless exempt u/s 54, 54B, 54EC, 54F etc.).

Conditions for charge

  1. There must be a capital asset.
  2. It must be transferred.
  3. Transfer must take place in the previous year.
  4. There must be a gain (or loss) from the transfer.
  5. The gain must not be exempt.
Capital asset — sec 2(14)

Includes: property of any kind held by the assessee, whether or not connected with business; securities held by FPIs; ULIPs not exempt u/s 10(10D).

Excludes: (i) stock-in-trade, consumable stores or raw materials held for business/profession; (ii) personal effects — movable property (wearing apparel, furniture, car) held for personal use, but not jewellery, archaeological collections, drawings, paintings, sculptures or any work of art (these remain capital assets); (iii) rural agricultural land in India — land not within a municipality/cantonment with population of 10,000 or more, and not within 2 km (population above 10,000 up to 1 lakh), 6 km (above 1 lakh up to 10 lakh) or 8 km (above 10 lakh) of its limits, measured aerially; (iv) Gold Deposit Bonds, Special Bearer Bonds 1991, deposit certificates under the Gold Monetisation Scheme.

Transfer — sec 2(47)

Includes: sale, exchange or relinquishment of the asset; extinguishment of any rights in it; compulsory acquisition under any law; conversion of a capital asset into stock-in-trade; maturity or redemption of a zero-coupon bond; allowing possession of immovable property in part performance of a contract (sec 53A TPA); any transaction (e.g., through membership of a co-operative society or company) that enables enjoyment of immovable property.

Transactions not regarded as transfer — sec 46, 47 (main ones)

  • Distribution of assets on partition of an HUF; transfer under a gift, will or irrevocable trust (sec 47(iii)) — but the recipient may be taxed u/s 56(2)(x) if not exempt.
  • Transfer between a holding company and its wholly-owned Indian subsidiary (and vice versa).
  • Transfer of assets in a scheme of amalgamation/demerger to an Indian company; allotment of shares in the amalgamated company.
  • Conversion of bonds/debentures into shares of the same company.
  • Distribution of assets by a company on liquidation (not taxable in the company's hands; shareholders are taxed u/s 46(2)).
  • Transfer of Sovereign Gold Bonds by redemption by an individual.
Indian example

Listed-equity gains from shares bought on NSE or BSE (e.g., shares of Reliance Industries or HDFC Bank) are taxed u/s 111A/112A because securities transaction tax (STT) is paid on them. Gains on debt mutual funds bought on or after 1 April 2023 are always short-term (sec 50AA) and taxed at slab rates.

4.2 Short-term and long-term capital gains — periods of holding

Rule for transfers on or after 23-7-2024 (sec 2(42A))

A capital asset is short-term if held for not more than 12 months (listed securities — listed shares, listed debentures/bonds, units of UTI, units of equity-oriented mutual funds, zero-coupon bonds, listed units of business trusts) or not more than 24 months (all other assets — land, building, unlisted shares, gold, jewellery, paintings, etc.). Held longer → long-term. Unlisted debentures/bonds, market-linked debentures and specified mutual funds (debt funds acquired on/after 1-4-2023) are always short-term (sec 50AA).

Special rules for counting the period of holding

  • Asset received by gift/will/inheritance/partition: include the previous owner's holding period.
  • Bonus shares/rights shares: from the date of allotment.
  • Shares in an amalgamated company: include the holding period of shares in the amalgamating company.
  • Flat through a co-operative society: from the date of allotment of shares/letter of allotment.
  • Shares allotted under ESOP: from the date of allotment.

4.3 Computation of capital gains, cost of acquisition and rates

Formula — sec 48

STCG / LTCG = Full value of consideration (FVC) − expenditure wholly and exclusively in connection with the transfer (brokerage, legal fees, stamp duty paid by seller) − cost of acquisition (COA) − cost of improvement (COI) − exemptions u/s 54 etc.

Grandfathered indexation option (land or building, or both, acquired before 23-7-2024, transferred by a resident individual or HUF): tax = lower of (a) 12.5% of LTCG computed without indexation; (b) 20% of LTCG computed with indexation, where
Indexed COA = COA × CII of year of transfer ÷ CII of year of acquisition (or of 2001-02 if acquired earlier);
Indexed COI = COI × CII of year of transfer ÷ CII of year of improvement.

Full value of consideration for land/building = higher of actual consideration and stamp duty value (sec 50C), unless SDV does not exceed 110% of actual consideration.

Cost of acquisition — special cases

SituationCost of acquisition
Asset acquired before 1-4-2001Actual cost or FMV on 1-4-2001, at the option of the assessee (for land/building, FMV on 1-4-2001 cannot exceed the stamp duty value on that date — Finance Act, 2020)
Asset received by gift/will/inheritance (sec 49(1))Cost to the previous owner (plus his improvements); if acquired by him before 1-4-2001, option of FMV on 1-4-2001; indexation (where allowed) from the year the assessee first held it
Bonus sharesNil (if allotted before 1-4-2001: FMV on 1-4-2001)
Rights sharesAmount paid; renunciation of rights: nil cost for the renouncer
Self-generated goodwill, trademark, brand, right to manufacture, tenancy rights, route permits, loom hoursNil
ESOP sharesFMV taken as perquisite on the date of exercise

Rules that apply to special-rate gains
  • Chapter VI-A deductions cannot be set off against STCG u/s 111A or LTCG u/s 112/112A (sec 80A/112/112A).
  • A resident individual whose other income is below the basic exemption limit can adjust the unused exemption against these gains.
  • Under the new regime for AY 2026-27, the sec 87A rebate is not available against tax on special-rate incomes such as 111A/112A (Finance Act, 2025 amendment).
  • Surcharge on 111A/112A gains and dividends is capped at 15%.

4.4 Exemptions from capital gains — sec 54, 54B, 54EC, 54F

FeatureSec 54Sec 54BSec 54ECSec 54F
WhoIndividual / HUFIndividual / HUFAny assesseeIndividual / HUF
Asset transferredLong-term residential house (income chargeable under HP)Agricultural land (urban) used by the individual or his parents (or HUF) for agriculture for 2 years immediately before transferLong-term land or building or bothAny long-term asset other than a residential house
Gain coveredLTCGSTCG or LTCGLTCGLTCG
Investment requiredPurchase one residential house in India 1 year before or 2 years after transfer, or construct within 3 years (two houses once in a lifetime if LTCG ≤ ₹2 crore)Purchase other agricultural land (urban or rural) within 2 years after transferBonds of NHAI, REC, PFC, IRFC or other notified long-term specified assets, within 6 months, redeemable after 5 yearsPurchase one residential house 1 year before/2 years after, or construct within 3 years
ExemptionLTCG or cost of new house, whichever is lower; cost capped at ₹10 croreCG or cost of new land, whichever is lowerLTCG or amount invested, whichever lower; maximum ₹50 lakh per FY (year of transfer and next year together)LTCG × (cost of new house, max ₹10 crore) ÷ net consideration; full exemption if entire net consideration invested

Capital Gains Account SchemeUnutilised amount deposited in CGAS by due date of returnYesNot applicableYes
Formulas

Sec 54: Exempt = min(LTCG, cost of new house (≤ ₹10 crore))
Sec 54B: Exempt = min(CG, cost of new agricultural land)
Sec 54EC: Exempt = min(LTCG, amount invested (≤ ₹50 lakh))
Sec 54F: Exempt = LTCG × amount invested (≤ ₹10 crore) ÷ net consideration, where net consideration = FVC − transfer expenses.

Course vs industry

The syllabus lists 54, 54B, 54EC and 54F; the course plan session list shows 54, 54EC and 54F. Prepare all four. Practitioners often combine 54 and 54EC on the same gain (house + bonds), which is allowed as long as the same rupee of gain is not exempted twice.

4.5 Capital gains — worked problems

Problem 1 — land acquired before 23-7-2024: both options, then 54EC

Mrs Rekha (resident) bought a plot in 2003-04 for ₹10,00,000; improvement in 2010-11 ₹2,00,000. Sold on 15-12-2025 for ₹80,00,000 (SDV same); brokerage 1% = ₹80,000.

  1. Option A (12.5%, no indexation): LTCG = 80,00,000 − 80,000 − 10,00,000 − 2,00,000 = ₹67,20,000; tax = 12.5% = ₹8,40,000.
  2. Option B (20% with indexation): indexed COA = 10,00,000 × 376 ÷ 109 = ₹34,49,541; indexed COI = 2,00,000 × 376 ÷ 167 = ₹4,50,299; LTCG = 79,20,000 − 34,49,541 − 4,50,299 = ₹40,20,160; tax = 20% = ₹8,04,032.
  3. Without any exemption, option B is lower: tax ₹8,04,032 (+ cess).
  4. With 54EC: she invests ₹50,00,000 in REC bonds within 6 months. Option A: taxable = 67,20,000 − 50,00,000 = 17,20,000 → tax ₹2,15,000. Option B: exemption limited to the gain ₹40,20,160 → taxable nil → tax nil.
  5. Best result: option B with 54EC — LTCG fully exempt. (Reverse insight: under option B she needed to invest only ₹40,20,160 in bonds for nil tax.)
Problem 2 — asset acquired before 1-4-2001 (FMV option, SDV cap)

Mr Das (resident) bought a house in 1995 for ₹2,00,000. FMV on 1-4-2001 ₹15,00,000; stamp duty value on 1-4-2001 ₹12,00,000. Sold in November 2025 for ₹1,20,00,000 (no expenses). Ignore exemptions.

  1. FMV on 1-4-2001 is capped at SDV on that date: min(15,00,000, 12,00,000) = ₹12,00,000. COA = higher of actual cost 2,00,000 and 12,00,000 = ₹12,00,000.
  2. Option A: LTCG = 1,20,00,000 − 12,00,000 = ₹1,08,00,000; tax at 12.5% = ₹13,50,000.
  3. Option B: indexed COA = 12,00,000 × 376 ÷ 100 = ₹45,12,000; LTCG = ₹74,88,000; tax at 20% = ₹14,97,600.
  4. Pay the lower: ₹13,50,000 (option A) + surcharge/cess as applicable. Indexation does not always help — for very old assets with high appreciation, 12.5% is often cheaper.
Problem 3 — listed shares: 112A with grandfathering, and 111A
  1. Ms Tara bought 1,000 listed shares in 2016 at ₹100; FMV (highest quoted price) on 31-1-2018 ₹250; sold in September 2025 at ₹400 (STT paid). COA = max(100, min(250, 400)) = ₹250. LTCG = 1,000 × (400 − 250) = ₹1,50,000.
  2. She also has other 112A LTCG of ₹1,50,000 in the year: total 112A LTCG = ₹3,00,000. Taxable = 3,00,000 − 1,25,000 = ₹1,75,000; tax at 12.5% = ₹21,875.
  3. STCG: shares bought in January 2025 and sold in November 2025 (≤ 12 months) with gain ₹1,50,000 → sec 111A at 20% = ₹30,000; with 4% cess = ₹31,200 (no 87A rebate on 111A tax in the new regime).
Problem 4 — sec 54 (sale of residential house)
  1. Mr Iyer's LTCG on sale of his residential house (after choosing the cheaper option) = ₹60,00,000. Within 2 years he buys a new flat for ₹45,00,000.
  2. Exempt u/s 54 = min(60,00,000, 45,00,000) = ₹45,00,000. Taxable LTCG = ₹15,00,000.
  3. If he had deposited ₹15,00,000 more in the Capital Gains Account Scheme before the return due date and used it for construction within 3 years, the full gain would be exempt. If the new flat is sold within 3 years, its cost is reduced by ₹45,00,000 for computing gain on that sale.
Problem 5 — sec 54F (proportionate exemption)
  1. Mr Khan sells a commercial plot: net consideration ₹50,00,000; LTCG ₹20,00,000. He owns no residential house and buys one for ₹30,00,000 within 2 years.
  2. Exempt = 20,00,000 × 30,00,000 ÷ 50,00,000 = ₹12,00,000. Taxable LTCG = ₹8,00,000.
  3. Reverse: to exempt the whole ₹20 lakh gain he must invest the whole net consideration of ₹50 lakh.
Problem 6 — sec 54B (agricultural land)

Mr Patil (resident) bought urban agricultural land in 2018-19 for ₹15,00,000 and used it for agriculture; sold it in October 2025 for ₹40,00,000; bought new agricultural land for ₹18,00,000 within 2 years.

  1. Option A: LTCG = 40,00,000 − 15,00,000 = 25,00,000; less 54B 18,00,000 = ₹7,00,000; tax at 12.5% = ₹87,500.
  2. Option B: indexed COA = 15,00,000 × 376 ÷ 280 = ₹20,14,286; LTCG = ₹19,85,714; less 54B 18,00,000 = ₹1,85,714; tax at 20% = ₹37,143.
  3. Tax payable = ₹37,143 (option B is lower) + cess. Had the land been rural agricultural land, it would not be a capital asset at all.
Problem 7 — sec 50C stamp duty value
  1. A flat is sold for ₹50,00,000. Case (i): SDV ₹56,00,000 > 110% of 50,00,000 (= 55,00,000) → FVC = ₹56,00,000. Case (ii): SDV ₹54,00,000 ≤ 55,00,000 → FVC = ₹50,00,000.
Self-check
1. Gold jewellery bought in March 2024 and sold in May 2026 — STCG or LTCG?

Held more than 24 months → LTCG at 12.5% (transfer after 23-7-2024).

2. Can 54EC be claimed on LTCG from sale of shares?

No — only on LTCG from land or building.

3. Is your personal car a capital asset?

No — personal effect; no capital gain or loss on its sale.

4.6 Income from other sources (sec 56–59)

Definition — sec 56(1)

Income of every kind which is not exempt and is not chargeable under any of the other four heads is chargeable under "Income from other sources" (a residual head). Sec 56(2) lists incomes always taxed here.

IncomeTreatment
Dividends (56(2)(i))Taxable at slab rates from AY 2021-22 (unless held as business stock); only interest expense up to 20% of dividend is deductible; TDS u/s 194
Interest on bank deposits (savings, FD, RD), interest on securities (56(2)(id)), post office deposits, NSC, company depositsTaxable (on accrual or receipt per method). Exempt: post office savings account interest up to ₹3,500 (₹7,000 joint) u/s 10(15); PPF interest. Deductions 80TTA/80TTB in old regime only
Family pension (received by legal heirs of a deceased employee)Taxable; deduction u/s 57(iia) = 1/3 of family pension or ₹25,000 (new regime) / ₹15,000 (old regime), whichever is lower
Winnings from lotteries, crossword puzzles, races including horse races, card games, gambling, betting of any form (56(2)(ib))Flat 30% u/s 115BB (plus surcharge/cess); no basic exemption, no deduction or expense (sec 58(4)); TDS 30% u/s 194B/194BB. Online games: sec 115BBJ at 30%
Gifts — money or property received without or for inadequate consideration (56(2)(x))See the gift rules below
Letting of plant, machinery or furniture (56(2)(ii)); composite letting of building with them where inseparable (56(2)(iii))Taxable here unless business income; deductions: repairs, insurance, depreciation (sec 57(ii), (iii))
Rent from sub-lettingTaxable here (tenant is not the owner); deduct rent paid to the landlord and related expenses
Director's sitting fees / remuneration not taxable as salaryTaxable here
Casual income (not lottery), e.g., prize from a TV quizIf winnings of the 56(2)(ib) kind: 30%; otherwise normal rates
Interest on income-tax refundTaxable here
Royalty / fees (not business or profession), e.g., occasional authorTaxable here
Agricultural income from land outside IndiaTaxable here (for ROR)
Interest on compensation/enhanced compensation (56(2)(viii))Taxable in year of receipt; 50% deduction u/s 57(iv)
Compensation on termination of employment received from a person other than the employer (56(2)(xi))Taxable here
Salary of MPs/MLAsTaxable here (no employer–employee relationship)

Gifts — sec 56(2)(x) (individual or any person as recipient)

ReceivedWithout considerationFor inadequate consideration
Sum of moneyIf aggregate in the PY exceeds ₹50,000 → whole amount taxable—
Immovable property (land/building)If stamp duty value exceeds ₹50,000 → SDV taxableIf SDV − consideration exceeds the higher of ₹50,000 and 10% of consideration → SDV − consideration taxable
Specified movable property (shares, securities, jewellery, archaeological collections, drawings, paintings, sculptures, works of art, bullion)If aggregate FMV exceeds ₹50,000 → whole FMV taxableIf FMV − consideration exceeds ₹50,000 → the difference taxable
Exempt gifts (not taxed at all)

Received (a) from a relative; (b) on the occasion of the marriage of the individual; (c) under a will or by inheritance; (d) in contemplation of death of the payer; (e) from a local authority; (f) from a registered charitable fund/trust/institution; (g) from an individual by a trust created solely for the relative; (h) in transactions not regarded as transfer u/s 47; (i) by an individual from any person for medical treatment of self or family for Covid-19 (specified conditions).

Relative (for an individual): spouse; brother or sister; brother or sister of the spouse; brother or sister of either parent; any lineal ascendant or descendant; any lineal ascendant or descendant of the spouse; spouse of each of the persons listed above. (A friend, cousin or nephew's friend is not a relative; a cousin is not a relative.)

Deductions u/s 57 and disallowances u/s 58

  • 57(i): commission or remuneration to a banker/agent for realising dividend or interest; interest on money borrowed to earn dividend only up to 20% of the dividend.
  • 57(ii), (iii): repairs, insurance, depreciation on plant/machinery/furniture/building let out; any other expenditure (not capital) incurred wholly and exclusively for earning the income.
  • 57(iia): family pension deduction (above).
  • 57(iv): 50% of interest on compensation/enhanced compensation.
  • 58: no deduction for personal expenses; interest/salary payable outside India without TDS; wealth tax; expenses under 40A; any expense against lottery/gambling winnings (58(4)).

Land bought from an unrelated person for ₹20,00,000; SDV ₹26,00,0006,00,000Difference 6,00,000 exceeds higher of ₹50,000 and 10% of 20,00,000 (2,00,000) → taxable6,00,000

Problem 2 — family pension, lottery, interest, dividend, letting of machinery
  1. Family pension ₹1,20,000 p.a.: deduction new regime = min(40,000, 25,000) = 25,000 → taxable ₹95,000 (old regime: min(40,000, 15,000) = 15,000 → ₹1,05,000).
  2. Lottery: net amount received ₹70,000 after TDS at 30%. Grossed-up winnings = 70,000 ÷ 0.70 = ₹1,00,000. Tax u/s 115BB = 30,000 + cess 1,200 = ₹31,200; TDS credit ₹30,000.
  3. Post office savings interest ₹4,000 (single account): exempt ₹3,500 → taxable ₹500.
  4. Dividend ₹1,00,000; interest on loan taken to buy the shares ₹30,000: deduction limited to 20% of dividend = ₹20,000 → taxable ₹80,000.
  5. Letting of machinery: rent ₹1,20,000; repairs ₹10,000; depreciation ₹15,000; insurance ₹5,000 → taxable ₹90,000.

4.8 Set-off and carry forward of losses (basics needed to reach GTI)

LossSet-off in the same year (sec 70, 71)Carry forward (years)Set off in later years against
House propertyAgainst any head up to ₹2,00,000 (old regime); not against other heads in new regime8 (sec 71B) — even if return is lateHP income only
Business (non-speculative)Against any head except salary8 (sec 72) — return must be filed in timeBusiness income
Speculation businessOnly against speculation income4 (sec 73)Speculation income
Short-term capital lossAgainst STCG or LTCG8 (sec 74)STCG or LTCG
Long-term capital lossOnly against LTCG8LTCG only

Lottery/gambling incomeNo loss can be set off against it——
