Worked example
  1. Salary ₹12,00,000; loss from let-out HP ₹2,50,000; STCL ₹40,000; LTCG (sec 112) ₹1,00,000.
  2. Old regime: HP loss set off against salary ₹2,00,000; ₹50,000 carried forward. STCL set off against LTCG → LTCG ₹60,000. GTI = 10,00,000 + 60,000 = ₹10,60,000.
  3. New regime: no HP loss set-off against salary; STCL still set off against LTCG. GTI = 12,00,000 + 60,000 = ₹12,60,000 (salary figure taken as income under the head, as in the old-regime line); HP loss of ₹2,50,000 carried forward to be set off only against HP income.

4.9 Unit IV exam kit

Likely questions: Define capital asset and transfer (5) · STCG vs LTCG under the 2024 rules (5) · Exemptions 54/54B/54EC/54F (10) · Cost of acquisition in special cases (5) · Incomes taxable under other sources (5) · Gifts u/s 56(2)(x) (5) · CG computation with exemption (10/15).

Model answer (10 marks) — "Explain the exemptions available under sec 54, 54B, 54EC and 54F"

Introduction. The Act encourages reinvestment of capital gains in housing, agriculture and infrastructure by exempting gains reinvested within prescribed time limits.

Sec 54 — residential house to residential house. Individual/HUF; LTCG on a residential house; new house bought 1 year before/2 years after or constructed within 3 years; exemption = lower of LTCG and cost (cap ₹10 crore); lock-in 3 years; CGAS for unutilised amount.

Sec 54B — agricultural land. Individual/HUF; urban agricultural land used for agriculture for 2 years by assessee/parents; STCG or LTCG; new agricultural land within 2 years; exemption = lower of gain and cost.

Sec 54EC — bonds. Any assessee; LTCG on land/building; investment within 6 months in NHAI/REC/PFC/IRFC bonds (5-year lock-in); maximum ₹50 lakh.

Sec 54F — any other asset to house. Individual/HUF; LTCG on any asset other than a residential house; exemption proportionate: LTCG × investment ÷ net consideration; must not own more than one other house.

Illustration. Net consideration ₹50 lakh, LTCG ₹20 lakh, new house ₹30 lakh → exempt u/s 54F = ₹12 lakh.

Conclusion. Sec 54/54F/54B require reinvestment in the same class of real asset; 54EC gives a financial-asset route. All are available under both regimes because they operate within the capital gains head.

Model answer (5 marks) — "What incomes are taxable under 'Income from other sources'?"

Meaning. Residual head u/s 56: any income not falling under the other four heads.

Specific incomes u/s 56(2): dividends; winnings from lotteries, crossword puzzles, races, card games and betting (30% u/s 115BB); interest on securities; rent from letting plant, machinery or furniture and composite letting; gifts above ₹50,000 from non-relatives; interest on enhanced compensation; compensation on termination of employment from a non-employer.

Other common items: bank/post office interest, family pension, sub-letting rent, director's sitting fees, interest on income-tax refund, royalty of an occasional author, foreign agricultural income.

Deductions: sec 57 (collection charges, repairs/depreciation, family pension 1/3 or ₹25,000, 50% of interest on compensation); none against lottery winnings.

Unit V — Computation of Tax Liability of Individuals

Units I–IV taught you how to compute income under each head. Unit V joins the pieces: add the heads, set off losses, subtract the few deductions the new regime allows, apply the slab rates and special rates, then rebate, surcharge and cess, then subtract taxes already paid, and finally file the return online. Section C of your ESE (15 marks, compulsory) is almost always a case of exactly this kind.

5.1 Computation of gross total income and taxable income — the master procedure

Intuition

Think of five separate buckets (Salary, House Property, Business/Profession, Capital Gains, Other Sources). You first fill each bucket using that head's own rules. Then you pour them into one big tank (Gross Total Income), let a small amount drain out through the deduction tap (Chapter VI-A), and what remains is Total Income. Tax is charged on what is left in the tank, some of it at "slab rates" and some at "special rates".

Definition

Gross Total Income (GTI) [sec 80B(5)] is the total income computed in accordance with the Act under the five heads, after applying clubbing provisions and after set-off of current-year and brought-forward losses, but before the deductions under Chapter VI-A (sec 80C to 80U).

Total Income [sec 2(45)] is GTI minus Chapter VI-A deductions, rounded off to the nearest multiple of ₹10 (sec 288A). Tax is levied on total income.

Why it matters

Your CIA-2 and CIA-3 rubrics give marks separately for (i) organising incomes under the correct head, (ii) applying provisions in reaching GTI, and (iii) choosing the correct slab. A clean step-by-step layout earns marks even when one figure goes wrong.

Formula — the skeleton of every computation

GTI = Salary + HP + PGBP + CG + IFOS (after clubbing and set-off)
Total Income (TI) = GTI − Chapter VI-A deductions  → round to ₹10
Tax = Slab tax on (TI − special-rate income) + Special-rate tax − Rebate 87A + Surcharge + Cess (4%)
Net payable = Tax − Relief (89/90/91) − TDS − TCS − Advance tax  → round to ₹10

Variables: normal income = income taxed at slab rates; special-rate income = STCG u/s 111A, LTCG u/s 112/112A, casual winnings u/s 115BB, etc.; cess = Health and Education Cess on (tax + surcharge).

The statement layout examiners expect

Computation of Total Income and Tax Liability of Mr X, resident, for AY 2026-27 (new regime u/s 115BAC)₹₹
I. Income from Salary (working note 1)
Gross salary…
Less: standard deduction u/s 16(ia)(75,000)…
II. Income from House Property (WN 2)
III. Profits and Gains of Business or Profession (WN 3)
IV. Capital Gains — STCG (111A / others), LTCG (112 / 112A) shown separately (WN 4)
V. Income from Other Sources (WN 5)
Gross Total Income…
Less: deductions under Chapter VI-A(…)
Total Income (rounded u/s 288A)…
Tax on normal income at slab rates / special rates / less 87A / add surcharge / add cess / less prepaid taxes…
Tax payable / (refund) rounded u/s 288B…
Trap
  • Exempt incomes (e.g., agricultural income, gift from a relative, sec 10(10) gratuity within limits) are not part of GTI. Show them under a note "exempt — not included".
  • Deductions under Chapter VI-A are subtracted from GTI, never from a head. 80CCD(2) is not a salary deduction under sec 16 — the employer's NPS contribution is first added to salary (sec 17(1)(viii)) and then deducted from GTI.
  • Chapter VI-A deductions cannot exceed GTI excluding special-rate incomes (sec 80A, 80AB context; 111A/112/112A/115BB incomes are excluded from the deduction base).
Indian example

When a salaried employee at an Indian IT company logs in to the Income Tax Department's e-filing portal, the pre-filled ITR already carries salary from Form 16, interest from banks and dividends reported in the Annual Information Statement (AIS). The portal's computation screen follows this same order: income by head → GTI → deductions → total income → tax → rebate → cess → taxes paid → balance.

Self-check
1. Is the 87A rebate deducted from GTI or from tax?

From tax. It is a rebate of tax, not a deduction from income.

2. Where is brought-forward business loss adjusted — before or after GTI?

Before. Set-off of current and brought-forward losses is part of computing GTI; only Chapter VI-A deductions come after GTI.

3. Total income works out to ₹12,34,567. What figure is taxed?

₹12,34,570 (rounded to the nearest ₹10 u/s 288A; ₹5 or more is rounded up).

5.2 Tax rates for individuals, rebate u/s 87A, surcharge and cess

Definition

Slab rates are progressive rates prescribed by the Finance Act (and, for the new regime, in sec 115BAC(1A)) under which each slice of income is taxed at the rate of its band. Rebate u/s 87A is a reduction in the tax of a resident individual whose total income does not exceed a threshold. Surcharge is an additional tax on the income tax of high-income assessees. Health and Education Cess is 4% of income tax plus surcharge.

A. New regime — sec 115BAC (default), AY 2026-27 NEW REGIME

Total income slab (₹)RateTax on the full slab (₹)Cumulative tax at top of slab (₹)
0 – 4,00,000Nil00
4,00,001 – 8,00,0005%20,00020,000
8,00,001 – 12,00,00010%40,00060,000
12,00,001 – 16,00,00015%60,0001,20,000
16,00,001 – 20,00,00020%80,0002,00,000
20,00,001 – 24,00,00025%1,00,0003,00,000
Above 24,00,00030%—3,00,000 + 30% of excess

Same slabs for every age (no higher exemption for senior citizens in the new regime). Available to individuals, HUFs, AOPs (other than co-operatives), BOIs and artificial juridical persons.

B. Old regime (opt-in) OLD REGIME

SlabBelow 60 yearsSenior citizen (60–79, resident)Super senior (80+, resident)
Up to 2,50,000NilNilNil
2,50,001 – 3,00,0005%NilNil
3,00,001 – 5,00,0005%5%Nil
5,00,001 – 10,00,00020%20%20%
Above 10,00,00030%30%30%
Rebate 87AResident individual with total income ≤ ₹5,00,000: rebate = tax or ₹12,500, whichever is lower

C. Rebate u/s 87A under the new regime (Finance Act, 2025)

Formula — 87A and marginal relief (new regime, resident individual)

If TI ≤ ₹12,00,000: Rebate = lower of (tax on normal income) and ₹60,000 → tax on normal income becomes nil.
If TI > ₹12,00,000: no rebate, but marginal relief: tax (before cess) cannot exceed (TI − 12,00,000). Relief = Tax − (TI − 12,00,000), if positive.

Marginal relief stops mattering at about TI = ₹12,70,588 (where 60,000 + 15% × excess = excess). Special-rate incomes: the rebate is not available against tax on incomes charged at special rates such as capital gains u/s 111A/112/112A (Finance Act, 2025 amendment), though such income counts in the ₹12 lakh total-income test. Non-residents get no 87A rebate.

D. Surcharge and cess

Total income (individual)Surcharge — new regimeSurcharge — old regime
Up to ₹50 lakhNilNil
₹50 lakh – ₹1 crore10%10%
₹1 crore – ₹2 crore15%15%
₹2 crore – ₹5 crore25%25%
Above ₹5 crore25% (capped)37%
  • Surcharge on tax attributable to dividend and capital gains u/s 111A, 112 and 112A is capped at 15%.
  • Marginal relief on surcharge: (tax + surcharge) on income just above a threshold cannot exceed (tax on the threshold income) + (income above the threshold).
  • Health and Education Cess = 4% of (tax after rebate + surcharge). Cess is charged even if there is no surcharge.
  • Rounding: total income to the nearest ₹10 (sec 288A); tax payable/refund to the nearest ₹10 (sec 288B).

E. Special rates (summary for Unit V computations)

IncomeSectionRateDeductions / exemption limit adjustment
STCG on listed equity shares / equity MF units (STT paid)111A20%No Chapter VI-A; resident can adjust unused basic exemption
LTCG on listed equity / equity MF units (STT paid)112A12.5% on gains above ₹1,25,000Same
Other LTCG11212.5% (or 20% with indexation for land/building acquired before 23-7-2024 by resident individual/HUF, whichever tax is lower)Same
Winnings from lottery, crossword, races, card games, online games115BB / 115BBJ30%No deduction, no basic exemption adjustment, no expenses
Other STCG (e.g., land held ≤ 24 months)—Slab ratesTreated as normal income
Worked example 1 — plain slab tax (easy)

Ms Anjali, resident, has total income (all normal) of ₹18,00,000 under the new regime. Compute tax and average rate.

  1. Tax up to ₹16,00,000 (cumulative column) = ₹1,20,000.
  2. 16,00,001 – 18,00,000 at 20% = 2,00,000 × 20% = ₹40,000.
  3. Tax = 1,20,000 + 40,000 = ₹1,60,000. No rebate (TI > 12 lakh); no surcharge (TI < 50 lakh).
  4. Cess 4% = ₹6,400. Total tax = ₹1,66,400.
  5. Average rate of tax = 1,66,400 ÷ 18,00,000 × 100 = 9.24% (the marginal rate is 20% + cess).
Worked example 2 — 87A and marginal relief (medium)

(a) Mr Kunal's only income is salary of ₹12,75,000 (gross). (b) Ms Priya has gross salary ₹12,90,000 and savings bank interest ₹5,000. Both resident, new regime.

(a) Kunal

  1. Salary after standard deduction = 12,75,000 − 75,000 = ₹12,00,000 = total income.
  2. Tax on 12,00,000 = ₹60,000; rebate u/s 87A = ₹60,000 (TI ≤ 12 lakh). Tax = Nil; no cess on nil tax.

(b) Priya

  1. Salary = 12,90,000 − 75,000 = ₹12,15,000; add interest ₹5,000 (no 80TTA in new regime). TI = ₹12,20,000.
  2. Tax at slab = 60,000 + 15% × 20,000 = ₹63,000. No rebate (TI > 12,00,000).
  3. Marginal relief: income above 12 lakh = ₹20,000; tax 63,000 exceeds 20,000 → relief = ₹43,000; tax = ₹20,000.
  4. Cess 4% = ₹800. Tax payable = ₹20,800.

Read the lesson: ₹20,000 of extra income above 12 lakh costs ₹20,800 of tax — the effective marginal rate in the relief band is 104% including cess, which is why tax planners try to keep income at or just below ₹12 lakh.

Worked example 3 — surcharge with marginal relief (hard)

Ms Farah (resident, new regime) has normal total income of (a) ₹60,00,000; (b) ₹50,50,000. Compute tax.

  1. Tax on ₹50,00,000 = 3,00,000 + 30% × 26,00,000 = ₹10,80,000.
  2. (a) Tax on 60,00,000 = 3,00,000 + 30% × 36,00,000 = ₹13,80,000. Surcharge 10% = ₹1,38,000. Marginal relief test: limit = 10,80,000 + (60,00,000 − 50,00,000) = ₹20,80,000; tax + surcharge = 15,18,000 < limit → no relief. Cess 4% on 15,18,000 = ₹60,720. Total = ₹15,78,720.
  3. (b) Tax on 50,50,000 = 3,00,000 + 30% × 26,50,000 = ₹10,95,000. Surcharge 10% = ₹1,09,500; tax + surcharge = ₹12,04,500.
  4. Limit = 10,80,000 + 50,000 = ₹11,30,000. Marginal relief = 12,04,500 − 11,30,000 = ₹74,500 → surcharge payable = 1,09,500 − 74,500 = ₹35,000.
  5. Tax + surcharge = ₹11,30,000; cess 4% = ₹45,200. Total = ₹11,75,200.
Worked example 4 — rebate cannot absorb special-rate tax (reverse thinking)

Mr Raghavan, 68, resident, receives pension from his former employer ₹6,00,000, fixed-deposit interest ₹3,20,000 and LTCG u/s 112A (listed shares) ₹2,00,000. New regime. Compute tax.

  1. Pension is "salary": 6,00,000 − standard deduction 75,000 = ₹5,25,000.
  2. Other sources: FD interest ₹3,20,000 (80TTB is an old-regime deduction; not allowed).
  3. Normal income = 5,25,000 + 3,20,000 = ₹8,45,000; LTCG 112A = ₹2,00,000; TI = ₹10,45,000 (≤ 12 lakh, so 87A is available).
  4. Tax on normal income = 20,000 + 10% × 45,000 = ₹24,500; rebate 87A = ₹24,500 → nil.
  5. Tax on LTCG = 12.5% × (2,00,000 − 1,25,000) = ₹9,375 — the rebate is not available against this special-rate tax.
  6. Cess 4% = ₹375. Tax payable = ₹9,750.
Course vs industry

Your course plan calls this block "Unit VI: Computation of Tax Liability" in its session table, while the syllabus calls it Unit V — same content. Many older textbooks describe the 87A rebate as "₹25,000 up to ₹7 lakh" (the AY 2024-25 and 2025-26 rule). For AY 2026-27 the Finance Act, 2025 raised it to ₹60,000 up to ₹12 lakh. Use ₹60,000/₹12 lakh unless the paper says otherwise.

Indian example

In the Union Budget presented on 1 February 2025, the Finance Minister announced that no income tax would be payable up to ₹12 lakh of income under the new regime (₹12.75 lakh for salaried taxpayers, thanks to the ₹75,000 standard deduction). This is exactly Kunal's case above.

Self-check
1. A resident's TI is ₹11,00,000, all normal income. Tax under new regime?

Slab tax = 20,000 + 30,000 = ₹50,000; rebate ₹50,000; tax nil.

2. Can a non-resident with TI ₹10 lakh claim 87A?

No. Rebate u/s 87A is only for resident individuals. Tax = ₹40,000 + cess ₹1,600 = ₹41,600.

3. On what base is cess charged?

4% of (income tax after rebate + surcharge).

4. What is the maximum surcharge rate under the new regime?

25% (the 37% rate applies only in the old regime above ₹5 crore).

5.3 Deductions from GTI (Chapter VI-A) — what the new regime allows and what it takes away

Intuition

The old regime is like a shop with high list prices but many discount coupons (80C, 80D, HRA…). The new regime is a shop with low list prices and almost no coupons. Unit V asks you to know exactly which few coupons still work in the new shop.

Definition

Chapter VI-A deductions (sec 80A to 80U) are amounts deducted from GTI to arrive at total income, subject to the rule that the aggregate deduction cannot exceed GTI, and cannot be claimed against STCG u/s 111A, LTCG u/s 112/112A or casual winnings u/s 115BB.

A. Deductions allowed from GTI under the new regime NEW REGIME

SectionWhatLimit
80CCD(2)Employer's contribution to the employee's NPS (Tier I) accountUp to 14% of salary (basic + DA forming part of retirement benefits) for all employers under the new regime
80CCH(2)Contribution by the Central Government to the Agniveer Corpus Fund of an AgniveerWhole contribution (the Agniveer's own contribution u/s 80CCH(1) is also allowed)
80JJAABusiness assessee (audited u/s 44AB) employing additional employees30% of additional employee cost for 3 years

Other items that survive in the new regime (not Chapter VI-A, but you must remember them): standard deduction ₹75,000 (sec 16(ia)); family pension deduction lower of 1/3 or ₹25,000 (sec 57(iia)); interest on borrowed capital for a let-out property (sec 24(b)); exemptions for gratuity, commuted pension, leave encashment on retirement, retrenchment and VRS compensation; transport allowance for specially-abled employees, conveyance/travel/daily allowances for official duty; capital gains exemptions u/s 54, 54B, 54EC, 54F; sec 10(10D) life insurance maturity (subject to conditions).

B. Not available in the new regime OLD REGIME ONLY

SectionNatureLimit (old regime, AY 2026-27)
80CLIC premium, PPF, EPF (own contribution), ELSS, 5-year bank FD, NSC, tuition fees of up to 2 children, housing loan principal, Sukanya Samriddhi, SCSSAggregate ₹1,50,000 (sec 80CCE)
80CCCPension fund of LIC/other insurer
80CCD(1)Employee's own NPS contribution (10% of salary; 20% of GTI for self-employed)
80CCD(1B)Additional own NPS contribution₹50,000 over and above ₹1.5 lakh

Also withdrawn in the new regime: HRA (10(13A)), LTC (10(5)), children education and hostel allowances, entertainment allowance and professional tax deductions (16(ii), 16(iii)), interest on self-occupied property (24(b)), set-off of HP loss against other heads, additional depreciation 32(1)(iia), and several business deductions (35AD, 35(2AA), 33AB, 10AA).

Worked example 1 — 80CCD(2) within the 14% limit (easy)

Basic salary ₹12,00,000, DA (forming part) ₹3,00,000. Employer contributes ₹2,40,000 to NPS. New regime.

  1. Salary for 80CCD(2) = 12,00,000 + 3,00,000 = ₹15,00,000; limit 14% = ₹2,10,000.
  2. ₹2,40,000 is included in gross salary u/s 17(1)(viii).
  3. Deduction u/s 80CCD(2) = lower of 2,40,000 and 2,10,000 = ₹2,10,000. The excess ₹30,000 stays taxed.
Worked example 2 — old-regime deduction package (medium)

Mr Dev (38, old regime) paid: PPF ₹60,000; LIC premium ₹25,000; ELSS ₹40,000; tuition fees for 2 children ₹50,000; housing loan principal ₹30,000; health insurance for self and family ₹22,000; for his parents aged 65 ₹38,000; savings bank interest earned ₹14,000. Compute deductions.

  1. 80C qualifying = 60,000 + 25,000 + 40,000 + 50,000 + 30,000 = ₹2,05,000 → restricted to ₹1,50,000.
  2. 80D = 22,000 (within ₹25,000) + 38,000 (within ₹50,000 for senior parents) = ₹60,000.
  3. 80TTA = lower of 14,000 and 10,000 = ₹10,000.
  4. Total Chapter VI-A = 1,50,000 + 60,000 + 10,000 = ₹2,20,000. Under the new regime the same payments give nil deduction.
Worked example 3 — 80GG (hard, old regime)

Ms Isha, self-employed, pays rent ₹12,000 p.m.; adjusted total income (before 80GG) ₹6,00,000; no HRA; owns no house.

  1. (a) ₹5,000 × 12 = ₹60,000.
  2. (b) 25% × 6,00,000 = ₹1,50,000.
  3. (c) Rent 1,44,000 − 10% × 6,00,000 = ₹84,000.
  4. Deduction = least = ₹60,000 (old regime only).
Indian example

Many private employers in India now offer an "NPS corporate model" option in the salary structure precisely because employer contributions u/s 80CCD(2) remain deductible under the default new regime; mutual-fund houses, by contrast, have seen ELSS (80C) lose appeal among young taxpayers who have moved to the new regime.

Trap

Students deduct 80C in a question that says "compute under the new regime". Zero marks for that step. Write: "80C, 80D and 80TTA are not allowed u/s 115BAC; only 80CCD(2) is claimed."

Self-check
1. Name the three Chapter VI-A deductions allowed under the new regime.

80CCD(2), 80CCH(2) (and 80CCH(1)), 80JJAA.

2. Can 80CCD(2) be deducted from LTCG u/s 112A?

No. Chapter VI-A deductions cannot be claimed against incomes taxed at special rates.

3. Is family pension deduction a Chapter VI-A deduction?

No. It is a deduction u/s 57(iia) under "Income from other sources"; under the new regime it is lower of 1/3 or ₹25,000.

5.4 Choosing between the old and new regimes

Intuition

Under the old regime you pay higher rates but on a smaller income (after HRA, 80C, 80D, home-loan interest…). Under the new regime you pay lower rates on a bigger income. The regime that wins depends on how much you can legitimately deduct. Compute both; choose the lower tax; write the reason.

Definition — the option (sec 115BAC(6))

The new regime applies by default. An individual/HUF without business or professional income may opt for the old regime every year in the return filed on or before the due date u/s 139(1). An individual/HUF with business or professional income opts out by filing Form 10-IEA on or before the due date u/s 139(1); having opted out, such a person can re-enter the new regime only once. A salaried employee also tells the employer his intended regime for TDS purposes, but the final choice is made in the return.

Worked example — Mr Rohit: compute under both regimes and advise

Mr Rohit (35, resident, Bengaluru) earns basic salary ₹9,00,000, HRA ₹3,00,000 and special allowance ₹6,00,000 (gross salary ₹18,00,000). He pays rent ₹2,70,000 p.a., professional tax ₹2,500, interest on housing loan for his self-occupied flat in another city ₹2,00,000, and invests: 80C items ₹1,50,000, own NPS ₹50,000 (80CCD(1B)), health insurance self ₹25,000 and senior-citizen parents ₹50,000. Advise him.

WN 1 — HRA exemption (old regime), non-metro 40%: least of (a) HRA received ₹3,00,000; (b) rent − 10% of salary = 2,70,000 − 90,000 = ₹1,80,000; (c) 40% × 9,00,000 = ₹3,60,000 → exempt ₹1,80,000.

ParticularsOld regime (₹)New regime (₹)
Gross salary18,00,00018,00,000
Less: HRA exempt u/s 10(13A)(1,80,000)—
Less: standard deduction u/s 16(ia)(50,000)(75,000)
Less: professional tax u/s 16(iii)(2,500)—
Income from salary15,67,50017,25,000
House property (SOP): interest u/s 24(b)(2,00,000)Nil (not allowed)
Gross total income13,67,50017,25,000
Less: 80C 1,50,000 + 80CCD(1B) 50,000 + 80D 25,000 + 50,000(2,75,000)—
Total income10,92,50017,25,000
Tax: old = 12,500 + 1,00,000 + 30% × 92,500; new = 1,20,000 + 20% × 1,25,0001,40,2501,45,000
Add: cess 4%5,6105,800
Tax liability1,45,8601,50,800

Advice: the old regime saves 1,50,800 − 1,45,860 = ₹4,940. Rohit should opt for the old regime in his return filed by the due date. If he stops paying the home-loan interest or moves into his own flat (losing HRA), the new regime becomes better.

Break-even deductions — a quick decision table

For a salaried resident below 60 whose only income is salary, the table shows how much more than the ₹50,000 old standard deduction the old regime must allow (HRA + 16(iii) + 24(b) + Chapter VI-A together) before it beats the new regime. Computed on slab tax (cess affects both equally).

Gross salary (₹)New-regime tax before cess (₹)Old-regime reductions needed beyond ₹50,000 to match (₹)
8,00,00002,50,000
10,00,00004,50,000
12,75,00007,25,000
15,00,00093,7505,43,750
20,00,0001,85,0007,08,333
25,00,000 and above3,07,500 at 25 lakh8,00,000

Factors to weigh (for a 5/10-mark answer)

  1. Size of eligible deductions — HRA, home-loan interest on SOP, 80C, 80D, 80CCD(1B), 80E, 80G. Compare with the break-even table.
  2. Nature of income — business/professional income restricts switching (Form 10-IEA, one re-entry); salaried persons can switch every year.
  3. Income level — up to ₹12 lakh (₹12.75 lakh salary) the new regime gives nil tax; the old gives nil only up to ₹5 lakh.
  4. House-property loss — the old regime allows up to ₹2 lakh set-off against other heads; the new regime allows none.
  5. Age — senior citizens have a higher basic exemption and 80TTB only under the old regime.
  6. Liquidity and simplicity — the new regime does not force lock-in investments; fewer proofs to keep.
  7. Surcharge — above ₹5 crore the new regime caps surcharge at 25% against 37%.
Indian example

The Government has said in Budget speeches that a large majority of individual taxpayers have moved to the new regime since it became the default from AY 2024-25; the Budget 2025 changes (nil tax up to ₹12 lakh) were designed to push this further.

Self-check
1. A salaried person chose the old regime last year. Can she choose the new regime this year?

Yes. A person without business income can choose afresh every year in the return filed by the due date u/s 139(1).

2. Which form must a person with business income file to opt out of the new regime?

Form 10-IEA, on or before the due date u/s 139(1).

3. Gross salary ₹10 lakh, total old-regime deductions ₹3 lakh beyond the standard deduction. Which regime?

New. Tax is nil in the new regime (TI 9.25 lakh ≤ 12 lakh); the old regime needs ₹4.5 lakh of such deductions just to reach nil.

5.5 Comprehensive problems — computation of total income and tax liability

Comprehensive problem 1 — all five heads, new regime (Section C level, 15 marks)

Mr Arjun Mehta (42), resident, Bengaluru, furnishes the following for PY 2025-26:

  1. Basic salary ₹90,000 p.m.; DA 20% of basic (forms part of retirement benefits); HRA ₹25,000 p.m. (he pays rent of ₹30,000 p.m.); bonus ₹1,50,000; employer's contribution to NPS 14% of (basic + DA); employer's contribution to RPF 12% of (basic + DA); professional tax paid ₹2,400.
  2. A flat let out at ₹35,000 p.m. (municipal value ₹3,80,000, fair rent ₹4,00,000, standard rent ₹4,50,000); municipal taxes paid by him ₹18,000; interest on loan taken for the flat ₹2,10,000.
  3. Part-time online tutoring business: net profit per P&L ₹3,20,000 after debiting personal expenses ₹15,000, a cash payment of ₹14,000 made to one supplier in a single day, income tax ₹20,000 and depreciation ₹40,000 (allowable under the Income-tax Rules ₹32,000), and after crediting dividend from an Indian company ₹12,000.
  4. STCG on listed shares held 8 months (STT paid) ₹80,000; LTCG on listed shares held 20 months (STT paid) ₹2,75,000.
  5. Savings bank interest ₹18,000; fixed-deposit interest ₹45,000; gift from a friend ₹60,000.
  6. TDS on salary ₹2,50,000.

Working notes

WN 1 — Salary. Basic 90,000 × 12 = ₹10,80,000; DA 20% = ₹2,16,000; salary for PF/NPS = ₹12,96,000. HRA 25,000 × 12 = ₹3,00,000, fully taxable (sec 10(13A) not available u/s 115BAC). Employer NPS 14% × 12,96,000 = ₹1,81,440, included u/s 17(1)(viii). Employer RPF 12% × 12,96,000 = ₹1,55,520 — exempt as it does not exceed 12% of salary. Employer PF + NPS = ₹3,36,960 ≤ ₹7,50,000, so no perquisite u/s 17(2)(vii). Professional tax: no deduction under the new regime.

WN 2 — House property. Expected rent = higher of MV 3,80,000 and FR 4,00,000, limited to SR 4,50,000 = ₹4,00,000. Actual rent = ₹4,20,000. GAV = higher = ₹4,20,000. NAV = 4,20,000 − 18,000 = ₹4,02,000. Standard deduction 30% = ₹1,20,600. Interest u/s 24(b) (let-out: fully allowed, even in new regime) ₹2,10,000. Income = ₹71,400.

WN 3 — Business. 3,20,000 + personal 15,000 + cash payment 14,000 (sec 40A(3): exceeds ₹10,000) + income tax 20,000 (sec 40(a)(ii)) + book depreciation 40,000 − tax depreciation 32,000 − dividend 12,000 (taxed under other sources) = ₹3,65,000.

WN 4 — Other sources. 18,000 + 45,000 + dividend 12,000 + gift 60,000 (aggregate gifts from non-relatives exceed ₹50,000, so the whole amount is taxable u/s 56(2)(x)) = ₹1,35,000. No 80TTA in new regime.

Return form: ITR-3 (he has business income). As he has business income, choosing the old regime would require Form 10-IEA before the due date.

Comprehensive problem 2 — set-off of losses under both regimes

Ms Sneha (30), resident: gross salary ₹9,75,000; loss from a let-out house property ₹2,40,000; business loss ₹1,50,000; STCL on listed shares ₹60,000; LTCG u/s 112A ₹3,00,000; lottery winnings (gross) ₹1,00,000. Compute tax under both regimes.

Conclusion: the new regime is better by ₹59,800 even though it disallows the HP-loss set-off, because the 87A rebate wipes out the tax on her normal income. (Lottery tax is normally deducted at source u/s 194B, so most of the ₹30,000 would already have been paid.)

Comprehensive problem 3 — reverse problem: the largest zero-tax pay package

An employer wants to design a pay package for a resident employee such that the employee pays no tax under the new regime. It will contribute to NPS 14% of basic salary, and basic salary is 50% of the cash salary. The employee has no other income. Find the maximum cash salary and cost to company (CTC).

  1. Let cash salary = C. Employer NPS = 14% × 0.5C = 0.07C; it is added to salary u/s 17(1)(viii) and deducted u/s 80CCD(2) (within the 14% limit), so it nets out.
  2. Total income = C + 0.07C − 75,000 − 0.07C = C − 75,000.
  3. For nil tax, TI ≤ ₹12,00,000 → C ≤ ₹12,75,000.
  4. Employer NPS = 0.07 × 12,75,000 = ₹89,250. CTC = 12,75,000 + 89,250 = ₹13,64,250.
  5. Check: TI = 12,75,000 + 89,250 − 75,000 − 89,250 = ₹12,00,000 → tax 60,000 − rebate 60,000 = nil.
Tip — presenting Section C

Write the heading, list assumptions (resident, new regime, age), put every figure that needs calculation into a numbered working note, show GTI and TI in bold, then a separate tax table. Close with one line: regime chosen, ITR form, and whether tax is payable or refundable.

5.6 Procedure for e-filing of the return, TDS and advance tax

Intuition

Tax is collected through the year (TDS by payers, advance tax by you), and after the year ends you file a return that says: "Here is my income, here is the tax on it, here is what has already been paid — so I owe this much or you owe me a refund." Since 2013 almost every individual return is filed online on the Income Tax Department's e-filing portal (incometax.gov.in).

Definition

A return of income u/s 139 is the prescribed form (ITR-1 to ITR-7) in which an assessee declares income, deductions, tax computed and taxes paid for a previous year. E-filing is the electronic furnishing of this return on the e-filing portal, followed by verification (electronic or physical) within the prescribed time.

A. Who must file (sec 139(1))

  • An individual whose total income (before exemptions u/s 54–54GB and before Chapter VI-A deductions) exceeds the basic exemption limit (₹4,00,000 under the new regime; ₹2,50,000/3,00,000/5,00,000 under the old regime).
  • Even below that limit, a person who (i) deposited more than ₹1 crore in current accounts, (ii) spent more than ₹2 lakh on foreign travel, (iii) paid electricity bills above ₹1 lakh, or (iv) meets other prescribed conditions (for example, business turnover above ₹60 lakh, professional receipts above ₹10 lakh, TDS/TCS of ₹25,000 or more, or savings-account deposits of ₹50 lakh or more).
  • A resident (other than RNOR) holding any asset or signing authority outside India, or who is a beneficiary of a foreign asset.
  • Filing is advisable even when not compulsory: to claim a refund of TDS, carry forward losses (a loss return must be filed by the due date, except HP loss and unabsorbed depreciation), and for visa/loan documentation.

B. Which ITR form (AY 2026-27)

FormWho uses it
ITR-1 (Sahaj)Resident individual (not RNOR) with total income up to ₹50 lakh from salary/pension, one house property, other sources (not lottery/races) and LTCG u/s 112A up to ₹1.25 lakh; agricultural income up to ₹5,000. Not for directors, holders of unlisted shares or foreign assets.
ITR-2Individuals and HUFs not having business/professional income (e.g., capital gains beyond ITR-1 limits, more than one house, foreign income, RNOR/NR).
ITR-3Individuals and HUFs having income from business or profession (including partners in firms).
ITR-4 (Sugam)Resident individuals, HUFs and firms (other than LLPs) with presumptive income u/s 44AD, 44ADA or 44AE and total income up to ₹50 lakh.
ITR-5 / ITR-6 / ITR-7Firms, LLPs, AOPs, BOIs / companies (other than those claiming sec 11) / trusts, political parties, institutions filing u/s 139(4A)–(4D).

C. Due dates and kinds of return (AY 2026-27)

ReturnSectionTime limitKey point
Original139(1)31 July 2026 for individuals not requiring audit; 31 October 2026 if accounts are audited; 30 November for transfer-pricing cases (CBDT may extend)Needed to carry forward business/capital losses and (for business cases) to exercise the regime option
Belated139(4)By 31 December 2026 (or before completion of assessment, if earlier)Late fee u/s 234F: ₹5,000; ₹1,000 if total income ≤ ₹5 lakh; interest u/s 234A
Revised139(5)By 31 December 2026 (or before completion of assessment)To correct an omission or wrong statement; can be revised more than once
Updated (ITR-U)139(8A)Within 48 months from the end of the AY (Finance Act, 2025 extended it from 24 months)Only to declare more income/pay more tax, with additional tax of 25%, 50%, 60% or 70% of the tax and interest depending on the year of filing
Defective139(9)Within 15 days of the notice (extendable)Otherwise treated as invalid

D. Step-by-step e-filing procedure (the syllabus item)

  1. Register / log in on the e-filing portal using PAN as user ID (PAN must be linked with Aadhaar; an inoperative PAN causes higher TDS and blocks refunds). Pre-validate a bank account for refunds.
  2. Collect and reconcile documents: Form 16 (TDS certificate for salary, issued by the employer by 15 June), Form 16A (non-salary TDS), Form 26AS (tax credit statement), AIS (Annual Information Statement: interest, dividends, securities transactions, rent, etc.) and TIS (Taxpayer Information Summary). Give feedback on AIS if any entry is wrong.
  3. File Income Tax Return: select AY 2026-27, mode (online, or offline utility with JSON upload), status (individual/HUF/others) and the correct ITR form; state the reason for filing.
  4. Choose the regime: the new regime applies by default; tick the old regime option if chosen (a person with business income must have filed Form 10-IEA).
  5. Validate pre-filled data: personal information, salary break-up, house property, capital gains schedules, other sources, exempt income, deductions (if old regime), taxes paid (TDS/TCS/advance tax from 26AS).
  6. Compute tax: the utility shows tax, interest u/s 234A/B/C and fee u/s 234F. If tax is payable, pay it as self-assessment tax u/s 140A through "e-Pay Tax" (Challan No. ITNS 280), and the challan details flow into the return.
  7. Preview and submit; an acknowledgement (ITR-V) is generated.
  8. Verify within 30 days of filing: Aadhaar OTP, EVC through pre-validated bank account/demat account, net-banking, or digital signature certificate (mandatory for audit cases); or send the signed ITR-V by speed post to CPC, Bengaluru. A return not verified in time is treated as not filed; late verification makes the verification date the filing date.
  9. Processing by the Centralised Processing Centre (CPC): an intimation u/s 143(1) shows arithmetic adjustments, demand or refund. The case may later be selected for scrutiny assessment u/s 143(3).
  10. Refund is credited to the pre-validated bank account with interest u/s 244A; a demand must be paid or responded to online.

E. Tax deducted at source and advance tax

  • TDS: the payer deducts tax when paying salary (sec 192, at average rate under the regime chosen by the employee), interest (194A), dividend (194), rent (194-I), professional fees (194J), lottery winnings (194B, 30%) and so on, and deposits it with the Government. The payee claims credit in the return.
  • TCS: the seller collects tax on certain sales (e.g., specified goods, foreign remittances above thresholds).
  • Advance tax (sec 207–211): payable when the estimated tax for the year, after TDS/TCS, is ₹10,000 or more. Resident senior citizens without business income are exempt. Instalments: 15% by 15 June, 45% by 15 September, 75% by 15 December, 100% by 15 March. Presumptive taxpayers u/s 44AD/44ADA may pay 100% by 15 March.
  • Interest: sec 234A (late filing), 234B (advance tax paid less than 90% of assessed tax), 234C (deferment of instalments), generally at 1% per month or part of a month.
Worked example 1 — advance tax schedule (easy)

Ms Nidhi, a consultant (not under 44ADA), estimates tax for PY 2025-26 at ₹2,10,000; TDS by clients ₹30,000. Prepare her advance-tax schedule.

  1. Net tax = 2,10,000 − 30,000 = ₹1,80,000 (≥ ₹10,000, so advance tax applies).
  2. By 15-6-2025: 15% = ₹27,000.
  3. By 15-9-2025: 45% = ₹81,000 cumulative → instalment ₹54,000.
  4. By 15-12-2025: 75% = ₹1,35,000 cumulative → instalment ₹54,000.
  5. By 15-3-2026: 100% = ₹1,80,000 cumulative → instalment ₹45,000.
Worked example 2 — late fee and choice of form (medium)

(a) Mr Om (salary only, TI ₹4,80,000) files on 20 September 2026 without any extension of the due date. (b) Ms Ruth (salary ₹14 lakh and STCG on sale of land ₹3 lakh) files on 10 July 2026. State the fee and the ITR form.

  1. (a) Belated return u/s 139(4). Total income does not exceed ₹5 lakh, so fee u/s 234F = ₹1,000. His tax is nil after rebate, so no interest u/s 234A arises. Form: ITR-1.
  2. (b) Filed before 31 July 2026: no fee. She has capital gains (other than 112A up to ₹1.25 lakh) and no business income → ITR-2.
Indian example

The CBDT's Centralised Processing Centre at Bengaluru processes returns filed online and issues intimations u/s 143(1); the Annual Information Statement (AIS), introduced in 2021, pulls data from banks, registrars, mutual funds and employers so that the department can match your return with third-party reports.

Course vs industry

From tax year 2026-27 the Income-tax Act, 2025 re-numbers these provisions (returns, TDS, advance tax) and speaks of "tax year" instead of PY/AY. The procedure on the portal remains the same. For AY 2026-27 returns (filed in 2026), quote the 1961 Act sections used above.

Self-check
1. Within how many days must an e-filed return be verified?

30 days from the date of filing.

2. A salaried individual has one house property and LTCG u/s 112A of ₹90,000. Which form?

ITR-1 (Sahaj), provided TI ≤ ₹50 lakh and other conditions are met (112A gain up to ₹1.25 lakh is allowed in ITR-1 from AY 2025-26).

3. Can an updated return be used to claim a bigger refund?

No. Sec 139(8A) returns can only increase income/tax; they cannot reduce tax or increase a refund.

5.7 Unit V exam kit

Likely questions: Explain the procedure for e-filing (10) · Deductions allowed under the new regime (5) · Old vs new regime — factors and computation (10/15) · Rebate u/s 87A and marginal relief (5) · Comprehensive computation of total income and tax (15, Section C) · Types of returns u/s 139 (5) · Advance tax instalments (5).

Model answer (10 marks) — "Explain the procedure for e-filing of income-tax returns"

Introduction. Sec 139 requires every person whose income exceeds the basic exemption limit (or who meets prescribed conditions) to file a return. Individuals file electronically on the Income Tax Department's e-filing portal; the return is processed centrally at CPC, Bengaluru.

1. Pre-requisites. PAN linked with Aadhaar, registration on the portal, pre-validated bank account.

2. Documents. Form 16/16A, Form 26AS, AIS/TIS, capital-gains statements, rent receipts and investment proofs (old regime).

3. Selection. AY 2026-27, filing status, correct ITR (ITR-1 for simple salary cases; ITR-2 with capital gains; ITR-3 with business; ITR-4 for presumptive), regime (new by default; Form 10-IEA for business cases opting out).

4. Filling and validating. Pre-filled data is checked head by head; exempt income and deductions entered; taxes paid auto-populated from 26AS.

5. Tax computation and payment. Balance tax with interest (234A/B/C) and fee (234F) is paid as self-assessment tax u/s 140A through e-Pay Tax.

6. Submission and verification. Submit, then e-verify within 30 days using Aadhaar OTP, EVC, net-banking or DSC, or post the signed ITR-V to CPC.

7. Processing and after. Intimation u/s 143(1); refund with interest u/s 244A or demand; possible scrutiny u/s 143(3); belated/revised return by 31 December; updated return within 48 months.

Illustration. A salaried employee with salary of ₹15 lakh and FD interest files ITR-1 by 31 July 2026, pays the small balance tax on interest through e-Pay Tax, and e-verifies with Aadhaar OTP the same day.

Conclusion. E-filing, pre-filled forms and AIS matching have made compliance faster and more transparent; the taxpayer's main duties are choosing the right form and regime, reconciling AIS, and verifying on time.

Model answer (5 marks) — "Which deductions are allowed from GTI under the new tax regime?"

Meaning. Sec 115BAC offers lower slab rates on condition that most exemptions and Chapter VI-A deductions are given up.

Allowed from GTI: (1) 80CCD(2) — employer's NPS contribution up to 14% of salary; (2) 80CCH — contributions to the Agniveer Corpus Fund; (3) 80JJAA — 30% of additional employee cost for eligible businesses.

Allowed while computing heads: standard deduction ₹75,000; family-pension deduction ₹25,000; sec 24(b) interest on let-out property; gratuity/commuted pension/leave-encashment exemptions; capital gains exemptions 54–54F.

Not allowed: 80C, 80D, 80E, 80G, 80TTA/TTB, HRA, LTC, professional tax, SOP interest.

Conclusion. Hence the new regime suits taxpayers with few deductions; with total income up to ₹12 lakh, rebate u/s 87A makes tax nil.

Model answer (5 marks) — "Explain rebate u/s 87A with marginal relief"

Meaning. Sec 87A gives a resident individual a rebate of tax where total income does not exceed a threshold.

New regime (AY 2026-27): TI ≤ ₹12,00,000 → rebate up to ₹60,000 (i.e., the whole slab tax). Just above ₹12 lakh, marginal relief limits tax to the income exceeding ₹12 lakh. Old regime: TI ≤ ₹5,00,000 → rebate up to ₹12,500.

Limits: not for non-residents, HUFs or firms; not against tax on special-rate incomes such as sec 111A/112A capital gains; granted before cess.

Illustration: TI ₹12,20,000 → slab tax ₹63,000 → marginal relief ₹43,000 → tax ₹20,000 + cess ₹800 = ₹20,800.

Self-check (2-mark style)
1. Define gross total income.

Aggregate of income under the five heads after clubbing and set-off of losses, before Chapter VI-A deductions [sec 80B(5)].

2. What is sec 288B?

Tax payable or refundable is rounded off to the nearest multiple of ₹10.

3. What is ITR-V?

The acknowledgement/verification form generated after e-filing; it is signed and posted to CPC only if the return is not e-verified.

Practice & Revision — whole-subject sections

Last year's ESE paper (October 2025) — every question solved

This is the actual end-semester paper from October 2025 (course code BBA301-5, 50 marks, 2 hours). Each question is solved exactly as that paper asked (AY 2025-26), followed by what would change if the same question appeared in your paper (AY 2026-27, new regime).

Read this before practising
  1. Pattern: Section A 3 × 5 = 15 (each with an internal OR), Section B 2 × 10 = 20 (internal OR), Section C 1 × 15 = 15 (compulsory case, split 10 + 3 + 2). Same as your course plan.
  2. Out of your syllabus: Q3(a) GST reverse charge and Q3(b) customs waters were set on last year's CO5 (GST and Customs). Your 2026 syllabus has no GST or customs unit, so expect Q3 this year to come from one of your five units (likely exempt incomes, capital vs revenue, e-filing or residential status). Brief answers are kept below, collapsed, only for completeness.
  3. Year change: the paper uses AY 2025-26 figures (new-regime slabs 0–3L nil; 87A rebate up to ₹7 lakh, max ₹25,000). Your paper is AY 2026-27: slabs 0–4L nil, 4–8L 5%, 8–12L 10%, 12–16L 15%, 16–20L 20%, 20–24L 25%, above 24L 30%; 87A rebate up to ₹12 lakh, max ₹60,000, not available on special-rate income; TDS on dividends only above ₹10,000.
  4. Almost every computation question says "new tax regime" (Q2, Q4(b), Q5(b), Q6; Q1(b) and Q4(a) are regime-neutral). Use the new regime unless a question explicitly says otherwise.
  5. Typo in the paper: Q1(b) says "A.Y. 2024-25" but retirement on 15 Dec 2024 falls in PY 2024-25, i.e. AY 2025-26. Point this out in one line and compute for AY 2025-26.

Q1(a) Residential status — Mr Khali (5 marks)

Question. PY 2024-25. Mr Khali left India for the first time on 15-09-2021 and came back on 01-09-2023. He left for Dubai on 15-06-2024 and came back on 14-02-2025 to settle in India. He claims he is an NRI. Do you agree?

Solution

Step 1 — days in India in PY 2024-25 (arrival and departure days both counted):

PeriodDays
01-04-2024 to 15-06-202476
14-02-2025 to 31-03-202546
Total122

Step 2 — basic conditions u/s 6(1): (a) 182 days or more? 122 — No. (b) 60 days or more in the PY and 365 days or more in the 4 preceding years?

Preceding PYDays in India
2020-21 (in India all year)365
2021-22 (till 15-09-2021)168
2022-23 (abroad)0
2023-24 (from 01-09-2023)213
Total746

122 ≥ 60 and 746 ≥ 365 → condition (b) is satisfied → Resident.

Step 3 — additional conditions u/s 6(6): he lived in India until September 2021, so he was resident in at least 2 of the 10 preceding years, and he was in India for well over 730 days in the 7 preceding years. Both satisfied → Resident and Ordinarily Resident (ROR).

Conclusion. His argument is wrong. As an ROR he is taxed in India on his global income, including income earned in Dubai. Line to add: the 60-day condition is replaced by 182 days only for an Indian citizen who leaves India for employment abroad (or as ship crew) in that year; the question does not say he left for employment, so the 60-day rule applies. If he had left for employment, he would be a Non-Resident for PY 2024-25. The other 182-day exception (an Indian citizen or PIO coming on a visit to India) also does not help: he was already living in India from 01-09-2023 and returned in February 2025 to settle, not to visit.

Q1(b) Taxable gratuity — Mr Ganguly (5 marks)

Question. Retired 15-12-2024 after 28 years 8 months; gratuity received ₹23,50,000; not covered by the Payment of Gratuity Act. Average basic salary for the 10 months ending 30-11-2024 ₹78,200 p.m.; DA ₹1,000 p.m. (80% forms part of salary for retirement benefits); commission 6% of turnover achieved by him, turnover for those 10 months ₹1,50,000.

Solution

Average salary (basic + DA forming part + commission at a fixed % of turnover) for the 10 months preceding the month of retirement:

Component₹ per month
Basic salary78,200
DA forming part (80% × 1,000)800
Commission (6% × 1,50,000 ÷ 10)900
Average salary79,900

Exempt u/s 10(10)(iii) = least of:

Limit₹
Statutory limit20,00,000
Actual gratuity received23,50,000
½ × average salary × completed years = ½ × 79,900 × 28 (the 8 months are ignored)11,18,600
Exempt (least)11,18,600

Taxable gratuity = 23,50,000 − 11,18,600 = ₹12,31,400 (AY 2025-26; the paper's "AY 2024-25" is a typo).

Same answer in AY 2026-27: the rule and the ₹20 lakh limit have not changed, and the exemption is available under the new regime.

Q2(a) Income from house property — Mr Rahul (5 marks)

Question. Two equal units: Unit I self-occupied, Unit II let out at ₹16,000 p.m. (2 months' rent unrealised). For the whole property: municipal value ₹2,30,000, standard rent ₹2,25,000, fair rent ₹2,40,000. Municipal tax 10%, paid half by Rahul and half by the tenant. Repairs ₹11,600, insurance ₹22,500, interest on capital for construction ₹83,000. New regime, AY 2025-26.

Solution

Split everything equally between the two units.

Unit II (let out)₹
Municipal value (½)1,15,000
Fair rent (½)1,20,000
Standard rent (½)1,12,500
Expected rent = higher of MV and FR, limited to SR1,12,500
Actual rent receivable (16,000 × 12) less unrealised rent (32,000)1,60,000
GAV = higher of expected rent and actual rent1,60,000
Less: municipal tax paid by owner (10% × 1,15,000 × ½)(5,750)
NAV1,54,250
Less: standard deduction 30% u/s 24(a)(46,275)
Less: interest on borrowed capital u/s 24(b) (½ × 83,000)(41,500)
Income from Unit II66,475

Unit I (self-occupied): annual value nil. Under the new regime no interest deduction is allowed for a self-occupied property, so income = nil (and no loss is created).

Income from house property = ₹66,475.

Points that earn marks: repairs and insurance are not deductible (the 30% standard deduction replaces all actual expenses); the tenant's half of municipal tax is ignored (alternative reading: if "half paid by Rahul" means he pays all of Unit I's tax and the tenant all of Unit II's, no municipal tax is deductible for Unit II and income = 1,60,000 − 48,000 − 41,500 = ₹70,500); unrealised rent is deducted from actual rent under Rule 4. The paper says "interest on capital" — treat it as interest on borrowed capital and say so; interest on the owner's own capital is never deductible. Same answer in AY 2026-27.

Q2(b) Income from profession — Dr Gupta (5 marks)

Solution
Particulars₹₹
Receipts: dispensary 2,35,000 + consultation 1,65,000 + operation fees 2,50,000 + visiting fees 50,000 + gifts from patients 30,0007,30,000
Less: cost of medicines consumed (purchased 1,25,000 − closing stock 35,000)90,000
Salaries to employees1,50,000
Medical seminar in Germany (to update professional knowledge)25,000
House, ½ used for profession: municipal tax (10% × 50,000 × ½) 2,500 + repairs (10,000 × ½) 5,0007,500
Depreciation: surgical equipment 48,000 × 15%7,200
Depreciation: medical books 30,000 × 40% (rate given)12,000(2,91,700)
Income from profession4,38,300

Surgical equipment is capital expenditure — only depreciation is allowed (15%, plant and machinery; state the rate you assume, and that the equipment and books were used for 180 days or more, otherwise half the rate). Gifts from patients are professional receipts. The residential half of the house is self-occupied, so its annual value is nil.

Line to add: gross receipts are below the sec 44ADA limit, so Dr Gupta could instead declare 50% of receipts (₹3,65,000) as presumptive income.

Income from profession = ₹4,38,300.

Q3(a) and 3(b) — GST and customs NOT IN YOUR 2026 SYLLABUS

Brief answers (only if you have spare time; not in your 2026 syllabus)
3(a) Reverse charge

Yes. Normally the supplier pays GST (forward charge), but under the reverse charge mechanism (sec 9(3)/9(4) CGST Act; sec 5(3) IGST Act) the recipient pays it. Examples: legal services by an advocate to a business entity; goods transport agency services to a registered business; sponsorship services to a body corporate. The recipient pays in cash and can then claim input tax credit. Due dates (monthly filer): GSTR-1 by the 11th of the next month; GSTR-3B and payment by the 20th; annual return GSTR-9 by 31 December after the year. Quarterly (QRMP) filers file GSTR-3B by the 22nd/24th after the quarter.

3(b) Territorial and customs waters

Territorial waters: the sea up to 12 nautical miles from the baseline; India's sovereignty extends to them, and "India" includes them (sec 2(27)), so import begins when goods enter Indian territorial waters (the taxable event for duty is when they cross the customs barrier, Garden Silk Mills). Customs waters (sec 2(28) Customs Act): territorial waters plus the contiguous zone, up to 24 nautical miles. Significance: India's power to levy customs duty extends to goods brought into its territorial waters, and customs officers can stop, board and search vessels and seize goods anywhere in customs waters.

Q4(a) Capital gains with sec 54 and 54EC — Ramesh (10 marks)

Question. Ramesh (66) sold a house in Pune on 01-10-2024 for ₹20,00,000; brokerage 1%. His father bought it on 01-01-1999 for ₹1,00,000; Ramesh inherited it on 05-07-2006. FMV on 01-04-2001: ₹1,40,000. He bought a house in Baramati on 07-03-2025 for ₹8,00,000 and REC bonds of ₹3,00,000 (redeemable after 3 years) on 20-04-2025. Taxable capital gain, AY 2025-26.

Solution
  1. Nature: inherited asset — the father's holding period counts (sec 2(42A)); held since 1999, so it is a long-term capital asset.
  2. Cost of acquisition: cost to the previous owner (sec 49(1)); acquired before 01-04-2001, so take the higher of actual cost ₹1,00,000 and FMV on 01-04-2001 ₹1,40,000 = ₹1,40,000.
  3. Rate: transfer after 23-07-2024 → LTCG taxed at 12.5% without indexation (sec 112). A resident individual transferring land or a building acquired before 23-07-2024 may instead pay 20% with indexation, whichever tax is lower. Compute both.
Particulars12.5% without indexation20% with indexation
Full value of consideration20,00,00020,00,000
Less: brokerage 1%(20,000)(20,000)
Net consideration19,80,00019,80,000
Less: cost / indexed cost (1,40,000 × 363 ÷ 100)(1,40,000)(5,08,200)
LTCG18,40,00014,71,800
Less: sec 54 — new house within 1 year before / 2 years after transfer(8,00,000)(8,00,000)
Less: sec 54EC — REC bondsNilNil
Taxable LTCG10,40,0006,71,800

Tax: 10,40,000 × 12.5% = ₹1,30,000 against 6,71,800 × 20% = ₹1,34,360. Choose 12.5% without indexation: taxable LTCG = ₹10,40,000, tax ₹1,30,000 (plus cess).

Why 54EC gives nothing (the trap in this question): (i) the bonds must be bought within 6 months of the transfer, i.e. by 31-03-2025 — 20-04-2025 is too late; (ii) 54EC bonds must be redeemable after 5 years; bonds redeemable after 3 years do not qualify.

Indexation base for inherited property: the solution indexes from 2001-02 (CII 100), the year from which the FMV option applies, following the view that indexation runs from when the previous owner held it (Bombay High Court, Manjula J. Shah). The literal reading uses the year the assessee first held it (2006-07, CII 122): indexed cost 4,16,557, LTCG 15,63,443, taxable 7,63,443 at 20% = ₹1,52,689. The 12.5% option is still cheaper, so the answer does not change. Age 66 makes no difference to the capital-gains computation. Alternative if you compare the actual tax: if Ramesh has no other income, the unused basic exemption of ₹3,00,000 is first set off against LTCG (sec 112): (10,40,000 − 3,00,000) × 12.5% = ₹92,500 against (6,71,800 − 3,00,000) × 20% = ₹74,360, so the 20% indexed option gives lower tax. Say that the choice depends on his other income. Same law in AY 2026-27; for a sale in PY 2025-26 use CII 376.

AY 2026-27 change: the dividend TDS threshold is now ₹10,000, so ₹8,970 would suffer no TDS and would be taken as received (8,970, not 9,967).

Q6 Section C case — Mrs Naina, total income and tax under the new regime (15 marks)

Question (summary). Mrs Naina turns 65 on 26-10-2024. PY 2024-25: pension ₹15,000 p.m.; dividend RIL ₹14,000; dividend Microsoft ₹70,000; agricultural income from Nepal ₹56,000; bank FD interest ₹80,000; lottery (net) ₹35,000 after 30% TDS; interest on Govt securities ₹50,000; cash gift ₹51,000 from a friend on her 35th wedding anniversary; diamond necklace ₹2 lakh from her sister in Dubai; FD with interest ₹51,000 assigned to her by a friend at her daughter's wedding; property worth ₹35 lakh under her uncle's will. Payments: LIC ₹24,000, mediclaim for son ₹6,000, ₹20,000 to a notified charity, ₹15,000 to PM CARES, ₹30,000 cancer treatment of her father (88).

(a) Statement of taxable income, new regime (10 marks)
Particulars₹₹
Salaries: pension 15,000 × 121,80,000
Less: standard deduction u/s 16(ia) (new regime, AY 2025-26)(75,000)1,05,000
Income from other sources
Dividend — RIL14,000
Dividend — Microsoft (foreign company)70,000
Agricultural income from Nepal (foreign land: taxable)56,000
Interest on bank FD (80TTB not allowed in new regime)80,000
Lottery: 35,000 ÷ 0.70 (grossed up)50,000
Interest on Govt securities50,000
Cash gift from friend (wedding anniversary is not marriage)51,000
FD assigned by friend at daughter's wedding (only the individual's own marriage is exempt)51,000
Necklace from sister (relative) — exemptNil
Property under uncle's will — exempt (inheritance)Nil4,22,000
Gross total income5,27,000

Less: deductions under Chapter VI-A — nil. 80C (LIC), 80D (mediclaim), 80G (charity, PM CARES) and 80DDB (cancer treatment) are not available under the new regime. Total income = ₹5,27,000 (of which ₹50,000 is lottery income at a special rate).

Gifts from non-relatives are aggregated: 51,000 + 51,000 = 1,02,000 > 50,000, so the whole amount is taxable.

(b) Tax liability (3 marks)
Particulars₹
Tax on normal income 4,77,000: 5% × (4,77,000 − 3,00,000)8,850
Less: rebate u/s 87A (total income ≤ ₹7 lakh; max ₹25,000), on normal-income tax(8,850)
Tax on lottery 50,000 × 30% (sec 115BB)15,000
Add: health and education cess 4%600
Tax liability15,600
Less: TDS on lottery(15,000)
Tax payable600

State the assumption: the rebate is applied only to tax on normal income, not to the special-rate lottery tax. For AY 2025-26 this was debated; if rebate is allowed on the lottery tax too, the whole ₹23,850 is covered up to ₹25,000, tax liability is nil and the ₹15,000 TDS is refunded. For AY 2026-27 the law now says the rebate is not available on special-rate income.

AY 2026-27 version: same total income; tax on 4,77,000 = 5% × 77,000 = 3,850, fully rebated (limit ₹12 lakh); lottery 15,000 + cess 600 = ₹15,600; payable after TDS ₹600.

(c) Tax planning under the new regime (2 marks)

The belief is only partly true: most exemptions and deductions are gone, but people still choose the new regime because its lower slabs and higher rebate (no tax up to ₹12 lakh from AY 2026-27; ₹12.75 lakh for salaried with standard deduction) often leave them paying less tax, without locking money into specified investments. Ways to save tax under the new regime:

  • Employer's contribution to NPS u/s 80CCD(2) (up to 14% of salary) and the Agniveer corpus u/s 80CCH.
  • Standard deduction of ₹75,000 for salary and pension; family pension deduction.
  • Tax-free perquisites and reimbursements that remain allowed (e.g. official travel, conveyance for official duty).
  • Capital-gains exemptions u/s 54, 54EC, 54F are still available; gifts from relatives are exempt.
  • Choosing income timing and the regime itself each year (salaried taxpayers can switch every year).
What this paper teaches about your exam
  • Almost every computation says "new tax regime" — drill new-regime rules: no HRA, no 80C/80D/80G, no SOP interest, standard deduction ₹75,000.
  • Traps are planted in the facts: bonds bought too late, 3-year bonds, gifts at a daughter's wedding, a sister vs a friend, net figures that need grossing up, foreign agricultural income.
  • Section B is 10 marks each — show a full statement with a reason next to every add-back; reasons earn most of the marks.
  • Section C always ends with a short theory part on the new regime — learn the "why choose the new regime" answer.
