Your "Basic concepts" handout contains theory (capital vs revenue receipts, income, gifts) rather than numerical questions; its content is taught in 1.6, 1.7 and 4.6.

Master formula and process sheet

TopicFormula / ruleVariables & when to use
Residential status (sec 6(1))Resident if (a) ≥ 182 days in PY, or (b) ≥ 60 days in PY and ≥ 365 days in 4 preceding PYsCount arrival and departure days as days in India. For Indian citizens leaving for employment/crew and for Indian citizens/PIOs visiting India, (b) uses 182 days (120 days for visitors with Indian income > ₹15 lakh).
ROR vs RNOR (sec 6(6))ROR if resident in ≥ 2 of 10 preceding PYs and ≥ 730 days in 7 preceding PYs; otherwise RNORAlso RNOR: 120-day visitor and deemed resident u/s 6(1A).
Scope (sec 5)ROR: all income worldwide. RNOR: Indian income + foreign income from business controlled/profession set up in India. NR: Indian income only"Indian income" = received or deemed received in India, or accrues/arises or is deemed to accrue in India.
Agricultural income — partial integrationTax = T(non-agri + agri) − T(agri + basic exemption limit)When agri income > ₹5,000 and non-agri income > basic exemption limit.
Composite income (Rules 7A/7B/8)Rubber 35% business / 65% agri; coffee (grown and cured) 25/75; coffee (grown, cured, roasted, ground) 40/60; tea 40/60Business portion taxable as PGBP.
Salary (gross)Basic + DA + taxable allowances + taxable perquisites + taxable retirement benefits + employer NPS + excess employer PF contribution/interestDue basis or receipt basis, whichever earlier.
Net salaryGross salary − 16(ia) standard deduction (₹75,000 new / ₹50,000 old) − 16(ii) entertainment (Govt, old) − 16(iii) professional tax (old)Standard deduction = lower of salary and the limit.
HRA exempt (old)Least of: HRA received; rent paid − 10% of salary; 50% (Mumbai, Delhi, Kolkata, Chennai) or 40% of salarySalary = basic + DA (retirement) + turnover commission, for the period of occupation.
Gratuity — covered by PG ActLeast of: ₹20 lakh; actual; 15/26 × last drawn salary × completed years (part > 6 months = 1 year)Salary = basic + DA.
Gratuity — not coveredLeast of: ₹20 lakh; actual; ½ × average salary of 10 months before the month of retirement × completed years (fraction ignored)Salary = basic + DA (retirement) + turnover commission. Government employees: fully exempt.
Commuted pensionWith gratuity: 1/3 of full commuted value exempt; without gratuity: 1/2 exempt. Full value = amount received ÷ % commutedGovernment employees: fully exempt. Uncommuted pension: fully taxable.
Leave encashment on retirement (non-Govt)Least of: ₹25 lakh; actual; 10 × average monthly salary; cash equivalent of unavailed leave (max 30 days per completed year)Average of 10 months preceding retirement. Encashment during service is fully taxable.
RFA (non-Govt), owned by employerPopulation > 40 lakh: 10% of salary; 15–40 lakh: 7.5%; ≤ 15 lakh: 5% (2011 census) − rent recoveredLeased: lower of lease rent and 10% of salary. Furnished: add 10% p.a. of cost of furniture or hire charges.
Motor car (partly personal, employer bears expenses)₹1,800 p.m. (≤ 1.6 litres) / ₹2,400 p.m. (> 1.6 litres) + ₹900 p.m. for chauffeurWholly personal: actual expenses + 10% of cost; wholly official: nil.
Interest-free loan perquisiteSBI lending rate on 1 April × maximum monthly outstanding − interest recoveredNil if aggregate loans ≤ ₹20,000 or loan for specified diseases.
RPFEmployer contribution above 12% of salary taxable; interest above 9.5% taxableAggregate employer PF + NPS + superannuation above ₹7.5 lakh taxable u/s 17(2)(vii).
GAV (let-out)Expected rent = max(MV, FRV) capped at SR; GAV = higher of ER and actual rent received/receivable (after unrealised rent); if ER > actual only because of vacancy, GAV = actualMV = municipal value, FRV = fair rent, SR = standard rent.
Income from HPNAV = GAV − municipal tax paid by owner; income = NAV − 30% of NAV − interest u/s 24(b)SOP: GAV nil; interest capped at ₹2 lakh (old regime only). Pre-construction interest: 1/5 per year for 5 years.
Arrears/unrealised rent recovered (sec 25A)Taxable = amount received − 30% of itTaxable even if the owner no longer owns the property.
Business incomeNet profit per P&L + inadmissible expenses debited + income taxable not credited − admissible expenses not debited − incomes credited but exempt or taxable under other heads40(a)(ia) 30%, 40A(2) excess, 40A(3) cash > ₹10,000 (₹35,000 transporters), 43B on payment.
Depreciation (block, WDV)Opening WDV + additions − sale proceeds; full rate if used ≥ 180 days, half rate if < 180 days in year of acquisitionBlock ceases or negative → STCG u/s 50.
Presumptive income44AD: 8% (6% digital) of turnover ≤ ₹2 crore (₹3 crore if cash receipts ≤ 5%); 44ADA: 50% of receipts ≤ ₹50 lakh (₹75 lakh); 44AE: ₹1,000 per ton per month (heavy) / ₹7,500 per month (others)No further deductions; books need not be kept.
Capital gainsFull value of consideration − transfer expenses − cost of acquisition − cost of improvementIndexed cost = cost × CII of year of transfer ÷ CII of year of acquisition (only for the 20% option on land/building acquired before 23-7-2024). CII 2025-26 = 376.
Holding period (transfers from 23-7-2024)Listed securities / equity MF units: > 12 months = long-term; all other assets: > 24 monthsSpecified MF/market-linked debentures: always short-term (sec 50AA).
CG rates111A: 20%; 112A: 12.5% above ₹1.25 lakh; 112: 12.5% (or 20% indexed option for land/building acquired before 23-7-2024)Transfers before 23-7-2024: 15%/10% above ₹1 lakh/20% indexed.
Sec 54 / 54F54: exempt = min(LTCG, cost of new house) (cost cap ₹10 crore). 54F: exempt = LTCG × cost of new house ÷ net consideration54 for residential house sold; 54F for any other long-term asset.
Sec 54EC / 54B54EC: min(LTCG, bonds bought within 6 months), max ₹50 lakh. 54B: min(CG, cost of new agricultural land within 2 years)54EC: LTCG on land/building only.
Gifts (sec 56(2)(x))Money: whole aggregate taxable if > ₹50,000; immovable property: stamp duty value (if difference > higher of ₹50,000 and 10% of consideration); movable property: FMV if > ₹50,000Exempt: relatives, marriage, will/inheritance, contemplation of death.
Family pension (sec 57(iia))Deduction = lower of 1/3 of family pension and ₹25,000 (new) / ₹15,000 (old)Taxed under other sources.
Casual income (115BB)Gross winnings × 30% (+ cess); grossing up: net ÷ 0.70No expense, no deduction, no basic exemption, no loss set-off.
GTI and TIGTI = five heads after clubbing and set-off; TI = GTI − Chapter VI-A; round to ₹10Chapter VI-A not against special-rate income.
New-regime tax0–4L nil; 4–8L 5%; 8–12L 10%; 12–16L 15%; 16–20L 20%; 20–24L 25%; > 24L 30%Quick formula above 24L: 3,00,000 + 30% × (TI − 24,00,000).
87A (new)TI ≤ 12L: rebate = min(slab tax, 60,000). TI > 12L: tax = min(slab tax, TI − 12,00,000)Residents only; not against special-rate tax.
Surcharge & cess10% (> 50L), 15% (> 1 cr), 25% (> 2 cr; new-regime cap); cess 4% of (tax + surcharge)Marginal relief: (tax + SC) ≤ tax at threshold + income above threshold.
Average rate of taxTax payable ÷ total income × 100Also used for relief u/s 89 and TDS u/s 192.
Advance tax15% / 45% / 75% / 100% by 15 June / Sept / Dec / MarchIf net tax ≥ ₹10,000.

Analytics angle — doing tax computations in Excel, Power BI, R, Jamovi and Orange3

Your CIA-3 asks you to build a realistic tax-consulting case and your CIA-1 asks you to analyse a perception survey. Spreadsheets and simple analytics tools make both faster and error-free.

Excel — a one-sheet new-regime tax calculator

CellLabelFormula
B2Gross salary(input)
B3Salary income=MAX(0,B2-MIN(75000,B2))
B4Other normal income (HP + PGBP + other STCG + IFOS)(input)
B5STCG u/s 111A(input)
B6LTCG u/s 112A(input)
B780CCD(2)=MIN(employer_NPS, 14%*basic_plus_DA)
B8Total income (rounded)=MROUND(B3+B4+B5+B6-B7,10)
B9Normal income=B8-B5-B6
B10Slab tax=SUMPRODUCT((B9>{400000;800000;1200000;1600000;2000000;2400000})*(B9-{400000;800000;1200000;1600000;2000000;2400000})*{0.05;0.05;0.05;0.05;0.05;0.05})
B11Special-rate tax=B5*20%+MAX(0,B6-125000)*12.5%
B12Slab tax after 87A / marginal relief=IF(B8<=1200000,0,MIN(B10,B8-1200000))
B13Total tax with cess=ROUND((B12+B11)*1.04,0)

Why the SUMPRODUCT works: the new-regime rates rise by exactly 5 percentage points at every threshold (0→5→10→15→20→25→30), so the tax equals the sum of 5% of the income above each threshold. For the old regime use differential rates {0.05;0.15;0.10} with thresholds {250000;500000;1000000}. Surcharge can be added with =IF(B8>5000000,10%,0) style nested IFs, plus a MIN for marginal relief.

Other Excel helpers:

  • Days in India for residential status: with arrival date in A2 and departure date in B2, =B2-A2+1; days falling inside PY 2025-26: =MAX(0,MIN(B2,DATE(2026,3,31))-MAX(A2,DATE(2025,4,1))+1); status: =IF(OR(C2>=182,AND(C2>=60,D2>=365)),"Resident","Non-resident").
  • HRA exemption: =MIN(HRA, MAX(0,Rent-10%*Salary), IF(Metro="Y",50%,40%)*Salary).
  • Gratuity (covered): =MIN(2000000, Actual, 15/26*Salary*ROUND(Years+Months/12-0.0001,0)) (or compute completed years with an IF on months > 6).
  • Indexed cost: =Cost*VLOOKUP(2025,CII_table,2,0)/VLOOKUP(AcqYear,CII_table,2,0); choose the lower tax with =MIN(OptionA_tax,OptionB_tax).
  • Regime comparison: build both columns and use =IF(Old_tax<New_tax,"Opt for old regime","Stay in new regime"); Data → What-If Analysis → Goal Seek to find break-even deductions.

Power BI (for a tax-perception survey or a client portfolio)

Load the survey table (respondent, occupation, income band, regime chosen, awareness score). DAX measures:

New Regime Share = DIVIDE(CALCULATE(COUNTROWS(Survey), Survey[Regime] = "New"), COUNTROWS(Survey))
Avg Awareness = AVERAGE(Survey[AwarenessScore])
SlabTax = VAR ti = SELECTEDVALUE(Clients[TotalIncome])
          RETURN SUMX({400000,800000,1200000,1600000,2000000,2400000}, MAX(0, ti - [Value]) * 0.05)

Visuals: stacked bar of regime choice by occupation; card for share filing on time; slicer for income band.

R — functions you can reuse

slab_new <- function(ti) sum(pmax(0, ti - c(4,8,12,16,20,24)*1e5) * 0.05)
tax_new <- function(normal, stcg111A = 0, ltcg112A = 0) {
  ti <- normal + stcg111A + ltcg112A
  t  <- slab_new(normal)
  t  <- if (ti <= 12e5) 0 else min(t, ti - 12e5)
  sp <- 0.20 * stcg111A + 0.125 * max(0, ltcg112A - 125000)
  round((t + sp) * 1.04)
}
tax_new(2242400, 80000, 275000)   # Arjun Mehta: 307164
sapply(c(1200000, 1220000, 1800000), tax_new)   # 0, 20800, 166400

Jamovi and Orange3

  • Jamovi (for the CIA-1 perception survey): import the questionnaire responses; Exploration → Descriptives for awareness scores; Frequencies → Contingency Tables (chi-square) to test whether regime choice depends on occupation; ANOVA to compare perceived fairness across income groups.
  • Orange3: File widget → Data Table → Distributions (income bands) → Box Plot (fairness score by occupation); a Tree or Logistic Regression widget can show which factors (income, deductions, business income) predict choosing the old regime.
Tip

In the written exam you cannot use Excel, but the SUMPRODUCT idea gives you a mental shortcut: new-regime tax = 5% of the income above ₹4L + 5% above ₹8L + 5% above ₹12L + … For ₹18 lakh: 5% × (14 + 10 + 6 + 2) lakh = 5% × 32 lakh = ₹1,60,000.

Question bank A — 30 MCQs (answer + why each wrong option is wrong)

Your CIA-1B is a 20-question MCQ test on Units I–II; these cover all five units. All answers are for AY 2026-27, new regime unless stated.

1. Income earned in PY 2025-26 is assessed in: (a) AY 2025-26 (b) AY 2026-27 (c) AY 2024-25 (d) the same year

(b) AY 2026-27.

(a) is the AY for PY 2024-25. (c) is two years earlier. (d) applies only to exceptions (e.g., a person leaving India permanently, discontinued business) — not the general rule.

2. Which is a direct tax? (a) GST (b) Customs duty (c) Income tax (d) Excise duty

(c) Income tax.

GST, customs and excise are indirect: the burden is shifted to the buyer, so impact and incidence fall on different persons.

3. "Person" u/s 2(31) does NOT include: (a) HUF (b) Firm (c) Local authority (d) A minor's guardian as a separate class

(d)

(a), (b), (c) are among the seven categories (individual, HUF, company, firm, AOP/BOI, local authority, artificial juridical person). A guardian is not a separate class; he may be a "representative assessee".

4. Agricultural income from land in India is: (a) fully taxable (b) exempt u/s 10(1) but used for rate purposes (c) taxable under PGBP (d) taxable only for RNOR

(b)

(a) contradicts sec 10(1). (c) applies only to the business portion of composite income (tea, coffee, rubber). (d) confuses it with foreign agricultural income, which is taxable for ROR.

5. An individual stays in India 70 days in PY 2025-26 and 400 days in the 4 preceding years; he is a foreign national. Status? (a) NR (b) Resident (c) Deemed resident (d) Cannot be determined

(b) Resident (condition 60 + 365 satisfied).

(a) ignores condition (b). (c) deemed residence u/s 6(1A) applies to Indian citizens not liable to tax elsewhere. (d) the data is sufficient; ROR/RNOR would need further data but residence is settled.

6. For an RNOR, which income is NOT taxable? (a) Salary received in India (b) Income from a business in London controlled from India (c) Rent from a London house received in London (d) Interest on an Indian bank deposit

(c)

(a) and (d) are Indian incomes, taxable for all. (b) is foreign income from a business controlled in India — taxable for RNOR too.

7. The new-regime standard deduction from salary for AY 2026-27 is: (a) ₹40,000 (b) ₹50,000 (c) ₹75,000 (d) ₹1,00,000

(c) ₹75,000.

(a) was the AY 2019-20 figure; (b) is the old-regime figure; (d) is not provided by law.

8. HRA exemption u/s 10(13A) under the new regime is: (a) least of three limits (b) fully exempt (c) not available (d) 50% exempt

(c) not available.

(a) is the old-regime rule. (b) and (d) do not exist.

9. Gratuity of a private employee covered by the Payment of Gratuity Act uses: (a) 15/26 × last drawn salary × completed years (b) ½ × average salary × completed years (c) 1/3 of commuted value (d) 10 months' average

(a)

(b) is for employees not covered. (c) is commuted pension. (d) is part of leave encashment and non-covered gratuity (average salary).

10. A private employee received commuted pension and also gratuity. Exempt portion of commuted pension: (a) Whole (b) 1/2 of full value (c) 1/3 of full value (d) Nil

(c)

(a) is for Government employees. (b) applies when no gratuity is received. (d) is wrong because sec 10(10A)(ii) grants a partial exemption.

11. RFA for a non-Govt employee in a city with population 30 lakh (2011), employer-owned: (a) 5% (b) 7.5% (c) 10% (d) 15% of salary

(b) 7.5%

(a) is for ≤ 15 lakh; (c) for > 40 lakh; (d) was the pre-1-9-2023 rate for cities above 25 lakh.

12. Employer's contribution to RPF is taxable: (a) fully (b) in excess of 12% of salary (c) in excess of 9.5% of salary (d) never

(b)

(a) is wrong because contributions up to 12% of salary are exempt. (c) 9.5% is the limit for interest credited, not for contributions. (d) ignores the 12% ceiling (and the ₹7.5 lakh aggregate limit u/s 17(2)(vii)).

13. For a self-occupied property, the new regime allows interest on housing loan of: (a) ₹2,00,000 (b) ₹30,000 (c) Nil (d) Actual interest

(c) Nil.

(a) and (b) are old-regime limits (acquisition/construction after 1-4-1999 and others). (d) applies to let-out property.

14. Standard deduction u/s 24(a) is: (a) 30% of GAV (b) 30% of NAV (c) ₹30,000 (d) Actual repairs

(b) 30% of NAV.

(a) uses the wrong base (municipal tax must be deducted first). (c) is the old pre-1999 SOP interest limit. (d) actual repairs are not deductible under HP.

15. Pre-construction interest is deductible: (a) in the year paid (b) in 5 equal instalments from the year of completion (c) never (d) in 3 instalments

(b)

(a) ignores sec 24(b) Explanation. (c) is wrong — it is allowed. (d) is an invented rule.

16. A cash payment of ₹15,000 to a supplier in a single day is: (a) fully allowed (b) 30% disallowed (c) 100% disallowed u/s 40A(3) (d) allowed on payment basis

(c)

(a) ignores the ₹10,000 limit. (b) 30% is for TDS default u/s 40(a)(ia). (d) payment basis is sec 43B.

17. Presumptive income u/s 44AD on digital receipts is: (a) 8% (b) 6% (c) 50% (d) 5%

(b) 6%

(a) 8% applies to other (cash) receipts. (c) 50% is 44ADA for professionals. (d) is not a presumptive rate.

18. Listed equity shares sold in November 2025 after 13 months are: (a) short-term (b) long-term (c) long-term only if held > 24 months (d) business income always

(b) long-term (holding > 12 months).

(a) wrong period; (c) 24 months applies to unlisted shares, land, building, gold etc.; (d) only if held as stock-in-trade.

19. LTCG u/s 112A for transfers on or after 23-7-2024 is taxed at: (a) 10% above ₹1 lakh (b) 12.5% above ₹1.25 lakh (c) 20% with indexation (d) 15%

(b)

(a) was the earlier rule. (c) was the old sec 112 rule for other assets. (d) was the old 111A rate.

20. Maximum investment eligible for exemption u/s 54EC: (a) ₹10 crore (b) ₹50 lakh (c) ₹1 crore (d) no limit

(b)

(a) is the cost cap for sec 54/54F. (c) and (d) are not provided.

21. Sec 54F exemption is: (a) LTCG or cost of new house, lower (b) LTCG × investment ÷ net consideration (c) whole LTCG always (d) cost of new house

(b)

(a) is sec 54. (c) only if the entire net consideration is invested. (d) ignores proportion.

22. Cash gifts of ₹30,000 and ₹25,000 from two friends during the year: taxable amount? (a) Nil (b) ₹5,000 (c) ₹55,000 (d) ₹25,000

(c) ₹55,000

Aggregate exceeds ₹50,000, so the whole aggregate is taxable. (a) ignores aggregation; (b) wrongly taxes only the excess; (d) is arbitrary.

23. Family pension ₹1,20,000: deduction u/s 57(iia) under the new regime: (a) ₹15,000 (b) ₹25,000 (c) ₹40,000 (d) ₹75,000

(b) ₹25,000 (lower of 1/3 = ₹40,000 and ₹25,000).

(a) is the old-regime cap. (c) ignores the cap. (d) is the salary standard deduction.

24. Lottery winnings are taxed at: (a) slab rates (b) 30% flat (c) 20% (d) exempt up to ₹4 lakh

(b) 30% u/s 115BB (plus cess).

(a) wrong because 115BB overrides slabs. (c) is the 111A rate. (d) basic exemption cannot be used against 115BB income.

25. Loss from house property under the new regime can be: (a) set off against salary up to ₹2 lakh (b) set off against any head (c) not set off against other heads (d) set off only against capital gains

(c)

(a) is the old-regime rule. (b) and (d) are not permitted.

26. Resident individual, TI ₹9,00,000 (all normal income), new regime. Tax payable: (a) ₹30,000 (b) ₹31,200 (c) Nil (d) ₹60,000

(c) Nil — slab tax ₹30,000 is fully rebated u/s 87A.

(a) ignores the rebate; (b) adds cess to an unrebated figure; (d) is the tax at ₹12 lakh.

27. Tax on TI of ₹15,00,000 (normal income) before cess, new regime: (a) ₹1,05,000 (b) ₹1,50,000 (c) ₹2,62,500 (d) ₹90,000

(a) ₹1,05,000 = 60,000 + 15% × 3,00,000.

(b) uses 10% on the whole income. (c) is the old-regime tax. (d) uses 10% for the 12–15L slice.

28. Which deduction is available under the new regime? (a) 80C (b) 80D (c) 80CCD(2) (d) 80TTA

(c)

(a), (b), (d) are old-regime only.

29. Health and Education Cess is levied at: (a) 2% (b) 3% (c) 4% on tax + surcharge (d) 4% on total income

(c)

(a) and (b) were earlier education cesses. (d) wrong base: cess is on tax, not income.

30. The e-filed return must be verified within: (a) 120 days (b) 30 days (c) 60 days (d) no limit

(b) 30 days (from 1-8-2022).

(a) was the earlier limit. (c) is not the rule. (d) unverified returns are treated as not filed.

Question bank B — 18 short-answer questions (2–3 marks) with answers

1. Define "assessee" u/s 2(7).

A person by whom any tax or other sum is payable under the Act, and includes every person against whom proceedings have been taken, a deemed assessee (e.g., a representative assessee), and an assessee in default (e.g., an employer who fails to deduct TDS).

2. Distinguish previous year and assessment year.

PY (sec 3) is the financial year in which income is earned (1-4-2025 to 31-3-2026); AY (sec 2(9)) is the 12 months starting 1 April immediately after the PY (2026-27), in which that income is assessed and taxed.

3. What is gross total income?

Total of incomes under the five heads computed as per the Act, after clubbing and set-off of losses, before Chapter VI-A deductions [sec 80B(5)].

4. Give any four exempt incomes of an individual.

Agricultural income (10(1)); share of profit from a firm (10(2A)); gratuity within limits (10(10)); commuted pension within limits (10(10A)); leave encashment on retirement within limits (10(10AA)); sum received from HUF as member (10(2)).

5. State the basic conditions of residence for an individual.

(a) In India for 182 days or more in the PY; or (b) 60 days or more in the PY and 365 days or more in the 4 preceding PYs. Satisfying either makes him resident.

6. Who is a "deemed resident" u/s 6(1A)?

An Indian citizen whose total income, other than foreign income, exceeds ₹15 lakh and who is not liable to tax in any other country because of domicile or residence. He is treated as RNOR.

7. What is a perquisite? Give examples.

Any benefit or amenity, in cash or kind, given by the employer to the employee in addition to salary (sec 17(2)): rent-free accommodation, motor car, free education, interest-free loans, club membership, domestic servants.

8. What is profit in lieu of salary?

Compensation on termination or modification of employment, payments from URPF (employer's share and interest), keyman insurance sums, and amounts received before joining or after leaving (sec 17(3)); taxable as salary.

9. What is "expected rent"?

The sum for which the property might reasonably be let from year to year: higher of municipal value and fair rent, but not exceeding standard rent under the Rent Control Act.

10. What is deemed let-out property?

Where an owner has more than two self-occupied houses, only two can be treated as SOP; the others are deemed to be let out and their expected rent is taxed (sec 23(4)).

11. State the rule u/s 43B.

Certain expenses (taxes and duties, employer PF/ESI contributions, bonus, leave encashment, interest to banks/NBFCs, payments to MSMEs beyond the permitted time) are allowed only in the year of actual payment, though payment up to the return due date is accepted for most items.

12. Define "capital asset" in two lines.

Property of any kind held by an assessee, whether or not connected with business, including securities held by FIIs, but excluding stock-in-trade, personal movable effects (other than jewellery, art, drawings, archaeological collections), rural agricultural land, and specified gold bonds/deposit schemes (sec 2(14)).

13. What is the cost inflation index?

A number notified by the CBDT for each financial year (base 2001-02 = 100; 2025-26 = 376) used to inflate cost for computing indexed cost of acquisition/improvement.

14. Who is a "relative" for sec 56(2)(x)?

Spouse; brother or sister; brother or sister of the spouse; brother or sister of either parent; any lineal ascendant or descendant; lineal ascendants/descendants of the spouse; and spouses of all these persons. Friends and cousins are not relatives.

15. State the rebate u/s 87A for AY 2026-27.

New regime: resident individual with TI up to ₹12 lakh gets rebate up to ₹60,000 (marginal relief above); old regime: TI up to ₹5 lakh, rebate up to ₹12,500.

16. What is a belated return?

A return filed after the due date u/s 139(1) but by 31 December of the AY (sec 139(4)); it attracts fee u/s 234F and cannot carry forward business/capital losses.

17. What is AIS?

The Annual Information Statement on the e-filing portal showing the taxpayer's financial transactions reported by third parties (salary, interest, dividends, securities, rent, TDS/TCS) for pre-filling and matching the return.

18. Distinguish tax avoidance and tax evasion.

Avoidance uses loopholes within the letter of law, against its spirit (legal but discouraged, e.g., GAAR). Evasion reduces tax by illegal means — concealment, false entries (punishable). Planning uses incentives the law intends (legal and encouraged).

Question bank C — 8 long-answer and case-study questions with full model answers

Structure for every 10-mark answer: introduction/definition → 5–8 headed points with section numbers → a short numerical illustration → conclusion. Case studies (15 marks): assumptions → working notes → computation → advice.

L1 (10 marks). Explain the rules for determining the residential status of an individual and the incidence of tax.
Model answer

Introduction. The scope of an individual's taxable income depends not on citizenship but on residential status for the relevant previous year (sec 5 read with sec 6). Status is determined afresh every year.

1. Basic conditions [sec 6(1)]. An individual is resident if he is in India for (a) 182 days or more in the PY, or (b) 60 days or more in the PY and 365 days or more in the four preceding PYs.

2. Exceptions to condition (b). For an Indian citizen leaving India for employment or as crew of an Indian ship, and for an Indian citizen or person of Indian origin visiting India, the 60 days are replaced by 182 days; for such a visitor with Indian income above ₹15 lakh, 120 days (he is then RNOR if resident only by this route).

3. Deemed resident [sec 6(1A)]. An Indian citizen with Indian income above ₹15 lakh who is not liable to tax in any country is deemed resident (RNOR).

4. Additional conditions [sec 6(6)]. A resident is ROR if he (i) was resident in at least 2 of the 10 preceding PYs and (ii) stayed in India 730 days or more in the 7 preceding PYs; otherwise RNOR.

5. Counting rules. Stay need not be continuous or at one place; both arrival and departure days are generally counted; the status is for the whole PY.

6. Incidence of tax [sec 5].

IncomeRORRNORNR
Received/deemed received in IndiaTaxableTaxableTaxable
Accrues/deemed to accrue in IndiaTaxableTaxableTaxable
Accrues outside India from business controlled / profession set up in IndiaTaxableTaxableNot taxable
Other foreign income (incl. foreign agricultural income)TaxableNot taxableNot taxable
Past foreign income brought into IndiaNot taxableNot taxableNot taxable

Illustration. Mr Rohan (your course problem) stayed 80 days in PY 2025-26 and 445 days in the four preceding years → resident; resident in only 2 of 10 preceding years but 565 days in 7 years → RNOR. His Indian salary is taxable; rent from a London flat received abroad is not.

Conclusion. Residential status decides "what" is taxable; the heads of income decide "how much". Always tabulate days year-wise before concluding.

L2 (10 marks). Discuss the tax treatment of allowances received by a salaried employee.
Model answer

Introduction. An allowance is a fixed periodic cash payment by the employer to meet specific expenses. All allowances are taxable as salary unless specifically exempted u/s 10.

1. Fully taxable: dearness allowance, city compensatory, overtime, lunch/tiffin, servant, cash, medical, officiating, non-practising, project, warden allowances.

2. Exempt to the extent spent for official purposes [sec 10(14)(i), Rule 2BB(1)]: travelling/tour/transfer, daily allowance, conveyance for official duty, helper, academic/research, uniform. Available in both regimes.

3. Exempt up to fixed limits [sec 10(14)(ii), Rule 2BB(2)]: children education ₹100 p.m. per child (max 2), hostel ₹300 p.m. per child (max 2), transport allowance for blind/deaf/orthopaedically disabled ₹3,200 p.m., underground mines ₹800 p.m., tribal area ₹200 p.m., border/remote and high-altitude allowances at notified rates. Under the new regime only the disability transport allowance survives among these.

4. HRA [sec 10(13A)]: least of HRA received, rent − 10% of salary, 50%/40% of salary — old regime only.

5. Entertainment allowance: fully included; Government employees (old regime) deduct least of ₹5,000, 20% of basic, actual u/s 16(ii).

6. Fully exempt: allowances to Government employees posted abroad (10(7)); allowances to UN employees; sumptuary allowance to High Court/Supreme Court judges.

Illustration. Mr Ramesh Swain (course problem): of fourteen allowances, only the non-exempt ones enter salary under the new regime; under the old regime education (₹100 × 2 children × 12), hostel and HRA exemptions reduce salary.

Conclusion. The new regime taxes almost every allowance; restructuring salary into exempt allowances matters only for those who choose the old regime.

L3 (10 marks). How is income from house property computed? Explain the deductions u/s 24.
Model answer

Introduction. Sec 22 charges the annual value of buildings or land appurtenant owned by the assessee, other than property used for his own business or profession.

1. Gross annual value [sec 23(1)]: expected rent (higher of municipal value and fair rent, limited to standard rent) compared with actual rent received/receivable (net of unrealised rent); the higher is GAV; if actual rent is lower because of vacancy, actual rent is GAV.

2. Municipal taxes: deducted only if borne and paid by the owner during the PY.

3. NAV = GAV − municipal taxes.

4. Sec 24(a) standard deduction: 30% of NAV, irrespective of actual repairs.

5. Sec 24(b) interest on borrowed capital: let-out — full interest (both regimes); self-occupied — up to ₹2,00,000 (old regime; ₹30,000 for repairs or pre-1999 loans), nil in new regime; pre-construction interest in 5 equal instalments from the year of completion.

6. Self-occupied property: annual value nil for up to two houses; others deemed let out.

7. Sec 25A: arrears or recovered unrealised rent taxed in the year of receipt after 30% deduction.

Illustration. Mr Arjun's flat: ER ₹4,00,000, actual rent ₹4,20,000 → GAV 4,20,000; NAV 4,02,000; 30% = 1,20,600; interest 2,10,000; income ₹71,400.

Conclusion. Only two deductions are allowed from NAV; loss under this head can be set off against other heads only up to ₹2 lakh in the old regime and not at all in the new regime.

L4 (10 marks). Explain the expenses expressly allowed and disallowed in computing business income, including expenditure on scientific research.
Model answer

Introduction. Business income is computed by allowing deductions u/s 30–37 and applying the disallowances in sec 40, 40A and 43B.

Allowed: rent, rates, taxes, repairs and insurance of buildings (30) and machinery (31); depreciation on block of assets (32); scientific research (35); insurance premium on stocks, employee bonus/commission, interest on borrowed capital, employer's contribution to recognised PF, bad debts written off, family planning expenditure by companies (36); and the general deduction u/s 37(1) for revenue expenditure laid out wholly and exclusively for business, not personal or capital.

Scientific research (sec 35): revenue expenditure on in-house research related to the business is fully deductible; capital expenditure (other than land) is fully deductible in the year incurred; pre-commencement expenditure of 3 years (salary and materials) is allowed in the year business starts; contributions to approved research associations, universities and companies are deductible at notified rates. Weighted deductions that existed earlier have been reduced to 100%, and some are not available in the new regime.

Disallowed: sec 40(a)(i)/(ia) — payments without TDS (100% for non-residents, 30% for residents); 40(a)(ii) — income tax; 40(b) — excess partner remuneration/interest; 40A(2) — excessive payments to relatives; 40A(3) — cash payments above ₹10,000 a day (₹35,000 for transporters); 43B — statutory dues, bonus, leave encashment, bank interest allowed only on payment; Explanation to 37(1) — penalties and expenses for offences.

Illustration. Arjun's tutoring business: net profit 3,20,000 + personal 15,000 + cash payment 14,000 + income tax 20,000 + depreciation difference 8,000 − dividend 12,000 = ₹3,65,000.

Conclusion. A P&L adjustment answer must list each item, the section, and whether it is added or deducted.

L5 (10 marks). Explain the chargeability and computation of capital gains after the Finance (No. 2) Act, 2024.
Model answer

Introduction. Sec 45 charges profits arising from the transfer of a capital asset in the PY in which the transfer takes place.

1. Conditions: a capital asset (sec 2(14)); a transfer (sec 2(47): sale, exchange, relinquishment, extinguishment, compulsory acquisition, conversion into stock-in-trade); profit; not exempt u/s 46/47/10.

2. Classification (transfers on or after 23-7-2024): listed securities and equity-oriented MF units are long-term if held more than 12 months; all other assets more than 24 months; specified MFs/market-linked debentures are always short-term.

3. Computation (sec 48): full value of consideration (stamp duty value for land/building if it exceeds consideration by more than 10%, sec 50C) − transfer expenses − cost of acquisition − cost of improvement. Indexation has been withdrawn, except that resident individuals/HUFs transferring land or building acquired before 23-7-2024 may pay the lower of 12.5% without indexation and 20% with indexation.

4. Rates: STCG u/s 111A 20%; LTCG u/s 112A 12.5% above ₹1.25 lakh; other LTCG u/s 112 12.5%; other STCG at slab rates.

5. Exemptions: 54, 54B, 54EC, 54F on reinvestment.

Illustration. Land bought in 2010-11 for ₹20 lakh, improved in 2015-16 for ₹5 lakh, sold in November 2025 for ₹90 lakh with 1% brokerage: option A tax ₹8,01,250; option B (indexed) tax ₹7,33,370 — option B chosen (see extra numerical N8).

Conclusion. The 2024 amendments simplified holding periods and rates; the key exam skills are classifying the gain, computing cost, and choosing the lower-tax option for pre-23-7-2024 land/buildings.

L6 (10 marks). What is agricultural income? Explain the tests and the partial integration of agricultural income.
Model answer

Definition. Sec 2(1A): (a) rent or revenue derived from land in India used for agricultural purposes; (b) income from agriculture on such land, or from processes to make the produce marketable, or from sale of such produce by the cultivator/receiver of rent in kind; (c) income from farm buildings (with conditions); and income from saplings/seedlings grown in a nursery.

Tests: land in India; used for agriculture (basic operations of tilling/sowing plus subsequent operations); income derived from such land; the assessee has an interest in the land (owner or tenant).

Not agricultural: dividend from a company engaged in agriculture, income from poultry, dairy, fisheries, sale of spontaneously grown trees, interest on arrears of rent.

Exemption and partial integration. Indian agricultural income is exempt u/s 10(1), but where it exceeds ₹5,000 and non-agricultural income exceeds the basic exemption limit, it is aggregated for rate purposes: Step 1 tax on (non-agri + agri); Step 2 tax on (agri + basic exemption limit); Step 3 tax = Step 1 − Step 2.

Illustration (new regime). Non-agricultural income ₹18,00,000, agricultural income ₹3,00,000: Step 1 tax on ₹21,00,000 = ₹2,25,000; Step 2 tax on ₹7,00,000 = ₹15,000; tax = ₹2,10,000 + cess ₹8,400 = ₹2,18,400 (without agricultural income it would be ₹1,60,000 + cess).

Conclusion. Agricultural income is not taxed as such, but it pushes other income into higher slabs.

L7 (15 marks, case study). Dr Meera Rao — compute total income and tax liability under the new regime and name the ITR form.
Model answer

Dr Meera Rao (35), resident, is employed by a Bengaluru hospital: basic salary ₹1,00,000 p.m., HRA ₹30,000 p.m. (she pays rent ₹35,000 p.m.), employer's NPS contribution 10% of basic, professional tax ₹2,400. She also has private consultation receipts of ₹18,00,000 (all through banking channels) and opts for sec 44ADA. Other data: STCG on sale of a plot held 20 months ₹3,00,000; LTCG on equity mutual fund units (STT paid) ₹1,80,000; savings interest ₹25,000; FD interest ₹60,000; gift from her cousin ₹75,000. TDS on salary ₹1,20,000; advance tax paid ₹2,00,000.

Assumptions: resident individual below 60; new regime (default); interest u/s 234B/234C ignored as dates of payment are not given.

WN 1 Salary: basic 12,00,000 + HRA 3,60,000 (taxable in full) + employer NPS 1,20,000 = 16,80,000 − 75,000 = ₹16,05,000. Professional tax not deductible in new regime.

WN 2 Profession (44ADA): receipts ₹18,00,000 ≤ ₹75 lakh (cash receipts nil) → 50% = ₹9,00,000.

WN 3 Capital gains: plot held 20 months (≤ 24 months) → STCG ₹3,00,000 at slab rates; equity MF units → LTCG u/s 112A ₹1,80,000.

WN 4 Other sources: 25,000 + 60,000 + gift 75,000 (a cousin is not a "relative"; gift exceeds ₹50,000) = ₹1,60,000.

L8 (15 marks, tax consulting case — CIA-3 style). Mr Vikram: compute tax under the new regime and suggest legal tax-planning measures.
Model answer

Mr Vikram (45), resident, a marketing manager: gross salary ₹24,00,000 (basic ₹12,00,000; the rest is special allowance); no employer NPS. He owns a flat let out at ₹3,00,000 p.a. (municipal tax paid ₹12,000; interest on housing loan ₹1,50,000). FD interest ₹80,000; LTCG on listed shares ₹1,00,000. Compute tax and suggest planning.

Present cess is ₹12,679.20; total 3,29,659.20 rounded to ₹3,29,660.

Saving from NPS restructuring: 3,29,660 − 2,83,040 = ₹46,620 a year, with the money going into his own retirement account.

Other legal planning measures:

  1. Book equity gains up to ₹1,25,000 each year (tax-free under 112A) instead of letting gains pile up.
  2. Consider moving part of FD money into instruments taxed only on redemption as capital gains, bearing in mind risk.
  3. Let-out property interest is fully deductible; prepaying the home loan saves interest but not tax on this flat.
  4. Check the old regime: at his income level, it wins only if reductions beyond ₹50,000 exceed about ₹8 lakh (see break-even table) — he does not have them, so stay in the new regime.
  5. Pay advance tax on non-salary income; file ITR-2 by 31 July 2026 (no business income).

Ethics note: these are tax-planning measures (using incentives the law intends), not avoidance or evasion.

Question bank D — 12 extra numericals with complete solutions

N1 — Residential status and scope (Unit I)

Ms Linda, a foreign national, first came to India in PY 2021-22. Days in India: 2021-22: 120; 2022-23: 90; 2023-24: 150; 2024-25: 40; 2025-26: 100. Incomes for PY 2025-26: salary for services in India ₹8,00,000; dividend from a US company received in the US ₹2,00,000; rent from a London flat received in London ₹3,00,000; profit of a German business controlled from India (received in Germany) ₹1,50,000; interest on an Indian bank deposit ₹40,000. Determine status and taxable income under each status.

Solution
  1. PY 2025-26: 100 days (≥ 60) and 4 preceding years = 120 + 90 + 150 + 40 = 400 days (≥ 365) → Resident.
  2. Earlier years: 2021-22 (120 days, 0 before) NR; 2022-23 (90; 120 before) NR; 2023-24 (150; 210 before) NR; 2024-25 (40) NR. Resident in 0 of 10 preceding years → RNOR (also 7-year stay 400 < 730).
  3. RNOR taxable: salary 8,00,000 + German business (controlled from India) 1,50,000 + Indian interest 40,000 = ₹9,90,000.
  4. For comparison: if ROR → all five = ₹14,90,000; if NR → 8,00,000 + 40,000 = ₹8,40,000.
N2 — HRA under both regimes (Unit II)

Basic ₹50,000 p.m., DA ₹10,000 p.m. (forms part of retirement benefits), HRA ₹24,000 p.m.; rent paid ₹25,000 p.m. in Delhi. Compute taxable HRA.

Solution
  1. Salary = (50,000 + 10,000) × 12 = ₹7,20,000. HRA received = ₹2,88,000. Rent = ₹3,00,000.
  2. Old regime — least of: (a) 2,88,000; (b) 3,00,000 − 72,000 = 2,28,000; (c) 50% (Delhi) × 7,20,000 = 3,60,000 → exempt ₹2,28,000; taxable ₹60,000.
  3. New regime: exemption not available → taxable ₹2,88,000.
N3 — Leave encashment on retirement (Unit II)

Mr Kiran, a private-sector employee, retired on 31-12-2025 after 20 years 4 months of service. Average basic salary of the last 10 months ₹60,000 p.m. Leave entitlement 30 days per year of service; leave availed 200 days. Leave encashment received ₹8,00,000. Compute taxable amount.

Solution
  1. Completed years = 20; leave credit = 30 × 20 = 600 days; unavailed = 600 − 200 = 400 days.
  2. Cash equivalent = 400 × 60,000 ÷ 30 = ₹8,00,000.
  3. Exempt u/s 10(10AA) = least of: ₹25,00,000; actual ₹8,00,000; 10 × 60,000 = ₹6,00,000; ₹8,00,000 → ₹6,00,000.
  4. Taxable = 8,00,000 − 6,00,000 = ₹2,00,000 (both regimes).
N4 — RFA and car, taxable salary and tax (Unit II → V)

Ms Bina, a private employee in a city with population 45 lakh (2011 census): basic ₹80,000 p.m.; DA ₹20,000 p.m. (forms part); bonus ₹60,000. Employer-owned unfurnished flat provided free. A car above 1.6 litres with chauffeur, used for office and private purposes, all expenses borne by the employer. No other income; new regime. Compute taxable salary and tax.

Solution
  1. Salary for RFA = 9,60,000 + 2,40,000 + 60,000 = ₹12,60,000; RFA = 10% = ₹1,26,000.
  2. Car = (2,400 + 900) × 12 = ₹39,600.
  3. Gross salary = 9,60,000 + 2,40,000 + 60,000 + 1,26,000 + 39,600 = ₹14,25,600; less 75,000 → taxable salary ₹13,50,600.
  4. Tax = 60,000 + 15% × 1,50,600 = ₹82,590 (TI above ₹12.71 lakh, so marginal relief gives no benefit).
  5. Cess 3,303.60 → total 85,893.60 → ₹85,890 (sec 288B).
N5 — Two houses: SOP and let-out with vacancy (Unit III)

Mr Qadir owns House 1 (self-occupied; interest on loan ₹1,80,000) and House 2 (let out at ₹25,000 p.m., vacant 1 month; MV ₹2,80,000; FRV ₹3,20,000; SR ₹3,00,000; municipal tax paid ₹24,000; interest ₹3,60,000). Compute HP income under both regimes.

Solution
  1. House 2: ER = higher of 2,80,000 and 3,20,000, capped at SR = ₹3,00,000. Actual rent for 11 months = ₹2,75,000. ER exceeds actual only because of vacancy → GAV = ₹2,75,000.
  2. NAV = 2,75,000 − 24,000 = ₹2,51,000; 30% = ₹75,300; interest ₹3,60,000 → loss −₹1,84,300.
  3. House 1 (SOP): annual value nil; interest: old regime −1,80,000 (within ₹2 lakh); new regime nil.
  4. Old regime: total loss −3,64,300; set off against other heads ₹2,00,000; carry forward ₹1,64,300.
  5. New regime: loss −1,84,300 cannot be set off against other heads; carried forward to be set off against future HP income.
N6 — Presumptive business income u/s 44AD (Unit III)

Mr Sohan, a trader, has turnover ₹2,50,00,000 in PY 2025-26, of which ₹10,00,000 was received in cash and the rest through banking channels. Compute presumptive income.

Solution
  1. Cash receipts = 10,00,000 ÷ 2,50,00,000 = 4% (≤ 5%) → the enhanced turnover limit of ₹3 crore applies, so 44AD is available.
  2. Digital receipts 2,40,00,000 × 6% = ₹14,40,000; cash 10,00,000 × 8% = ₹80,000.
  3. Presumptive income = ₹15,20,000; no further deduction for expenses.
N7 — Business income from P&L (Unit III)

Net profit per P&L ₹5,00,000 after debiting: depreciation ₹60,000 (allowable ₹45,000); donation to an approved charity ₹10,000; salary to proprietor's son ₹50,000 (reasonable ₹30,000); cash payment to a supplier on one day ₹25,000; provision for bonus not paid till the return date ₹30,000; income tax ₹40,000; GST penalty ₹5,000; advertisement ₹15,000. Credited: savings interest ₹8,000; LTCG on shares ₹50,000; bad debts recovered (earlier allowed) ₹12,000. Compute business income.

Solution

Less: LTCG (capital gains)(50,000)
Business income5,87,000

Advertisement is allowed; bad debts recovered are taxable u/s 41(4) and are already credited, so no adjustment.

N8 — Land: 12.5% vs indexed 20%, with sec 54EC (Unit IV)

Mr Yash (resident) bought land in 2010-11 for ₹20,00,000, improved it in 2015-16 for ₹5,00,000 and sold it on 15-11-2025 for ₹90,00,000 (stamp duty value ₹95,00,000); brokerage 1%. Compute the tax on LTCG, and the tax if ₹30,00,000 is invested in 54EC bonds within 6 months. CII: 2010-11 = 167; 2015-16 = 254; 2025-26 = 376.

Solution
  1. Sec 50C: SDV 95,00,000 does not exceed 110% of consideration (99,00,000) → full value = ₹90,00,000. Brokerage = ₹90,000; net consideration = ₹89,10,000.
  2. Option A (no indexation): 89,10,000 − 20,00,000 − 5,00,000 = ₹64,10,000 × 12.5% = ₹8,01,250.
  3. Option B (indexation): indexed cost = 20,00,000 × 376/167 = ₹45,02,994; indexed improvement = 5,00,000 × 376/254 = ₹7,40,157; LTCG = 89,10,000 − 45,02,994 − 7,40,157 = ₹36,66,849 × 20% = ₹7,33,370 (rounded).
  4. Lower tax: Option B, ₹7,33,370 (plus cess).
  5. With 54EC ₹30,00,000: Option A: 34,10,000 × 12.5% = ₹4,26,250; Option B: 6,66,849 × 20% = ₹1,33,370 → Option B, ₹1,33,370.
N9 — Sec 54F on jewellery (Unit IV)

Ms Zoya sold gold jewellery held for 6 years; net consideration ₹40,00,000; cost ₹15,00,000. She bought a residential house for ₹28,00,000 within a year and owns no other house. Compute taxable LTCG and tax.

Solution
  1. LTCG (transfer after 23-7-2024, no indexation for jewellery) = 40,00,000 − 15,00,000 = ₹25,00,000.
  2. Exempt u/s 54F = 25,00,000 × 28,00,000 ÷ 40,00,000 = ₹17,50,000.
  3. Taxable LTCG = ₹7,50,000; tax u/s 112 at 12.5% = ₹93,750 (plus cess).
N10 — Income from other sources (Unit IV)

Mr Uday received: ₹1,00,000 from his brother; ₹30,000 and ₹35,000 from two friends; ₹2,00,000 from a friend on his marriage; a plot from a friend without consideration (stamp duty value ₹12,00,000); lottery winnings (gross) ₹5,00,000; family pension ₹1,80,000; interest on enhanced compensation ₹2,00,000. Compute IFOS (new regime).

Solution
  1. Brother (relative): exempt. Marriage gift: exempt.
  2. Friends: 30,000 + 35,000 = ₹65,000 > ₹50,000 → whole ₹65,000 taxable.
  3. Plot without consideration: SDV ₹12,00,000 taxable.
  4. Lottery: ₹5,00,000 (taxed at 30% u/s 115BB = ₹1,50,000).
  5. Family pension: 1,80,000 − lower of (60,000, 25,000) = ₹1,55,000.
  6. Interest on enhanced compensation: 2,00,000 − 50% u/s 57(iv) = ₹1,00,000.
  7. Total IFOS = 65,000 + 12,00,000 + 5,00,000 + 1,55,000 + 1,00,000 = ₹20,20,000 (of which ₹5,00,000 at 30%).
N11 — Advance tax schedule (Unit V)

Estimated tax of Mr Vinay for PY 2025-26 after TDS: ₹2,40,000 (no presumptive income). Show the instalments.

Solution
Due dateCumulative %Cumulative ₹Instalment ₹
15 June 202515%36,00036,000
15 September 202545%1,08,00072,000
15 December 202575%1,80,00072,000
15 March 2026100%2,40,00060,000

Shortfall in any instalment attracts interest u/s 234C; if total advance tax is below 90% of assessed tax, interest u/s 234B applies.

N12 — Retirement year: gratuity, leave encashment, commuted pension and tax (Units II + V)

Mr Paul, a private employee covered by the Payment of Gratuity Act, retired on 30-9-2025 after 30 years 7 months. Basic salary ₹80,000 p.m. (no DA) until retirement. He received gratuity ₹18,00,000 and leave encashment ₹10,00,000 (leave credit 30 days per year; availed 500 days). Pension from 1-10-2025 ₹30,000 p.m.; on 1-1-2026 he commuted 50% and received ₹12,00,000. No other income; new regime. Compute tax.

Solution
  1. Salary April–September = 80,000 × 6 = ₹4,80,000.
  2. Gratuity: completed years 31 (7 months > 6); 15/26 × 80,000 × 31 = ₹14,30,769; exempt = least of 20,00,000, 18,00,000, 14,30,769 → taxable ₹3,69,231.
  3. Leave encashment: leave credit 30 × 30 = 900 days − 500 = 400 days → 400 × 80,000 ÷ 30 = ₹10,66,667; 10 × 80,000 = ₹8,00,000; exempt = least of 25,00,000, 10,00,000, 8,00,000, 10,66,667 = ₹8,00,000 → taxable ₹2,00,000.
  4. Commuted pension: full value = 12,00,000 ÷ 50% = ₹24,00,000; gratuity received → exempt 1/3 = ₹8,00,000 → taxable ₹4,00,000.
  5. Uncommuted pension: Oct–Dec 3 × 30,000 = 90,000 + Jan–Mar 3 × 15,000 = 45,000 → ₹1,35,000.
  6. Gross salary = 4,80,000 + 3,69,231 + 2,00,000 + 4,00,000 + 1,35,000 = ₹15,84,231; less 75,000 = ₹15,09,231; TI rounded = ₹15,09,230.
  7. Tax = 60,000 + 15% × 3,09,230 = ₹1,06,384.50; cess ₹4,255.38; total ₹1,10,639.88 → ₹1,10,640.

Differences-between master table

Every "distinguish between" pair the paper can set. For a 5-mark answer, pick 5–6 rows and add a one-line example.

PairFirstSecond
Direct vs indirect taxImpact and incidence on the same person; progressive; e.g., income taxBurden shifted to consumer; generally regressive; e.g., GST, customs
Previous year vs assessment yearYear of earning (PY 2025-26)Year of assessment (AY 2026-27); tax rates of the Finance Act apply
Capital vs revenue receiptFrom fixed capital/source of income (sale of machinery, compensation for loss of source); exempt unless specifically taxed (e.g., capital gains)From circulating capital/normal business (sale of stock, rent, interest); taxable unless exempt
Capital vs revenue expenditureEnduring benefit, acquires/improves a fixed asset; only depreciation allowedRecurring, for day-to-day running; deductible
Tax planning vs avoidance vs evasionPlanning: uses intended incentives (NPS, 54EC); legal and ethicalAvoidance: exploits loopholes (legal but against intent; GAAR); Evasion: concealment/false entries (illegal)
Exempt income vs deductionNever enters GTI (sec 10)Enters GTI, then subtracted (Chapter VI-A) or within a head (sec 16, 24, 57)
GTI vs total incomeSum of five heads after clubbing and set-offGTI less Chapter VI-A deductions, rounded to ₹10
ROR vs RNOR vs NRROR: taxed on world incomeRNOR: Indian income + foreign income from business controlled in India; NR: Indian income only
Allowance vs perquisiteCash payment for an expense; taxable as per sec 10(14)/(13A)Benefit in kind (or reimbursement); valued under Rule 3
RPF vs URPF vs SPF vs PPFRPF: employer share > 12% and interest > 9.5% taxable yearly; SPF: exempt (employer share and interest, subject to limits)URPF: nothing taxable yearly; at withdrawal employer share + interest taxed as salary, own-contribution interest as IFOS; PPF: own scheme, interest exempt
Commuted vs uncommuted pensionLump-sum; exempt fully (Govt) or 1/3 / 1/2 of full value (others)Periodic; fully taxable as salary
Gratuity: covered vs not covered by PG Act15/26 × last drawn salary × years (part > 6 months = 1)½ × 10-month average salary × completed years (fraction ignored)
Let-out vs self-occupied propertyGAV computed; municipal tax, 30% and full interest allowedAnnual value nil; interest up to ₹2 lakh (old regime only)
GAV vs NAVHigher of expected rent and actual rent (vacancy rule)GAV minus municipal taxes paid by owner
Business vs professionTrade, commerce, manufacture (sec 2(13))Occupation needing intellectual skill/qualification (doctor, lawyer, CA)
Sec 44AD vs 44ADAEligible business; 8% / 6% of turnover; limit ₹2 crore (₹3 crore)Specified professions; 50% of receipts; limit ₹50 lakh (₹75 lakh)
STCG vs LTCGHeld ≤ 12 months (listed) / ≤ 24 months (others); 20% (111A) or slabHeld longer; 12.5% (112/112A); exemptions 54–54F
Sec 54 vs 54FResidential house sold; exempt = lower of LTCG and investmentAny other asset sold; exempt proportionate to net consideration invested; ownership condition
Sec 54B vs 54ECUrban agricultural land to agricultural land; STCG or LTCG; 2 yearsLand/building LTCG into specified bonds within 6 months; cap ₹50 lakh; 5-year lock-in
Old vs new regimeHigher rates; exemptions and deductions; basic limit ₹2.5 lakh; rebate up to ₹5 lakh TIDefault; lower rates; SD ₹75,000, 80CCD(2); basic limit ₹4 lakh; rebate up to ₹12 lakh TI
Rebate vs deductionReduces tax (87A)Reduces income (Chapter VI-A)
Surcharge vs cessAdditional tax on high incomes (10%–37%); marginal relief4% of tax + surcharge for everyone who pays tax
Belated vs revised vs updated returnBelated: after due date, by 31 Dec, fee u/s 234F; Revised: corrects an earlier return, by 31 DecUpdated: within 48 months, only to increase tax, with additional tax 25–70%
TDS vs advance taxDeducted by the payer at sourcePaid by the taxpayer in four instalments on estimated income
Rent under HP vs under IFOSLetting of a building ownedSub-letting, letting of machinery/furniture, composite letting (if inseparable)

Common mistakes and examiner traps (18)

  1. Wrong year. Writing AY 2025-26 or using the ₹7 lakh rebate. For PY 2025-26 it is AY 2026-27 and ₹12 lakh / ₹60,000.
  2. Deducting old-regime items in a new-regime question (HRA, 80C, 80D, professional tax, SOP interest). State "not allowed u/s 115BAC".
  3. Forgetting marginal relief when TI is between ₹12 lakh and about ₹12.71 lakh.
  4. Applying 87A to special-rate income (111A/112A capital gains, lottery). The rebate covers only slab tax.
  5. Counting days wrongly in residential status: count both arrival and departure days; 2024 is a leap year (February 2024 = 29 days, in PY 2023-24).
  6. Treating a cousin or friend as a relative for gifts; and taxing only the excess over ₹50,000 (the whole aggregate is taxable).
  7. Gratuity: using 30 days instead of 26 for covered employees; rounding service up for non-covered employees (only completed years).
  8. Commuted pension: taking 1/3 of the amount received instead of 1/3 of the full commuted value.
  9. RFA salary base: forgetting bonus/commission, or including DA not forming part of retirement benefits; using the old 15% rate.
  10. HP: deducting municipal tax that is unpaid or paid by the tenant; taking 30% of GAV instead of NAV; ignoring the vacancy rule.
  11. HP loss: setting off more than ₹2 lakh against salary (old regime), or any amount (new regime).
  12. PGBP: adding back book depreciation but forgetting to deduct tax depreciation; forgetting to remove incomes taxable under other heads (dividend, interest, capital gains).
  13. 40A(3): the limit is per person per day (₹10,000), and the whole payment is disallowed, not the excess.
  14. Capital gains period: applying 36 months (old rule) or 24 months to listed shares (12 months).
  15. Indexation: indexing gold/jewellery or shares transferred after 23-7-2024; only land/building acquired before 23-7-2024 by resident individuals/HUFs gets the 20%-indexed option.
  16. Sec 50C: replacing consideration by SDV even when SDV is within 110% of consideration.
  17. Chapter VI-A against special-rate income: deductions can only reduce normal income.
  18. Not rounding: TI to nearest ₹10 (288A) and tax payable to nearest ₹10 (288B); and not writing assumptions (resident, regime, age).
Biggest single trap

Reading "compute salary income" and then computing tax. Answer exactly what is asked: taxable salary, GTI, total income, or tax liability. Extra steps earn nothing; missing steps lose marks.

One-page cheat sheet (AY 2026-27) + exam-day checklist

From last year's paper

Almost every computation was set "under the new tax regime". Watch the planted traps: 54EC bonds must be bought within 6 months and be 5-year bonds; gifts are exempt only from relatives or on the individual's own marriage; gross up net lottery (÷ 0.70) and net dividends; foreign agricultural income is taxable. GST and customs (last year's Q3) are not in your 2026 syllabus. Solved paper.

New-regime slabs (115BAC)
SlabRateTax at top
0–4LNil0
4–8L5%20,000
8–12L10%60,000
12–16L15%1,20,000
16–20L20%2,00,000
20–24L25%3,00,000
> 24L30%3L + 30% excess

87A: TI ≤ 12L → nil (max ₹60,000; residents; not on special-rate tax). Above 12L: tax ≤ TI − 12L (till ~12.71L). Salary nil-tax point: ₹12.75L.

Old regime

2.5L nil / 2.5–5L 5% / 5–10L 20% / >10L 30%. Seniors 3L, super seniors 5L. 87A: TI ≤ 5L, max ₹12,500. SD ₹50,000.

Surcharge & cess

>50L 10% · >1cr 15% · >2cr 25% · >5cr 37% (old only; new capped 25%). 15% cap on dividend/111A/112/112A. Marginal relief. Cess 4% on (tax + SC). Round TI and tax to ₹10.

Residential status

182 days, or 60 + 365 (182 for citizens leaving for employment / Indian visitors; 120 if Indian income > 15L). ROR: 2 of 10 + 730 in 7. Deemed resident: citizen, > 15L, not taxed abroad → RNOR.

Salary

SD 75,000 (new). Gratuity: 20L cap; 15/26 (covered) or ½ avg (not covered); Govt fully exempt. Commuted pension: 1/3 (with gratuity) or 1/2 of full value. Leave encashment: 25L; 10 months; 30 days/year. RFA: 10% / 7.5% / 5% (40L / 15L population). Car: 1,800 / 2,400 + 900 driver p.m. RPF: 12% / 9.5%. Aggregate PF+NPS+SAF > 7.5L taxable. HRA: old only.

House property

ER = max(MV, FR) ≤ SR. GAV = max(ER, actual) [vacancy → actual]. NAV = GAV − MT paid. Less 30% NAV, less interest (let-out full; SOP 2L old only; pre-construction 1/5 × 5). HP loss: old 2L set-off, new nil; c/f 8 years.

PGBP

Add back: personal, capital, income tax, 40A(3) cash > 10,000, 40(a)(ia) 30%, 40A(2) excess, 43B unpaid, penalties, book depreciation. Deduct: tax depreciation, incomes of other heads. 44AD 8%/6% (2cr/3cr); 44ADA 50% (50L/75L); 44AE ₹1,000/ton/month heavy, ₹7,500/month others. Sec 35: 100% scientific research (capital except land too).

Capital gains

LT: listed > 12 m; others > 24 m. 111A 20% · 112A 12.5% above 1.25L · 112 12.5% (land/building pre-23-7-2024: or 20% indexed, lower). 50C 110%. CII 2025-26 = 376. 54 (house→house, 10cr cap) · 54B (agri land, 2 yrs) · 54EC (bonds, 6 months, 50L) · 54F (LTCG × invested ÷ net consideration).

Other sources

Gifts: aggregate > 50,000 → all taxable (relatives, marriage, will exempt). Family pension: 1/3 or 25,000 (new). Lottery 30% flat, gross-up ÷ 0.70. Enhanced-compensation interest: 50% deduction. Dividends taxable (interest deduction ≤ 20%).

Unit V & filing

GTI → less 80CCD(2) (14%), 80CCH, 80JJAA → TI. ITR-1 (≤ 50L salary/1 HP/OS/112A ≤ 1.25L), ITR-2 (no business), ITR-3 (business), ITR-4 (presumptive). Due 31 July (non-audit) / 31 Oct (audit). Belated/revised: 31 Dec. Updated: 48 months. Verify: 30 days. 234F: 5,000 / 1,000. Advance tax ≥ 10,000: 15/45/75/100%.

Exam-day checklist
  1. Write "AY 2026-27" and "new regime u/s 115BAC (default)" at the top of every computation unless told otherwise.
  2. If the question paper gives rates or limits, use them and write "as per rates given".
  3. Section A (3 of 5 × 5 marks): definition + 4–5 points + example. About 10 minutes each.
  4. Section B (2 of 3 × 10 marks): headed points or a full computation with working notes. About 22 minutes each.
  5. Section C (15 marks, compulsory case): assumptions → working notes → GTI → TI → tax → advice/ITR form. Allow 35 minutes.
  6. For residential status, draw the year-wise day table first.
  7. Show section numbers next to every exemption/deduction/disallowance.
  8. Show exempt items as "Nil — exempt u/s …" so the examiner sees you considered them.
  9. Check: 87A/marginal relief, special-rate incomes separated, cess added, rounding done.
  10. Leave 5 minutes to re-add every total.
Final reminder — question-paper rates win

All figures in this guide follow the Finance Act, 2025 for AY 2026-27. If your paper states different slabs, limits, city populations or CII values, solve with the paper's figures and note that you have done so.

Taxation Laws & Practice (BBA303-5) — Complete End-Semester Study Guide. Prepared from your syllabus, course plan and course handouts; law as applicable to PY 2025-26 / AY 2026-27 (Income-tax Act, 1961 as amended by the Finance Act, 2025). This guide teaches concepts in its own words; always prefer your textbook and the question paper where they state figures.
